#### p08 — p08 — Asadi A, Ghaffari A. "SCTD-ICA: An ICA-Based Approach for Fetal ECG Extraction From Single Channel Abdominal ECG." (DOI 10.1109/JBHI.2025.3609696; PMID 40956750; Xplore doc 11164915). ACCESS:...

**Mức truy cập:** `abstract_only` · **Tải về:** không · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** low

**A. Problem.** Extracting the fetal ECG (FECG) from a SINGLE abdominal ECG (AECG) channel, and detecting fetal QRS/R-peaks from it. Stated framing (abstract, verbatim sense): the AECG is a mixture dominated by the maternal ECG (MECG) plus other sources, so separating FECG from AECG is hard. The specific technical obstacle the paper targets is that ICA — the classical workhorse for fECG separation — structurally REQUIRES multi-channel observations, and is therefore unavailable in the single-channel setting that low-cost, wearable, continuous home monitoring implies. The paper's contribution is a route to make ICA usable with one electrode pair. NOTE ON EVIDENCE LEVEL: everything below is derived from the abstract, IEEE index terms/author keywords, and bibliographic metadata only. The full text (Methods/Results) could not be obtained — the paper is closed access with no repository copy anywhere. Fields that the accessible text does not cover are marked "not reported"; I have not inferred any number.

**B. Data.** Datasets (from abstract, explicit): TWO real datasets, no simulated data mentioned. 1) ADFECGDB — Abdominal and Direct Fetal Electrocardiogram Database (PhysioNet). 2) Set-A of the 2013 PhysioNet/Computing in Cardiology Challenge Database, abbreviated "PCDB" in the paper. Number of subjects/records used: not reported (in accessible text). Sampling rate: not reported (in accessible text). Number of channels used: the method is explicitly SINGLE-channel — one abdominal lead is the input. Which of the available abdominal channels was selected per record, and whether a channel-selection rule was applied, is not reported. Record length / window length: not reported. Train/validation/test subsets: not reported — and the method appears to be unsupervised (ICA + non-negative sparse coding), so a training set may not exist at all; this is not confirmable from the abstract. How ground truth was created: not reported in the accessible text. Contextual note, clearly flagged as NOT from this paper: ADFECGDB is publicly documented as carrying reference fetal R-peak annotations obtained from a DIRECT fetal scalp electrode recorded simultaneously with the abdominal leads (i.e. a genuinely independent invasive reference), and PhysioNet/CinC 2013 Set-A ships expert/consensus fetal QRS reference annotations. The paper's abstract does not restate this, so treat the mechanism as database-provided rather than paper-reported. This dataset pair (ADFECGDB + CinC2013 Set-A) is the de-facto standard...

**C. Method.** Named method: SCTD-ICA = "Single Channel Time Delay ICA". Pipeline as stated in the abstract (three explicit stages): 1) TIME-DELAY MAPPING. The single-channel recording is mapped into multidimensional data using "the time delay method" — i.e. a delay-coordinate / Hankel-style embedding that turns one time series into a multi-row observation matrix. This is precisely the manoeuvre that restores the multi-channel condition ICA needs. (The paper's own framing: "A notable limitation of ICA is its requirement for multi-channel data. To overcome this constraint, the proposed pipeline uses the time delay method to map single-channel data into multidimensional data.") 2) ICA. The resulting multidimensional matrix is fed to an ICA algorithm to unmix components. 3) AUTOMATED FETAL-COMPONENT SELECTION VIA POWER SPECTRUM. "the analysis of the power spectrum leads to the automated identification of the fetal component" — i.e. a spectral criterion picks which independent component is fetal, with no human in the loop. This automation is a genuine contribution: many ICA-based fECG papers select the fetal component manually. Additional components evidenced by the AUTHOR KEYWORDS (authoritative, author-supplied, retrieved from the Xplore metadata record): "Fetal ECG extraction; independent component analysis; NON-NEGATIVE SPARSE CODING; R-PEAK; single-channel extraction; time delay method". So non-negative sparse coding is a first-class element of the pipeline alongside ICA, and R-peak...

**D. Model.** No neural network model. SCTD-ICA is a classical unsupervised signal-processing / blind-source-separation pipeline (delay embedding -> ICA -> non-negative sparse coding -> spectral component selection -> R-peak detection). Architecture: not applicable (no learned architecture). Input shape: a single-channel abdominal ECG time series, delay-embedded into a multidimensional matrix. The embedding dimension (number of delayed copies / rows) and the delay lag tau are NOT REPORTED in the accessible text — these are exactly the two hyperparameters we would most want, and they are unavailable. Output shape: extracted single-channel FECG waveform plus fetal R-peak locations. Training procedure: not reported; the pipeline appears unsupervised and per-record, with no gradient training implied anywhere in the abstract or keywords. Hyperparameters (ICA variant and its convergence settings, number of components retained, PCA/whitening rank, filter cutoffs and IIR orders, window length, overlap, spectral band used for fetal-component identification, R-peak threshold and refractory period): all not reported in the accessible text. Parameter count: not applicable.

**E. Evaluation.** Train/test split: not reported. Cross-validation: not reported. Subject-independent? not reported explicitly. LEAKAGE JUDGEMENT — structurally LOW risk, but formally UNCLEAR from the accessible text, for the following reasoning: (a) The method is unsupervised blind source separation applied per recording. There is no learned mapping fitted on labelled fetal R-peaks, so the classic failure mode — random segment-wise splitting inside the same subjects, which lets a network memorise a subject's fetal morphology and inflates F1 — cannot occur here by construction. Every record is processed on its own terms. (b) Evaluation spans TWO independent databases (ADFECGDB and CinC2013 Set-A) collected under different protocols, which is effectively a cross-dataset check and further lowers risk. (c) The residual risk is hyperparameter leakage, not data leakage: if the embedding dimension, delay lag, spectral-selection band, filter cutoffs, and peak-picking threshold were tuned by inspecting F1 on the SAME records that are then reported, the reported numbers are optimistically biased even without a trainable model. Whether a held-out tuning set or a fixed a-priori parameter set was used is NOT REPORTED, so I record this as UNCLEAR and would not treat 96.14% / 95.76% as a fully clean out-of-the-box figure until the full text is checked. (d) Whether all records of each database were used or a subset was excluded for quality is not reported — silent record exclusion is a common and material...

**F. Results.** Reported in the abstract, the only accessible source: - Fetal QRS detection F1 on ADFECGDB = 96.14%. - Fetal QRS detection F1 on PCDB (CinC 2013 Set-A) = 95.76%. - Qualitative claim: "In comparison to state-of-the-art methods, the proposed pipeline demonstrates comparable performance with approaches based on deep learning." - Concluding claim: "the proposed single-channel pipeline is appropriate for continuous maternal and fetal health monitoring." Sensitivity / PPV / accuracy / MAE of fetal heart rate / RR-interval error / per-record breakdowns / statistical tests / runtime / ablations: all not reported in the accessible text (they very likely exist in the full paper's tables, but I did not see them and will not invent them). Comparison baselines used and their numbers: not reported in the accessible text. Ablation of the time-delay embedding parameters: not reported in the accessible text.

**G. Limitations.** Limitations acknowledged by the paper: not reported (no limitations section is visible at abstract level). Limitations I can assert from the accessible evidence: 1) ACCESS: closed access, no preprint, no repository copy — so the method is not independently reproducible from what is publicly available, and no code repository is advertised in any metadata I could retrieve. 2) NO REPORTED HYPERPARAMETERS at abstract level: embedding dimension, delay lag, filter design, spectral band, and peak-picking rules are all invisible. For a pipeline whose entire novelty is a delay embedding, the absence of the embedding parameters in accessible text is the single biggest practical gap. 3) MODELLING ASSUMPTION: ICA on a delay-embedded single channel implicitly assumes the maternal and fetal contributions are approximately linearly mixed and statistically independent, and that the mixing is stationary over the analysis window. Real AECG violates this — maternal/fetal amplitude ratio drifts with fetal position, respiration modulates the mixing, and uterine EMG is non-stationary. The abstract gives no evidence about robustness to these. 4) DELAY EMBEDDING OF ONE CHANNEL DOES NOT CREATE INDEPENDENT SENSORS: shifted copies of one signal are a mathematically legitimate way to build a matrix, but they are not physically independent observations, so identifiability of the fetal source is weaker than in true multi-channel ICA. The paper does not (at abstract level) characterise when this breaks....

**H. Relevance.** HIGH relevance, and for one sharp reason: this paper independently validates the exact FIRST STAGE of our pipeline. 1) TAKENS/DELAY EMBEDDING IS VALIDATED ON THIS EXACT TASK. Our TDA pipeline begins with a Takens delay embedding of the single-channel AECG. SCTD-ICA begins with the same operation ("the time delay method to map single-channel data into multidimensional data"). A peer-reviewed IEEE JBHI paper reaching F1 96.14% / 95.76% on ADFECGDB and CinC2013 Set-A with a delay embedding as its cornerstone is strong external evidence that the delay-coordinate representation of a single abdominal lead genuinely carries separable fetal information. That materially de-risks our step 1 and gives us a citable justification for it in our own paper's motivation. 2) IT IS THE IDEAL CLASSICAL BASELINE — AND THE ONE WE MUST BEAT. SCTD-ICA and our method share the identical input (one abdominal channel) and the identical first transform (delay embedding), then diverge: SCTD-ICA applies LINEAR unmixing (ICA + non-negative sparse coding) to the embedded matrix; we apply NONLINEAR/topological analysis (persistent homology -> persistence images -> CNN) to the same embedded point cloud. This is an unusually clean controlled comparison — same representation, different readout — and it is the single most persuasive framing available for our contribution. Report against 96.14% / 95.76% on the same two databases. 3) IT SETS OUR EVALUATION PROTOCOL. Adopt ADFECGDB + CinC2013 Set-A and report F1...

**I. Research opportunity.** The gap this paper leaves open is precise and it is ours to fill. 1) THE DELAY EMBEDDING IS BUILT AND THEN READ OUT ONLY LINEARLY. SCTD-ICA constructs a delay-coordinate representation of the single channel — geometrically, a point cloud in reconstructed state space — and then discards all of its geometry by applying ICA/sparse coding, which are linear, second/higher-order-statistics operations on the matrix. The nonlinear structure of that trajectory (loops per cardiac cycle, nested maternal and fetal cycles) is never touched. Persistent homology on precisely this embedding is the untried readout: H1 loops correspond to the quasi-periodic cardiac orbits, and two interleaved periodicities (maternal ~1-1.7 Hz, fetal ~2-4 Hz) is the textbook case where H1 persistence separates what a linear unmixing struggles to identify. Nobody in this paper's lineage has done that. 2) SEPARATION VS DETECTION. This pipeline is an EXTRACTION method that is scored by detection F1 — the fetal waveform must be reconstructed first, and R-peaks are found afterwards. Our route can skip reconstruction entirely and detect fetal QRS directly from topological features, which sidesteps the linear-mixing assumption that limits ICA. Framing our work as "detection without separation" is a distinct contribution axis this paper cannot claim. 3) THE HARD CASES ARE UNCHARACTERISED. No reported behaviour under maternal-fetal QRS overlap, low SNR, or varying gestational age. A per-case failure analysis at...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

- Fetal QRS detection F1 on ADFECGDB = 96.14% — source: abstract (verbatim: 'F1 metrics for fetal QRS detection in ADFECGDB and PCDB are obtained at 96.14% and 95.76%, respectively'), retrieved from EuropePMC REST record for PMID 40956750 and confirmed identically in the IEEE Xplore metadata record for document 11164915
- Fetal QRS detection F1 on PCDB (PhysioNet/CinC 2013 Challenge Set-A) = 95.76% — source: abstract, same sentence, same two independent retrievals
- Number of evaluation datasets = 2 (ADFECGDB and Set-A of PCDB) — source: abstract
- Number of input channels = 1 (single-channel abdominal recording) — source: abstract and title
- Journal pages = 3568-3578, volume 30, issue 4 — source: EuropePMC journalInfo/pageInfo metadata for PMID 40956750
- Issue year = 2026 (April issue); online publication date 2025-09-16 — source: EuropePMC printPublicationDate '2026-04-01' and Unpaywall published_date '2025-09-16'
- Reference count = 49 — source: Crossref API record for DOI 10.1109/JBHI.2025.3609696 (field 'reference-count')
- Times cited = 4 — source: Crossref API 'is-referenced-by-count' as of retrieval on 2026-09-08
- PMID = 40956750; DOI = 10.1109/JBHI.2025.3609696; IEEE Xplore document number = 11164915 — source: EuropePMC, Crossref, and the doi.org 302 redirect Location header
- Open-access status = closed, 0 OA locations, has_repository_copy = false — source: Unpaywall API for the DOI; corroborated by OpenAlex (pdf_url null, is_oa false), EuropePMC (isOpenAccess N, inEPMC N), and Semantic Scholar (openAccessPdf.url empty)

#### p09 — Xu X, Zhang Y, Rao Y, Xu Y, Gao Y, Tu H. A Deep Learning-Guided Ensemble Empirical Mode Decomposition Method for Single-Channel Fetal Electrocardiogram Extraction. (CNN-2xEEMD / IMFClassifier1D)....

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** có (`p09_Xu2026-CNN2xEEMD.pdf`, 5,918,413 byte) · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** Extracting the fetal ECG from a SINGLE abdominal ECG channel, where the fetal component is only 1/5 to 1/10 the maternal amplitude (or less) and no spatial information is available for ICA/PCA-style blind source separation. EEMD is a good fit for the nonstationary AECG because it needs no predefined basis, but its practical bottleneck is choosing WHICH of the resulting intrinsic mode functions are fetal-related. Prior EEMD work (Liu and Luan 2015; Barnova et al. 2021; Dash and Nath) resolved this with fixed frequency bands, energy/correlation thresholds, or outright manual visual inspection, which either fails at low SNR or is not automatable. The paper's specific problem statement is therefore narrow and honest: automate IMF selection after EEMD, so that a semi-automatic pipeline becomes fully automatic without losing the interpretability of the classical stages.

**B. Data.** Three tiers. TRAINING/LOSO POOL, 20 subjects, 1505 IMF samples of 10,000 points each, 744 labelled fetal-related (49.44%). Tier 1: 15 synthetic single-channel AECG cases produced by an H2-3000F maternal-fetal ECG simulator (Xuzhou Mingsheng Electronic Technology, Xuzhou, China; four 18650 cells, dual outputs for maternal-only and mixed maternal-fetal), annotated on a 500 Hz grid and resampled to 1000 Hz, spanning maternal HR 60-120 bpm, fetal HR 110-160 bpm, and fetal:maternal R amplitude ratios from 1:1.17 (case 1) down to 1:16.0 (case 5); all noise-augmented to exactly 10 dB SNR. Table 1 lists every case's M_HR / M_Amp / F_HR / F_Amp / ratio. Tier 2: 5 clinical ADFECGDB recordings at 1000 Hz - r01, r04, r07, r08, r10 - with ONE abdominal channel used as the model input; the simultaneous direct fetal scalp lead was used only during training to locate fetal R-peaks and thereby label the IMFs, and was excluded entirely at inference. Tier 3, external and never trained on: DaISy (KU Leuven), 250 Hz, a single 10 s record with 5 abdominal channels, of which channels 2 and 3 were chosen after inspecting the spectra (energy concentrated in the 8-45 Hz fetal band) and time-domain sharpness; DaISy provides no fetal annotations so the authors manually verified fetal R-peaks beat by beat and excluded ambiguous beats. Also NIFEA DB (PhysioNet non-invasive fetal ECG arrhythmia database), cases NR14 and ARR01. GROUND TRUTH provenance by tier: simulator-known R-peak indices (synthetic, x2...

**C. Method.** A modular, non-end-to-end pipeline named CNN-2xEEMD, in which a learned classifier replaces exactly one heuristic step. Order of operations: (1) 50 Hz notch + 4th-order zero-phase Butterworth 0.1-100 Hz; (2) maternal R-peak detection with NeuroKit2, falling back to amplitude thresholding with a 500 ms minimum RR (implying a 120 bpm maternal ceiling) if that fails; (3) maternal template by coherent averaging over fixed-length windows centred on maternal R-peaks, T_m(t) = (1/N_m) sum_k x(t - t_m,k), re-laid at every maternal R-peak and subtracted from the AECG; (4) EEMD stage 1 on the residual, giving 8 IMFs, each scored by IMFClassifier1D, and the TOP-2 scoring IMFs summed to form the initial FECG estimate; (5) fetal R-peaks detected on that estimate with NeuroKit2 plus a 10-60 Hz bandpass, and the difference between this optimised estimate and the raw stage-1 estimate taken as a residual; (6) EEMD stage 2 on that residual, same CNN, TOP-1 scoring IMF retained; (7) final zero-phase 4th-order Butterworth 10-60 Hz. The authors give an explicit physical rationale for the asymmetric N1=2 / N2=1 choice: stage-1 input still carries maternal remnants and low-frequency noise with fetal energy spread across two adjacent IMFs, so two are kept to preserve QRS morphology; stage-2 input is already purified with cardiac energy concentrated, so one suffices and suppresses residual high-frequency noise. They note the stage-2 selected IMF's mean fetal probability is significantly higher than...

**D. Model.** IMFClassifier1D. INPUT 1 x 10,000 (one IMF, 10 s at 1000 Hz). Layer stack, transcribed from Figure 5: Conv1d(1->32, k=31, s=2) + BN + ReLU + MaxPool(4) -> 32x2500; Conv1d(32->64, k=21, s=2) + BN + ReLU + MaxPool(4) -> 64x625; Conv1d(64->128, k=15, s=1) + BN + ReLU + MaxPool(4) -> 128x156; Conv1d(128->256, k=11) + BN + ReLU + MaxPool(4) -> 256x39; Conv1d(256->512, k=7) + BN + ReLU + AdaptiveAvgPool1d(8) -> 512x8; Flatten -> 4096; Linear(4096->1024) + ReLU + Dropout(0.6); Linear(1024->256) + ReLU + Dropout(0.5); Linear(256->1) + Sigmoid. OUTPUT: one scalar fetal-relatedness probability per IMF. 5.91 M parameters (dominated by the 4096->1024 FC layer at ~4.19 M), 0.08 GFLOPs, 2.24 ms inference latency (hardware not reported). TRAINING: Adam, learning rate 1e-4, weight decay 5e-4, train batch 64, validation batch 128, 60 epochs per fold, 3 independent runs with different random seeds, loss = binary cross-entropy with logits (Eq. 4). Class imbalance: negatives in each training fold resampled to approximately 2x the number of positives, justified on the grounds that non-fetal IMFs are far more morphologically heterogeneous (maternal residuals, baseline wander, EMG) than fetal ones and the network needs broad exposure to them; note this is internally inconsistent with the reported 49.44% positive rate of the raw pool. Regularisation: BatchNorm in every conv block, ReLU throughout, dropout 0.6 then 0.5 in the FC head, described as "a relatively high dropout rate". Padding, MaxPool...

**E. Evaluation.** Two separate evaluations with very different leakage profiles. (A) IMF classifier: strict leave-one-subject-out over the 20 subjects (15 simulated + 5 ADFECGDB) - each fold holds out one entire subject and trains on the other 19, and the paper states this was chosen specifically to prevent inter-subject leakage. Three seeds, producing a 3 x 20 AUC matrix; folds whose validation set was single-class had AUC set to 0.5 to avoid bias. LEAKAGE RISK: LOW for the classifier. Segments from a subject never straddle the train/test boundary, which is exactly the discipline our project needs. (B) Extraction/detection: DaISy and NIFEA are fully independent external datasets not used in any training fold - cross-dataset, so LEAKAGE RISK: LOW. Two real caveats remain. First, the N1/N2 selection sweep (Table 3) was tuned on the 15 simulated TRAINING cases, and the tuned configuration (2,1) is the one carried into the external evaluation; that is acceptable practice but means the pipeline hyperparameters are not independent of the training data. Second, and more important than leakage, the external evaluation is statistically negligible: DaISy is a single 10 s record and, back-computing from the reported precision/recall, the four external test cases contain roughly 22 + 22 + 24 + 25 = about 93 reference fetal beats in total, so headline F1 values move by several percentage points per single beat. Third, the ablation study reports AUC 0.9445 while the headline LOSO figure is 0.9282, and...

**F. Results.** IMF classifier, LOSO, 3 seeds: mean AUC 0.9282 +/- 0.0189 across 20 subjects. Per subject (Table 2), simulated cases 0.8840 (case 9) to 0.9647 (case 12); clinical r01 0.9335, r04 0.9425 (SD 0.0024, most stable), r07 0.8550 (worst overall), r08 0.9840 (best overall), r10 0.9646. Most per-subject SDs were below 0.05, the exception being case 13 at 0.0628. IMF-selection sweep on the 15 simulated cases (Table 3): (N1=2, N2=1) gave CC 0.94 +/- 0.02, F1 0.93 +/- 0.02, SNRimp 14.8 +/- 0.9 dB and was significantly better than (1,1) CC 0.81 / F1 0.85 / 11.2 dB (p=0.001), (3,1) CC 0.85 / F1 0.88 / 12.5 dB (p=0.002) and (2,2) CC 0.91 / F1 0.87 / 13.1 dB (p=0.015). Architecture ablations (Table 4, all vs the proposed configuration by paired t-test): proposed AUC 0.9445 at 5.91 M params; kernel schedules at identical 5.91 M params - increasing [7,11,15,21,31] 0.9176 (p=0.001, a 2.69% relative drop), uniform-small [15,15,15,11,7] 0.9249 (p=0.003), uniform-large [31,31,21,15,11] 0.9273 (p=0.008), symmetric [21,15,11,15,21] 0.9276 (p=0.007), deep-large [11,15,21,31,31] 0.9193 (p=0.001); channel schedules - wide [64,128,256,512,1024] 0.9403 at 14.44 M (p=0.042), narrow [16,32,64,128,256] 0.9246 at 2.72 M (p=0.002), uniform [64x5] 0.9276 at 1.01 M (p=0.006), bottleneck [64,32,64,128,256] 0.9366 at 2.76 M (p=0.019); pooling - pure max 0.9387 at 4.85 M (p=0.089, NOT significant), pure adaptive 0.9333 (p=0.013). Backbone comparison (Table 5): IMFClassifier1D 5.91 M / 0.08 GFLOPs / 2.24 ms / AUC...

**G. Limitations.** Author-acknowledged: only 5 real clinical training subjects with no geographic or demographic diversity, so generalisation to sub-5 dB SNR, multiple pregnancies and fetal arrhythmias is unproven; the method is structurally single-channel-only and cannot be extended to multichannel without redesigning the fusion; it is fully supervised and needs a direct fetal reference lead to produce labels, which is exactly what is unavailable in the deployment setting; validation was algorithmic only, with no hardware-in-the-loop test on a wearable prototype. Additional problems I identify from reading. (1) The external test set is roughly 93 reference beats in total across four cases (derived from the reported precision/recall), and DaISy is a single 10 s recording, so every headline F1 is decided by one or two beats and no confidence interval is possible. (2) No R-peak matching tolerance window is stated, which makes the detection F1 non-reproducible and incomparable to the literature the paper benchmarks against. (3) Table 8 reports DaISy 'Accuracy' as 0.0172 and 0.0152 alongside F1 = 0.9565 and 0.9302, which is arithmetically impossible - an uncorrected error that casts doubt on the metric implementation. (4) The ablation AUC (0.9445) does not match the headline LOSO AUC (0.9282) and the ablation validation protocol is never specified. (5) The class-balancing description (resample negatives to 2x positives) contradicts the stated 49.44% positive rate of the pool. (6) Figure 5...

**H. Relevance.** Two things transfer cleanly and one does not. TRANSFERS - the backbone recipe. This is the only paper in our set that ablates 1D-CNN hyperparameters on fetal-ECG-like input at a held-constant parameter budget, and the finding is directly usable: a coarse-to-fine kernel schedule [31,21,15,11,7] beats the reverse by 2.69% AUC with 5.91 M params in both cases, a pyramid channel schedule beats wide, narrow, uniform and bottleneck alternatives, and max-pooling early with adaptive-average-pooling last beats either alone. We can adopt that backbone for the CNN that consumes our persistence images (or as the raw-signal branch of a two-stream model) and cite a controlled ablation rather than hand-waving. TRANSFERS - the protocol and preprocessing. Their LOSO discipline, their ADFECGDB labelling recipe (direct scalp lead, 8-45 Hz, amplitude threshold, 300 ms refractory), their filter chain (50 Hz notch, 0.1-100 Hz zero-phase 4th-order Butterworth for analysis, 10-60 Hz for the fetal band, 8-45 Hz as the fetal QRS energy band), their 10 s / 10,000-sample window, their internal-resample-to-fixed-length trick for mixing 250 Hz and 1000 Hz datasets in one model, and their exact noise-augmentation recipe are all things we can copy verbatim to expand our small real dataset and to make our numbers defensible. Their argument for rejecting Transformers and LSTMs is also directly quotable for us: the discriminative content of fetal QRS is local, translation-invariant and quasi-periodic, not...

**I. Research opportunity.** Four openings. (1) THE DETECTOR ITSELF IS UNLEARNED. Everything downstream of the CNN is classical - NeuroKit2 finds both maternal and fetal R-peaks on the reconstructed waveform, and every reported miss traces back to that stage (maternal-overlap morphology distortion, weak QRS plus EMG). Nobody has replaced that peak-picker with a learned per-sample detector, let alone a topology-aware one. That is exactly our slot. (2) THE FAILURE MODE IS SPECTRAL AND THEY QUANTIFIED IT. Missed beats show 15-20% lower 20-40 Hz energy and elevated 0-10 Hz energy; false positives show >40 Hz EMG energy. Any method whose evidence is band energy or amplitude will fail there by construction. Persistent homology on a Takens embedding scores the existence and persistence of a cycle rather than its band power, so it should survive amplitude depression better - this is a sharp, testable differentiation that this paper hands us on a plate. (3) NO USABLE EVALUATION STANDARD. They report no R-peak matching tolerance, report an impossible accuracy column, and evaluate on ~93 beats. A properly powered, tolerance-explicit (e.g. +/-50 ms), record-wise or LOSO evaluation on the full ADFECGDB and NIFEA sets would by itself be a contribution, and would let us report the first comparable single-channel fetal-QRS numbers against this family of methods. (4) TDA AS THE IMF SELECTOR. Their entire learned contribution is deciding which IMFs are fetal - a decision they make from a 10 s waveform with 5.91 M...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

- Mean IMF-classifier AUC 0.9282 +/- 0.0189 over 20 LOSO subjects x 3 seeds - Abstract and Section 3.1
- Per-subject AUC range 0.8550 (ADFECGDB r07) to 0.9840 (ADFECGDB r08); simulated subgroup 0.8840-0.9647; largest instability case 13 SD 0.0628, most stable r04 SD 0.0024 - Table 2
- Training pool: 1505 IMF samples of 10,000 points from 20 subjects, 744 positive = 49.44% - Section 2.5 (end)
- DaISy channel 2: precision 0.9167, recall 1.0000, F1 0.9565, CC 0.96, SNRimp 15.88 dB - Tables 7 and 8
- DaISy channel 3: precision 0.9524, recall 0.9091, F1 0.9302, CC 0.94, SNRimp 13.39 dB - Tables 7 and 8
- NIFEA NR14: P 0.9130 / R 0.8750 / F1 0.8936 / accuracy 0.7279; NIFEA ARR01: P 1.0000 / R 0.7200 / F1 0.8372 / accuracy 0.7188 - Table 8
- Derived by me from the reported precision/recall of Table 8: approx. 22 reference beats on DaISy ch2 (TP 22, FP 2, FN 0), 22 on ch3 (TP 20, FP 1, FN 2), 24 on NR14 (TP 21, FP 2, FN 3), 25 on ARR01 (TP 18, FP 0, FN 7) - total external evaluation is roughly 93 fetal beats. NOT stated in the paper.
- Baselines on DaISy: Auto-2xEEMD ch2 F1 0.8293 / CC 0.82 / recall 0.7727 (p=0.002 vs CNN) and ch3 F1 0.7000 / CC 0.61 / recall 0.6364 (p=0.003); Manual-2xEEMD ch2 F1 0.9565 / CC 0.90 / SNRimp 17.18 dB (p=0.147) and ch3 F1 0.9565 / CC 0.92 / SNRimp 15.03 dB (p=0.089) - Table 7
- Kernel-schedule ablation at a constant 5.91 M params: decreasing [31,21,15,11,7] AUC 0.9445 vs increasing [7,11,15,21,31] 0.9176 (p=0.001), uniform-small 0.9249 (p=0.003), uniform-large 0.9273 (p=0.008), symmetric 0.9276 (p=0.007), deep-large 0.9193 (p=0.001) - Table 4; the paper states this is a 2.69% improvement...
- Channel-schedule ablation: pyramid [32,64,128,256,512] 0.9445 / 5.91 M vs wide [64..1024] 0.9403 / 14.44 M (p=0.042), narrow [16..256] 0.9246 / 2.72 M (p=0.002), uniform 64x5 0.9276 / 1.01 M (p=0.006), bottleneck 0.9366 / 2.76 M (p=0.019) - Table 4
- Pooling ablation: hybrid 0.9445 vs pure max 0.9387 / 4.85 M (p=0.089, not significant) vs pure adaptive 0.9333 (p=0.013) - Table 4

#### p10 — Alidash KE, Hesar HD. Deep learning-based approach for accurate detection of fetal QRS complexes in abdominal ECG signals. Scientific Reports 15:39260 (2025). DOI 10.1038/s41598-025-22999-9....

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** có (`p10_Alidash2025-fQRSCNN.pdf`, 4,927,420 byte) · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** Fetal QRS complexes must be located in non-invasive abdominal ECG to obtain fetal heart rate and detect abnormal FHR patterns and congenital heart defects, but the AECG is dominated by the maternal ECG plus powerline interference, muscle activity, baseline wander and motion artifacts. The authors argue that the prevailing solutions - hybrid CNN+LSTM, dilated CNN-LSTM, dual-attention autoencoders, RCED-Net, W-net - achieve good accuracy only at the cost of heavy architectures, large annotated datasets and elaborate preprocessing that explicitly removes or decomposes the maternal ECG, which introduces decomposition artifacts, memory/latency cost, and impracticality for portable or low-resource prenatal monitoring. The specific problem attacked is therefore: can a deliberately simple 1D-CNN, with minimal preprocessing, no maternal-ECG removal and very few training records, detect fetal QRS accurately enough for clinical use?

**B. Data.** Dataset: PhysioNet/Computing in Cardiology Challenge 2013, Set A, also called the Non-Invasive FECG Database (NI-FECGDB), https://physionet.org/content/challenge-2013/1.0.0/. Each record is 1 minute of 4-channel abdominal ECG sampled at 1 kHz = 60,000 samples per channel. Set A contains 75 records; the authors excluded 7 for inaccurate reference annotations (listed as a54, a33, a38, a47, a52, a54, a71, a74 - a54 appears twice, so 7 unique) following Behar et al., and a further 8 for missing data at specific timestamps in one or all four channels which would break the 1 s windowing (a01, a02, a07, a09, a11, a16, a18, a43), leaving 60 usable records (75 - 7 - 8 = 60, consistent). Number of distinct pregnant subjects, gestational ages, and demographics are not reported; records are treated as the unit. Two data regimes are used: 25 records (20 train / 5 test) in approach 1, and all 60 records in approaches 2-5 (48 train / 12 test for the 80-20 split; 20 train / 40 test for the 33-67 split). Sampling rate 1 kHz, 4 channels, all four fed to the network simultaneously. Ground truth was NOT created by the authors: the challenge database supplies expert reference annotations giving the timestamps of fetal QRS occurrences. The authors' contribution is the conversion of those timestamps into network-ready labels - each record is cut into 1 s windows and each 100 ms sub-segment receives a binary label, 1 if a reference fetal QRS timestamp falls inside that 100 ms interval and 0...

**C. Method.** A single 1D-CNN performs dense, low-resolution temporal segmentation of the raw (filtered) 4-channel AECG. No maternal ECG cancellation, no blind source separation, no adaptive/Kalman filtering of MECG, no time-frequency image transform, no pretrained backbone, no recurrent or attention module - deliberately "a CNN solely". The signal is filtered (approach-dependent), min-max normalised per record, cut into 1 s / 1000-sample windows with a 100 ms hop, and mapped by the CNN to a 10-dimensional vector of sigmoid outputs, one per 100 ms bin, indicating fetal-QRS presence. The network is trained as a regression onto the binary target vector using MSE (or MAPE etc.), and the continuous sigmoid outputs are converted to binary decisions by a global fixed threshold of 0.4 or 0.5 (Fig. 13). The choice of a 100 ms output frame is explicitly justified by the classical convention that a detected fetal QRS counts as a true positive when within 50 ms of the reference annotation, so a 100 ms frame is the coarsest bin consistent with that tolerance. Five experimental "approaches" vary the preprocessing depth, the number of records, and the train-test ratio while holding the architecture, batch size, learning rate and metrics fixed, in order to isolate the effect of data quantity and preprocessing.

**D. Model.** Architecture (Figs. 10 and 11), valid padding everywhere (verified: 1000 -> floor((1000-9)/2)+1 = 496; 496 -> floor((496-7)/2)+1 = 245; 245 -> 241; 241 -> 239). Input 1000 x 4. Conv1D(16 filters, kernel 9, stride 2) + ReLU -> 496x16; BatchNorm -> 496x16; Conv1D(32, kernel 7, stride 2) + ReLU -> 245x32; BatchNorm -> 245x32; Dropout(0.2) -> 245x32; Conv1D(64, kernel 5, stride 1) + ReLU -> 241x64; BatchNorm -> 241x64; Conv1D(64, kernel 3, stride 1) + ReLU -> 239x64; Dropout(0.2) -> 239x64; BatchNorm -> 239x64; Conv1D(512, kernel 1, stride 1) + ReLU -> 239x512; BatchNorm -> 239x512; Flatten -> 122,368; Dense(256, sigmoid); BatchNorm; Dropout(0.2); Dense(64, sigmoid); BatchNorm; Dense(10, sigmoid). Totals 5 conv, 7 BN, 3 dropout, 3 dense layers; 31,407,738 parameters (Table 13). Strides of 2 are used only in the first two conv layers, explicitly to shrink the feature map. ReLU is used after every conv (motivated by cost and by citing dying-ReLU mitigation via BN); sigmoid is used in all three dense layers including the hidden ones. BN is stated to be purely a regulariser against internal covariate shift and vanishing/exploding gradients; dropout purely against overfitting. Training: optimiser Adam; learning rate 0.00003 chosen after an explicit sweep; loss MSE (MAPE for the best approach-1 result); epochs vary per approach (30, 35, 40, 50, 70, 100 were all tried; approach 1 uses 100 with MSE and 70 with MAPE, approach 2 uses 32, approaches 3 and 4 use 50, approach 5 uses 100);...

**E. Evaluation.** Splits: 80-20 train-test in approaches 1, 2, 4 and 5, and 33-67 in approach 3. Table 10 gives these in record units - approach 1: 25 signals = 20 training + 5 testing; approach 5: 60 signals = 48 training + 12 testing; approach 3: 20 training + 40 testing records. No k-fold cross-validation and no leave-one-subject-out. The authors explicitly describe the varying 80-20 vs 33-67 splits as "serving a cross-validation-like role", which it is not. Subject-independent? Unclear, and this is the central weakness. Two statements conflict. (1) Table 10 reports the split in whole-record counts, which reads as a record-wise (and hence subject-independent) split - low leakage risk. (2) The Methods state "prior to partitioning the data into test and validation sets, the data is first shuffled, and then split before being fed into the proposed network", which reads as pooling all 1 s windows across records and shuffling them before splitting - which, given the 100 ms hop and 90% overlap between adjacent windows, would place near-duplicate windows (sharing 900 of 1000 samples) on both sides of the boundary and constitute severe leakage that would largely explain the very high accuracies. The paper never resolves which was done, never names which records were held out, and never states a subject-independence claim. My judgement: leakage risk MEDIUM-TO-HIGH and formally UNCLEAR - the record counts in Table 10 favour a record-wise split, but the explicit shuffle-before-split sentence is...

**F. Results.** All figures are means +/- SD over 5 runs, at 100 ms bin level. Approach 1 (25 records, HPF only), MSE: thr 0.5, 100 epochs - accuracy 96.98+/-0.21, sensitivity 97.76+/-0.29, specificity 94.2+/-0.41, precision 98.34+/-0.11, F1 93.31+/-0.45, MSE 0.02+/-0.005; thr 0.4 - accuracy 96.70+/-0.32, sensitivity 97.23+/-0.25, specificity 94.89+/-0.63, precision 98.5+/-0.18, F1 92.8+/-0.7 (Table 3). Approach 1 with MAPE, thr 0.5, 70 epochs (best overall in the paper): accuracy 98.36+/-0.07, sensitivity 98.79+/-0.13, specificity 96.85+/-0.43, precision 99.09+/-0.12, F1 96.35+/-0.16, MSE 0.01+/-0 (Tables 4, 11). Approach 2 (60 records, HPF only) is the worst: thr 0.5, 32 epochs - accuracy 92.56+/-0.09, sensitivity 96.93+/-0.15, specificity 81.54+/-0.87, precision 94.47+/-0.22, F1 83.67+/-0.34 (Table 5). Approach 3 (60 records, 33-67 split): HPF only, thr 0.5 - accuracy 90.95+/-0.51, specificity 74.95+/-1.76, F1 79.47+/-1.24 (Table 6); after adding median filter, notch filter and [0,1] normalisation, thr 0.5 - accuracy 94.54+/-0.47, sensitivity 96.65+/-0.18, specificity 86.23+/-1.53, precision 96.25+/-0.44, F1 87.54+/-1.1 (Table 7). Approach 4 (DWT), thr 0.4, 50 epochs - accuracy 95.69+/-0.18, sensitivity 97.36+/-0.1, specificity 90.05+/-0.45, precision 97.09+/-0.13, F1 90.98+/-0.42 (Table 8). Approach 5 (60 records, full preprocessing, no DWT), thr 0.4, 100 epochs, the abstract's headline - accuracy 96.79+/-0.87, sensitivity 97.91+/-1.01, specificity 92.79+/-1.5, precision 97.88+/-0.43,...

**G. Limitations.** 1. Four-channel input is architecturally hard-wired (1000 x 4), and single-channel performance is never measured - so the method cannot be transferred to single-lead monitoring without redesign and its numbers cannot be read as a single-channel baseline. 2. Temporal resolution is capped at 100 ms by construction, so no sample-accurate R-peak, no usable RR interval, and therefore no fetal heart rate variability: at 140 bpm the RR is about 428 ms and 100 ms quantisation is a ~23% RR error, which undercuts the paper's own clinical motivation of detecting FHR variability and decelerations. 3. Metrics are per-100 ms-bin binary classification, not beat-level matched detection; the standard PhysioNet 2013 challenge scoring and any RR/FHR error are absent, yet Table 13 places these bin-level numbers side by side with prior beat-level results. 4. The metric convention appears inverted (see key_numbers): the class treated as positive for sensitivity and precision has an implied prevalence of ~0.72-0.80 and is therefore the QRS-ABSENT bin, so the headline sensitivity overstates true fetal-QRS recall by roughly 2-5 points. 5. "Lightweight" is not supported: 31.4M parameters, of which 99.74% sit in the single Flatten -> Dense(256) layer; the useful conv stack is only 60,144 parameters. This also forces a fixed 1 s input length. 6. Single dataset, single subset (Set A only), quality-filtered by removing 15 of 75 records, with no hidden Set B/C, no external database (ADFECGDB, PCDB), and...

**H. Relevance.** Same task family (fetal QRS from abdominal ECG), same dataset lineage (PhysioNet/CinC 2013), and a recent, fully reported architecture - so it is a good reference point, but it is a 4-channel method and therefore an upper-bound comparator rather than a like-for-like single-channel baseline. Four concrete things our project can take. (a) The preprocessing recipe is specified to a level we can implement directly and should adopt as our common front-end so that TDA and non-TDA arms are compared at matched preprocessing: 2nd-order zero-phase Butterworth HPF at 5 Hz; 1st-order Butterworth LPF at 3.17 Hz to estimate and subtract the baseline; 60 ms median filter with threshold-based replacement for impulsive artifacts; 0.26 s median filter for residual baseline; notch for mains; per-record min-max to [0,1] (which they found beats [-1,1]). (b) The evidence that preprocessing matters enormously at fixed architecture - F1 79.47 -> 87.54 and specificity 74.95 -> 86.23 from adding median + notch + normalisation alone (Tables 6 -> 7) - is the strongest methodological argument in the paper and tells us that any claimed TDA gain must be demonstrated against a properly preprocessed baseline or it is confounded. (c) The "no maternal ECG removal" result is directly supportive of our design premise: a learned representation can separate fetal from maternal QRS without explicit source separation, which is exactly what we would claim for a topological representation - Takens embedding plus...

**I. Research opportunity.** The paper leaves six exploitable gaps, several of which define our contribution. 1. Single-channel is untouched. The input is hard-wired to 4 channels and the authors never ablate channel count, so the field's most practically important question - how much accuracy is lost going from 4 abdominal leads to 1, and whether a richer per-channel representation recovers it - is open. This is precisely our niche: run their architecture at C=1 as our honest baseline, then test whether Takens embedding plus persistent homology plus persistence images closes the gap. 2. The 100 ms output resolution is a self-imposed ceiling nobody has lifted on this dataset with this style of model. Predicting a per-sample or per-10-ms mask, or regressing the offset within the bin, would give usable RR and hence FHR variability - the clinical endpoint the paper invokes but cannot deliver. 3. The 31.3M-parameter Flatten -> Dense(256) head is an obvious and unexamined waste: replacing it with a fully convolutional 1x1 head or global pooling would cut parameters by roughly three orders of magnitude (the conv stack is only 60k), allow variable-length input, and permit a fair "does TDA help" comparison at matched capacity - as it stands, any comparison against this model is confounded by 31M parameters of memorisation capacity, which is also the most plausible benign explanation for the very high scores. 4. No beat-level or challenge-standard metrics are reported by anyone in their comparison table on a...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

- Best result overall (Table 4 MAPE row, repeated in Table 11 'First approach' row; approach 1, 25 records, HPF only, threshold 0.5, 70 epochs): accuracy 98.36+/-0.07%, sensitivity 98.79+/-0.13%, specificity 96.85+/-0.43%, precision 99.09+/-0.12%, F1 96.35+/-0.16%, MSE 0.01+/-0
- Headline/abstract result (Table 9 first row, repeated in Table 11 'Fifth approach' row; approach 5, 60 records, full preprocessing, MSE, threshold 0.4, 100 epochs): accuracy 96.79+/-0.87%, sensitivity 97.91+/-1.01%, specificity 92.79+/-1.5%, precision 97.88+/-0.43%, F1 93.02+/-1.36%, MSE 0.03+/-0.005
- Approach 1 with MSE (Table 3): threshold 0.5 / 100 epochs - accuracy 96.98+/-0.21%, sensitivity 97.76+/-0.29%, specificity 94.2+/-0.41%, precision 98.34+/-0.11%, F1 93.31+/-0.45%; threshold 0.4 - accuracy 96.70+/-0.32%, sensitivity 97.23+/-0.25%, specificity 94.89+/-0.63%, precision 98.5+/-0.18%, F1 92.8+/-0.7%
- Worst approach (Table 5; approach 2, 60 records, HPF only, threshold 0.5, 32 epochs): accuracy 92.56+/-0.09%, sensitivity 96.93+/-0.15%, specificity 81.54+/-0.87%, precision 94.47+/-0.22%, F1 83.67+/-0.34%
- Preprocessing ablation at fixed architecture and fixed 33-67 split (Table 6 -> Table 7, approach 3, threshold 0.5): adding median filter + notch filter + [0,1] normalisation raised accuracy 90.95+/-0.51 -> 94.54+/-0.47%, specificity 74.95+/-1.76 -> 86.23+/-1.53%, F1 79.47+/-1.24 -> 87.54+/-1.1%. This is a +8.07 F1...
- DWT hurt (Table 8 vs Table 9, both 60 records, threshold 0.4): with bior4.4 DWT, accuracy 95.69+/-0.18%, sensitivity 97.36+/-0.1%, specificity 90.05+/-0.45%, precision 97.09+/-0.13%, F1 90.98+/-0.42%; without DWT, accuracy 96.79+/-0.87%, F1 93.02+/-1.36%
- Approach 3 at 33-67 split, HPF only (Table 6): threshold 0.5 - accuracy 90.95+/-0.51%, sensitivity 95.83+/-0.4%, specificity 74.95+/-1.76%, precision 92.61+/-0.49%, F1 79.47+/-1.24%; threshold 0.4 - accuracy 90.29+/-0.77%, sensitivity 93.29+/-0.62%, specificity 79.87+/-2.85%, precision 93.84+/-0.8%, F1 79.43+/-1.27%

The effective receptive field of each of the 10 outputs is nevertheless the full 1000 ms window because of the...
- Learning-rate ablation (Table 14): 0.00003 -> 96.79% final validation accuracy, high training stability, low overfitting risk (selected); 0.0001 -> 95.12%, moderate, moderate; 0.0003 -> 93.45%, unstable, high; 0.001 -> N/A, divergent, failed to converge, severe

#### p11 — Orvas I, Radu A, Galli A, Neacsu A, Peri E. A Complex UNet Approach for Non-Invasive Fetal ECG Extraction Using Single-Channel Dry Textile Electrodes. arXiv:2506.22457v1 [eess.SP], 16 Jun 2025. DOI...

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** có (`p11_Orvas2025-ComplexUNet.pdf`, 1,854,470 byte) · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** Continuous, home-based, non-invasive fetal monitoring requires comfortable, durable electrodes. Dry textile electrodes enable this but introduce far more noise than wet Ag/AgCl electrodes (notably triboelectric noise from electrode-skin friction, plus motion artefacts). Simultaneously, home monitoring favours a MINIMAL number of electrodes, so blind-source-separation methods that exploit multichannel redundancy (ICA, PCA, piCA) are unusable. The paper therefore targets the hardest combination in the field: extract the fetal ECG (and detect fetal R-peaks / estimate fetal heart rate) from a SINGLE abdominal channel acquired with DRY textile electrodes, where the fECG is buried under a much larger maternal ECG whose spectrum overlaps it, plus EMG/EHG and acquisition noise. The authors state no prior method exists for this exact combination, and that no public dataset of dry-electrode fECG suitable for deep-network training exists either.

**B. Data.** THREE datasets. (1) IN-SILICO (training + quantitative testing). Built in three steps. (a) Noise extraction: 9 recordings from NON-pregnant women, 4 dry textile electrodes around the umbilicus, 180 s each; bipolar derivations to cancel common mode; 50 Hz powerline removed by a 10th-order IIR notch (1 Hz bandwidth) and a 10th-order IIR bandpass 1-100 Hz applied; the adult ECG was then removed channel-by-channel with the BSS-based QRS-identification algorithm of Galli et al. (ref [12]), leaving pure noise/artefact. (b) Noise characterisation: pink noise in 0-10 Hz, white noise in 10-100 Hz, plus a Gaussian-mixture impulse component. Randomised for variability: pink band 0 Hz to a random cutoff in 9-12 Hz; white band randomised between 60 and 90 Hz; Gaussian mixture x_i = (sigma0*(1-u_i) + sigma1*u_i)*z_i with z_i ~ N(0,1), sigma0 = 1 (background), sigma1 = 10 (impulse), impulse-selection probability p = 0.1. The three components were normalised, weighted (pink 2, white 0.2, Gaussian-mixture 0.15), summed in the Fourier domain, then inverse-FFT'd to the time domain. (c) Generation: 10,100 clean one-minute synthetic mECG+fECG pairs from the fecgsyn generator (ref [22]), with per-recording random parameter draws to mimic multiple gestational periods, maximum fECG amplitude 20 uV, additive noise at SNR 5-20 dB. Split: 100 simulated files reserved as the test/benchmark set, remaining ~10,000 used for training. GROUND TRUTH: exact, by construction - the clean synthetic fECG...

**C. Method.** The core idea is to move the problem into the complex short-time-Fourier domain and keep it complex end to end. A single-channel abdominal signal s_ECG in R^L is converted by STFT into a complex spectrogram S_ECG in C^(F x T). A complex-valued U-Net H maps it to a fetal spectrogram S_fECG = H(S_ECG), and an inverse STFT returns the time-domain fECG. Every layer of the network operates in the complex domain, so both magnitude and phase are processed jointly; the authors argue explicitly that magnitude-only approaches (which is what a real-valued network fed a magnitude spectrogram does) discard the phase information that carries timing/morphology, and produce "incongruous predictions". Training is fully supervised on the in-silico dataset, where the clean fECG target is known. Neither fine-tuning nor domain adaptation is performed for the in-vivo datasets - the model trained on synthetic dry-electrode-like data is applied zero-shot to PhysioNet 2013 wet-electrode data and to real dry-electrode recordings. R-peak detection is a downstream step: peaks are located on the reconstructed fECG waveform, and detection quality is scored against the reference annotations. The peak-picking rule itself is not described. Baselines re-implemented/run for comparison: Extended Kalman Filter (EKF), Extended Kalman Smoother (EKS), SVD-based template subtraction (applied twice: first to remove mECG, then to denoise the residual fECG), and two real-valued deep baselines, UNet and Attention UNet...

**D. Model.** ARCHITECTURE - CUNet (Complex UNet). Topology: standard U-Net with Encoder, Bottleneck, Decoder, and skip connections concatenating encoder features into the corresponding decoder stage; every operator is complex-valued. Layers are grouped into CDown blocks (encoder) and CUp blocks (decoder); each block = a sequence of complex convolutional layers + complex normalisation layers, terminated by a downsampling (CDown) or upsampling (CUp) operator with SAMPLING FACTOR 2 per block. From the Fig. 3 diagram the network has approximately 3 downsampling stages, a bottleneck, and 3 upsampling stages, with 3 skip connections, bracketed by a Diagonal layer at input and output; the text never states depth numerically. Complex convolution (Eq. 1): for layer i with input/output channel counts N_{i-1}, N_i, complex weight tensor W_i in C^(N_{i-1} x N_i x k x k) with kernel size k != 0, complex bias b_i in C^{N_i}, and complex nonlinearity R_i : C^{N_i} -> C^{N_i}, the mapping is Y = R_i( W_i^Re * Re(x) + i * ( W_i^Im * Im(x) ) ) i.e. real and imaginary parts are stored/convolved as separate real tensors so that ordinary autodifferentiation and backpropagation apply (the authors note autodiff on genuinely complex data is problematic and this separation restores compatibility with standard training practice). Diagonal layer (Eq. 2), the paper's own architectural novelty, inspired by ref [35]: Lambda = Diag( e^{i*beta_{i,1}}, ..., e^{i*beta_{i, F x T}} ) with beta_{i,k} in [0, 2*pi) for 1 <=...

**E. Evaluation.** SPLITS. In-silico: a fixed hold-out of 100 simulated "subjects" from the 10,100 generated recordings; the remaining ~10,000 were used for training. The paper states explicitly that the comparison in Table II was computed "on the same simulated dataset of 100 subjects ... not used for the training of the networks". The split is therefore RECORDING/SUBJECT-WISE, not a random segment split within shared recordings. No cross-validation is performed (single fixed split); no validation set is described. In-vivo PhysioNet 2013 (75 recordings) and in-vivo dry (4 subjects): pure cross-dataset, ZERO-SHOT evaluation - the paper states the model "had no exposure to this dataset" and was applied "without additional fine-tuning". No subject from either in-vivo set appears in training. SUBJECT-INDEPENDENT? Yes for the in-vivo evaluations (trained on synthetic only, so trivially subject-independent). For the in-silico evaluation, "subject-independent" holds in the sense that the 100 test recordings are separate fecgsyn draws not used in training; leave-one-subject-out is not performed. LEAKAGE RISK: LOW overall, for concrete reasons. (i) The main headline generalisation claims are synthetic-to-real and cross-dataset (train synthetic dry-noise -> test PhysioNet wet-electrode; -> test real dry recordings), which is the lowest-leakage regime available and is exactly the setting where the real-valued UNet/AttUNet baselines collapse (F 54.3 / 51.6 %) while CUNet holds (77.8 %) - a pattern that...

**F. Results.** IN-SILICO, 100 held-out simulated subjects (Table II; PRD lower-better, others as marked): CUNet: PRD 26.2 +/- 11.6 / PCC 95.9 +/- 5.7 / F-score 99.8 +/- 0.2 % / SE 99.6 +/- 0.4 % / HR_err 0.40 +/- 0.81 bpm (best on all five) AttUNet: PRD 63.1 +/- 19.8 / PCC 77.4 +/- 15.0 / F 98.4 +/- 10.1 / SE 98.2 +/- 9.0 / HR_err 1.19 +/- 1.67 UNet: PRD 64.6 +/- 18.9 / PCC 77.4 +/- 13.4 / F 99.3 +/- 1.6 / SE 98.8 +/- 2.6 / HR_err 1.24 +/- 1.47 SVD: PRD 72.1 +/- 110.4 / PCC 75.9 +/- 31.6 / F 92.0 +/- 32.4 / SE 90.2 +/- 28.3 / HR_err 17.7 +/- 23.7 EKS: PRD 79.0 +/- 45.1 / PCC 66.2 +/- 28.6 / F 88.7 +/- 28.0 / SE 88.1 +/- 28.2 / HR_err 8.1 +/- 17.7 EKF: PRD 79.6 +/- 45.2 / PCC 65.0 +/- 28.6 / F 88.7 +/- 27.0 / SE 88.2 +/- 28.2 / HR_err 8.0 +/- 17.5 CUNet's PRD is less than half AttUNet's; the authors call this "a substantial reduction in distortion". SNR STRATIFICATION, CUNet only (Table I; SNR here treats mECG + acquisition noise + artefacts all as noise, hence negative dB, and higher SNR corresponds to later gestational age / larger fetal heart): SNR [-25,-20): PRD 80.39 +/- 24.63 / PCC 64.65 +/- 22.93 / F 99.84 +/- 0.36 / SE 99.69 +/- 0.71 SNR [-20,-15): PRD 43.70 +/- 15.45 / PCC 89.23 +/- 9.83 / F 99.81 +/- 0.21 / SE 99.62 +/- 0.42 SNR [-15,-10): PRD 30.75 +/- 9.50 / PCC 94.97 +/- 3.80 / F 99.79 +/- 0.24 / SE 99.59 +/- 0.48 SNR [-10,-5): PRD 25.04 +/- 5.87 / PCC 96.88 +/- 1.51 / F 99.79 +/- 0.24 / SE 99.58 +/- 0.48 SNR [-5,0): PRD 21.89 +/- 5.06 / PCC 97.80 +/- 1.13 / F 99.83 +/- 0.21 /...

**G. Limitations.** AUTHOR-ACKNOWLEDGED (Section IV-C): trained primarily in-silico, so fine-tuning on a diverse real-world dataset could improve it; extremely low SNR (early pregnancy, tiny fECG amplitude) can still defeat it; a large-scale clinical validation study is required before clinical use; training a complex-valued U-Net costs more computation than UNet/AttUNet or classical methods, and while GPU-suitable, embedding it in a wearable would need further optimisation and additional work for real-time medical interpretation. ADDITIONAL, FROM MY READING. 1. REPRODUCIBILITY IS THE DOMINANT WEAKNESS. The paper does not report: loss function, optimiser, learning rate, batch size, number of epochs, kernel size k, channel widths N_i, number of complex conv layers m, network depth, parameter count, STFT window function/length/hop/FFT size, sampling rate, input segment length, or input normalisation. Code is promised only "upon acceptance". A reader cannot reimplement CUNet from this text. 2. NO ABLATIONS. The two claimed novelties - the learnable Diagonal (phase-shift) layer and the learnable convex mixture of CReLU/GK/GroupSort - are never ablated, and the learned mu_j values are never shown. The only implicit ablation is CUNet vs real-valued UNet/AttUNet, which confounds complex arithmetic, the diagonal layers, the activation mixture, and possibly capacity. 3. THE HEADLINE APPLICATION IS UNQUANTIFIED. Dry textile electrodes motivate the entire paper, but the 4-subject dry dataset has no...

**H. Relevance.** DIRECTLY APPLICABLE TO OUR SINGLE-CHANNEL fECG + TDA PROJECT. 1. THE CENTRAL TRANSFERABLE ARGUMENT. This paper's whole result is that the representation matters more than the backbone: same U-Net topology, real-valued magnitude-domain -> PRD 64.6 / PCC 77.4; complex-valued phase-preserving -> PRD 26.2 / PCC 95.9. That is precisely the structural claim our TDA pipeline makes - that a geometric/topological representation (Takens delay embedding -> persistent homology -> persistence images) carries information a raw-amplitude CNN throws away. We can cite this as prior evidence that changing the input representation, not the layer stack, is where the gains live in single-channel fECG, and frame persistence images as an alternative to the complex spectrogram. 2. BASELINE NUMBERS TO BEAT AND TO REPORT. Table III gives us a directly comparable single-channel PhysioNet/CinC 2013 leaderboard: EKF F 80.0 %, CUNet F 77.8 %, EKS F 77.8 %, SVD F 75.6 %, UNet F 54.3 %, AttUNet F 51.6 %, with SE and HR_err alongside. This is exactly the table our results section should extend with a TDA row. 3. EVALUATION PROTOCOL WE SHOULD COPY VERBATIM. +/-50 ms R-peak matching tolerance; SE = TP/(TP+FN); F-score = 2TP/(2TP+FN+FP); HR_err = sqrt(mean over beats of (fHR_hat - fHR)^2) in bpm; PRD and PCC for waveform quality when a reference exists. Also copy the SNR-stratified reporting of Table I - it is a far more informative robustness statement than a single aggregate number. 4. A FREE SYNTHETIC...

**I. Research opportunity.** GAPS THIS PAPER LEAVES OPEN, ranked by how directly our project can occupy them. 1. IT NEVER OPTIMISES FOR DETECTION. CUNet is a reconstruction model whose R-peak scores are a by-product of an undocumented peak-picking step. Their own Table I proves detection quality and waveform quality decouple (F-score flat at 99.8 % while PRD goes 21.9 -> 80.4). Nobody in this paper builds a DIRECT single-channel fetal-QRS detector optimised end-to-end for detection with a documented decision rule. That is exactly the slot a TDA -> CNN detector fills, and it is a fair, well-benchmarked comparison because their Table III numbers exist. 2. NO TOPOLOGICAL OR DELAY-EMBEDDING REPRESENTATION IS CONSIDERED AT ALL. The paper explores exactly two representations, real-magnitude spectrogram and complex spectrogram, and shows the choice of representation is decisive. Persistence images from a Takens embedding are an untried third option with a natural theoretical story (quasi-periodic cardiac cycles produce persistent 1-cycles whose birth/death structure is amplitude-invariant), and the paper's own framing - "complex data captures both amplitude AND phase" - is one step short of "delay-coordinate geometry captures the full attractor". 3. THE COMPLEX-VS-REAL COMPARISON IS CONFOUNDED AND UNABLATED. Because they never ablate the Diagonal layer, the learnable activation mixture, or matched parameter counts, the specific source of the 2.5x PRD gain is unknown. A clean ablation (real-magnitude vs...

#### p12 — Wahbah M, Zitouni MS, Al Sakaji R, Funamoto K, Widatalla N, Krishnan A, Kimura Y, Khandoker AH. "A deep learning framework for noninvasive fetal ECG signal extraction." Front Physiol 15:1329313. DOI...

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** có (`p12_Wahbah2024-BiLSTM.pdf`, 10,612,269 byte) · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** Fetal wellbeing monitoring needs a low-cost, non-invasive, technician-free alternative to Doppler CTG and invasive fetal scalp ECG, especially in low-resource settings and during disruptions (pandemics, earthquakes). Abdominal ECG is that alternative, but the fetal ECG is buried in a composite of maternal ECG plus noise. The authors' framing is that the state of the art lacks work that goes all the way from raw abdominal signal to automatically detected fetal R-peaks to a clinically usable fetal heart rate. The concrete problem solved here: given a 12-channel abdominal composite signal, detect where the fetal R-peaks are (without first reconstructing a clean fetal ECG waveform) and turn them into a fetal HR time series.

**B. Data.** Private, not publicly available. 70 pregnant women (healthy and with health conditions), no records of fetal abnormalities, from three centres: Children's National Hospital, USA (12 subjects, 17%); Kanagawa Children's Medical Center, Japan (13, 19%); Tohoku University Hospital, Japan (45, 64%). Recording: 12 bipolar electrodes on the maternal abdomen, subject supine, sampling frequency 1 kHz, resolution 16 bits; hardware/setup described in the authors' earlier Doshi et al. 2019 paper. Only a 1-minute excerpt of each recording was used in this work. Filtering: bandpass 0.05-100 Hz plus a 50 Hz or 60 Hz notch for mains interference. GROUND TRUTH IS ALGORITHMIC, NOT HUMAN: the fetal ECG was first separated from the composite abdominal signal by the BSSR method (maternal ECG cancellation combined with blind source separation with a reference; Sato et al. 2007), and the fetal R-peak locations were then found on that separated signal by a custom MATLAB R2022b routine following the authors' earlier Wahbah et al. 2021 work. There is no expert/cardiologist annotation and no inter-annotator check. IRB: Tohoku University (2020-1-951, 2015-2-80-1) and Children's National Hospital; written informed consent obtained. Only 26 of the 70 subjects could be used in the subject-independent test because the remaining 44 subjects' information could not be disclosed under the IRB, so those 44 were training-only. Demographics for those 26 (Table 3): gestational age 30.3 +/- 5.6 weeks (range...

**C. Method.** Three-stage pipeline plus a labelling convention. (1) FRPL (fetal R-peak labelling): the 1-min record is cut into non-overlapping 65 ms frames; a frame gets Class 1 if a ground-truth fetal R-peak annotation falls inside it, otherwise Class 0. The 65 ms figure is a compromise between the authors' own measurement of the fetal QRS true-positive duration at 1 kHz (~70 ms) and the 50 ms used by Vo et al. 2020. (2) A stacked bidirectional-LSTM classifier consumes each 65-sample x 12-channel frame and outputs {R-Peak, Other}. Note the design intent stated in the paper: the network is expected to pick out the fetal component "among the remaining inputs from 11 channels", i.e. it uses cross-channel structure to discriminate fetal from maternal QRS. (3) FECGPP (fetal ECG post-processing) is applied to the predicted peak train to reduce false detections arising from class imbalance — the paper states only its purpose, never its algorithm. (4) FHR is then computed as 60 / RR over 10 s windows (Li et al. 2007 convention), and FHRE (fetal heart rate enhancement) removes artefacts using the signal-dependent rank-order mean SD-ROM algorithm (Chandra et al. 1998) plus adaptive filtering (Wessel et al. 2000); the authors explicitly say the filter type "is not of great significance" here.

**D. Model.** Architecture (MATLAB R2022b Deep Learning Toolbox), Table 1 and Figure 3: sequenceInputLayer(12 features) -> bilstmLayer -> dropoutLayer(0.5) -> bilstmLayer -> dropoutLayer(0.5) -> bilstmLayer (last-timestep output) -> fullyConnectedLayer(2) -> softmaxLayer -> classificationLayer(weighted). HIDDEN SIZES ARE INCONSISTENT IN THE PAPER: the body text and the Figure 3 block diagram both say 100, 50 and 20 hidden units, but Table 1 lists activations of 400, 200 and 100 and weight shapes of (input 1600x12, recurrent 1600x200), (input 800x400, recurrent 800x100), (input 400x200, recurrent 400x50). Under MATLAB's convention (InputWeights = 8*H x InputSize, RecurrentWeights = 8*H x H, output width = 2*H for a BiLSTM), those shapes correspond unambiguously to H = 200, 100, 50. So Table 1 and the text disagree by a factor of two on layers 1-2 and by 20 vs 50 on layer 3. Input shape: 65 timesteps x 12 features per sample (65 ms at 1 kHz). Output shape: one 2-class label per 65 ms window (sequence-to-label, not sequence-to-sequence). Training: 80-100 epochs; scheduled learning rate starting at 0.0005 and dropped by a factor of 0.1 halfway through the epochs; gradient threshold (clipping) 1; minimum sequence length set to 65 to match the input sequence length; dropout 0.5 at two points; weighted classification with w_c = ns/(nc*ns_c), giving w1 = 3.17 for R-peak and w0 = 0.59 for Other. Optimiser name, mini-batch size, and weight initialisation are not reported. Parameter count is not...

**E. Evaluation.** Two schemes, explicitly separated. (a) Subject-DEPENDENT: 5-fold cross-validation, with a stated 75% train / 25% test split and standardisation using training-set statistics. Fold construction is described only as "conducted on the used dataset" with no statement that folds are subject-disjoint; the authors themselves label it "subject-dependent", which means segments from the same subject appear in both training and test folds. LEAKAGE RISK: HIGH for the 93.0%/94.2% numbers — the classifier can memorise a subject's fetal QRS morphology, electrode placement and noise signature from adjacent 65 ms windows of the same 1-minute recording. Because the whole record is only 60 s long and is cut into 923 contiguous frames, neighbouring train and test frames are separated by as little as 65 ms, so this is close to a worst case for temporal leakage. (b) Subject-INDEPENDENT: leave-one-subject-out over 26 subjects, training on all others (including the 44 IRB-restricted subjects) and testing on the held-out one; "no prior knowledge of the testing subject data exists in the network". LEAKAGE RISK: LOW for the 88.8% +/- 6.4% number — this is the figure to compare against. There is NO cross-dataset or cross-centre test even though three centres and two acquisition sites are pooled, so centre-specific effects are not isolated. There is no held-out final test set separate from the CV folds, no reported hyperparameter-selection protocol (hyperparameters were chosen by "preliminary...

**F. Results.** Subject-dependent 5-fold CV (Table 2), network alone: per-fold accuracy 92.5 / 93.4 / 93.5 / 93.2 / 92.6%, average 93.0%; per-fold F1 0.96 / 0.97 / 0.97 / 0.96 / 0.96, average 0.96. After FECGPP: average accuracy 94.2%, average F1 0.97. Confusion matrices (Figure 6, 24,050 segments each). Panel A (network alone): TN 19344, FP 961, FN 712, TP 3033; specificity 95.3%, sensitivity 81.0%, NPV 96.4%, PPV 75.9%. Panel B (with FECGPP): TN 19445, FP 860, FN 533, TP 3212; specificity 95.8%, sensitivity 85.8%, NPV 97.3%, PPV 78.9%. ROC AUC = 0.80 (Figure 7). Subject-independent LOSO over 26 subjects: average accuracy 88.8% +/- 6.4%, per-subject range 68.5% (subject 5, BMI 33.0, GA 33.4 wk, maternal complications) to 97.1% (subject 16). Subgroup breakdown from the Discussion: 16 subjects with healthy maternal condition and normal BMI -> 90%; 5 subjects with BMI > 26 -> 85%; all subjects with BMI outside normal range -> 84%; subjects with maternal complications -> 87% vs without -> 91%; subjects without obstetric complications -> 87.5%; GA < 30 weeks -> 88% vs GA > 34 weeks -> 93%; the 28-34 week vernix caseosa window -> 86.7%. FHR validation against BSSR (26 fetuses, n = 156 R-R intervals): Pearson r = 0.56, p = 1.58e-14, regression y = 0.74x + 33.68; Bland-Altman bias -5.49 with limits of agreement +18.05 and -29.03 (unit misprinted as "weeks" in the paper; from the plot axes it is clearly bpm). Figure 4 shows FECGPP output still contains large HR dropouts (to ~50 bpm) that FHRE...

**G. Limitations.** Acknowledged by the authors: performance drops for high maternal BMI, for maternal complications, for gestational age below 30 weeks and during the vernix caseosa period; equipment and human error in electrode placement; the Bland-Altman plot shows worsening agreement at longer R-R intervals. Not acknowledged but material: (1) The headline F1 of 0.96/0.97 is the majority-class figure — I recomputed the negative-class F1 from Panel B as 2*0.973*0.958/(0.973+0.958) = 0.9654, an exact match to the reported 0.97, whereas the R-peak-class F1 is only 0.822 (Panel B) and 0.784 (Panel A). A reader taking "F1 = 0.97" as a fetal-QRS detection score is off by 0.15. (2) PPV of 78.9% means over 21% of reported fetal beats are false, which for HRV or FHR variability analysis is disqualifying. (3) FECGPP is one of four headline contributions and buys +4.8 pts of sensitivity, yet its algorithm is never given — the result cannot be reproduced. (4) Ground truth is BSSR output, so the network is trained and scored against another algorithm's opinion; BSSR failures are systematically invisible and the ceiling is BSSR's own accuracy. (5) Only 60 s per subject (~140 beats) and only 26 subjects in the honest LOSO test. (6) No public benchmark, no cross-dataset test, and no comparison whatsoever against any baseline — not against classical fetal-QRS detectors, not against Vo et al. 2020 or Zhou et al. 2021 which they cite, not even against a plain LSTM or a CNN. (7) The 65 ms output granularity...

**H. Relevance.** Directly relevant as a problem-formulation and evaluation template, and as a soft performance target, but NOT as a single-channel baseline. Transferable to our single-channel fECG + TDA project: (1) The FRPL formulation — slide a fixed-duration window, label it positive iff a reference R-peak falls inside, classify each window — is precisely the shape our pipeline wants, because a Takens embedding + persistent homology + persistence image naturally produces one fixed-size image per window, which then feeds one CNN logit. This paper is the cleanest published precedent for treating fetal R-peak detection as per-window binary classification rather than as waveform reconstruction or dense segmentation. (2) The class-imbalance handling is concrete and directly reusable: prevalence is ~15.6% positive at 65 ms windows, and w_c = ns/(nc*ns_c) gives w1 = 3.17, w0 = 0.59. (3) The standardisation discipline — z-score with TRAINING-set mean and SD, applied to both train and test — is the right pattern and worth copying verbatim, since per-window normalisation would both leak test statistics and destroy the amplitude information that persistence diagrams encode. (4) The preprocessing chain (0.05-100 Hz bandpass + mains notch at 1 kHz) is a sane, standard front end. (5) The full training recipe (LR 5e-4 with x0.1 step at half of 80-100 epochs, gradient clip 1.0, dropout 0.5) is a ready-made starting point. (6) The two-tier evaluation (subject-dependent 5-fold AND LOSO, reported side by...

**I. Research opportunity.** Several open gaps this paper leaves that our project can occupy. (1) SINGLE CHANNEL: the whole method depends on 12 simultaneous abdominal channels and on the network discriminating the fetal source "among the remaining inputs from 11 channels". There is no reduced-lead ablation at all, so the question of how much of the 88.8% survives with one channel is completely open — and that is exactly our setting. A TDA representation is a plausible answer, because persistent homology of a Takens embedding characterises the recurrent geometry of a single scalar trace and does not need spatial diversity. (2) RESOLUTION: the 65 ms hard window caps RR precision and plausibly causes the weak FHR agreement (r = 0.56, LoA spanning ~47 bpm). A method that emits sample-level peak positions — or regresses a within-window offset — should beat this on Bland-Altman even at equal window-level accuracy, and nobody has shown that here. (3) FALSE POSITIVES: PPV 78.9% is the clear weak point, and the paper's only remedy (FECGPP) is undocumented. An explicit, published post-processor — refractory period, physiological RR bounds, RR-interval consistency or a Viterbi/HMM decode over the window logits — is an obvious, publishable improvement with a stated algorithm. (4) BENCHMARKING: no public dataset, no baseline comparison, no reproducible code or data. Running the same window-classification formulation on PhysioNet 2013 / ADFECGDB / NIFECGDB with event-level F1 at the standard 50 ms tolerance, and...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

- Dataset: 70 pregnant women; Children's National Hospital USA 12 (17%), Kanagawa Children's Medical Center 13 (19%), Tohoku University Hospital 45 (64%) — Section 2.1 Dataset
- Sampling frequency 1 kHz, resolution 16 bits, 12 bipolar abdominal electrodes, 1-minute excerpt used — Section 2.1 Dataset
- Preprocessing: bandpass 0.05-100 Hz, notch at 50 Hz or 60 Hz — Section 2.1 Dataset
- Window/label duration 65 ms (between the authors' measured ~70 ms fetal QRS duration at 1 kHz and the 50 ms of Vo et al. 2020) — Section 2.2 Fetal R-peak labeling method
- Train/test split 75% / 25%; standardisation with training-set mean and SD — Section 2.3 Training and testing scheme
- BiLSTM hidden units stated as 100, 50, 20 in text and Figure 3 — Section 2.4 Fetal ECG detection network and Figure 3 block diagram
- Table 1 activations 400 / 200 / 100 with weight shapes 1600x12 & 1600x200, 800x400 & 800x100, 400x200 & 400x50, FC input 2x100 — Table 1 (these imply hidden units 200/100/50, contradicting the text)
- Dropout 0.5 at two positions — Table 1 and Figure 3
- Class weights w1 (R-peak) = 3.17, w0 (Other) = 0.59 from w_c = ns/(nc*ns_c) — Section 2.4, Eq. 2
- Training: 80-100 epochs, learning rate 0.0005 dropped by factor 0.1 halfway, gradient threshold 1, minimum sequence length 65 — Section 3 (training-phase paragraph)
- Subject-dependent 5-fold accuracy per fold 92.5 / 93.4 / 93.5 / 93.2 / 92.6, average 93.0%; F1 per fold 0.96 / 0.97 / 0.97 / 0.96 / 0.96, average 0.96 — Table 2
- With FECGPP: average accuracy 94.2%, average F1 0.97 — Section 3.1 and Figure 6B header
