### N2. fECG — cổ điển, benchmark và dữ liệu

#### p13 — Behar J, Johnson AEW, Clifford GD, Oster J. A Comparison of Single Channel Fetal ECG Extraction Methods. DOI 10.1007/s10439-014-0993-9. (Read via the author's PhD thesis Ch.6, arXiv:1606.01093, +...

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** có (`p13_Behar2014_thesis_arxiv.pdf`, 7,421,380 byte) · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** Non-invasive fetal monitoring from the maternal abdominal ECG (AECG) is hampered because the fetal ECG (FECG), the maternal ECG (MECG) and noise overlap in BOTH time and frequency, making it a hard source-separation problem. Most published solutions use blind source separation (ICA/PCA) requiring 8-16 abdominal channels, which is impractical for portable/low-cost monitors. This paper asks: among purely TEMPORAL (observation-domain) methods that need only ONE abdominal channel plus ONE maternal chest reference, which cancels the MECG best so that fetal QRS (FQRS) and fetal heart rate can be reliably detected? It also asks, for the first time systematically, how much the choice of preprocessing filter cut-offs matters. The authors introduce a novel echo state network (ESN) based MECG canceller and benchmark it against LMS, RLS and four template-subtraction variants.

**B. Data.** CRDB (Chest Reference Database), assembled by the authors: 262 min (4 h 22 min) of abdominal ECG from 9 pregnant women containing 37,452 reference FQRS annotations, all acquired at fs = 1 kHz, 16-bit. It has two disjoint parts. CRDB1 (TRAINING): 14 records manually selected from the PhysioNet Non-Invasive FECG Database (NIFECGDB), which itself is 55 multichannel recordings from a SINGLE subject at 21-40 weeks gestation, recorded with a g.BSamp biosignal amplifier; acquisition filtering 0.01-100 Hz bandpass + 50 Hz notch. The 14 selected records (154, 192, 244, 274, 290, 323, 368, 444, 597, 733, 746, 811, 826, 906) were those where FQRS were visible on at least one channel; gestational age 22-40 weeks. One minute of signal starting 30 s into each record was extracted. Two chest + three abdominal channels retained per record. GROUND TRUTH: 2,148 FQRS MANUALLY annotated by a single annotator on the channel with the most visible FECG. Each of the 3 abdominal channels treated independently -> 3 x 14 = 42 min and 3 x 2,148 = 6,444 reference FQRS. CRDB2 (TEST): eleven 5-minute records from 8 pregnant women taken from a private commercial database (IRB approved: Summa Health System RP#12018, Brigham and Women's Hospital RP#2010-P-002778/1). Each raw record has 28 abdominal channels, one maternal chest channel, and the INVASIVE fetal SCALP ECG (SECG). One chest channel and 4 abdominal channels showing a visible FECG trace were manually selected. GROUND TRUTH: a...

**C. Method.** Two-stage pipeline, repeated independently for every abdominal channel: (1) cancel the MECG from the abdominal channel, (2) run a fixed Pan-Tompkins QRS detector on the residual to get FQRS. Stage 1 methods compared: - TEMPLATE SUBTRACTION (baselines): build an average maternal cycle centred on the maternal R-peak (fixed windows 0.20 s for P, 0.10 s for QRS, 0.40 s for T) and subtract it from each incoming maternal cycle. Four adaptivity levels: TSc (single scalar scaling of the whole template, scalar chosen to minimise MSE), TSm (P, QRS, T scaled independently: a = (T'T)^-1 T'm), TSlp (past cycles weighted, rather than equally averaged, to minimise MSE), TSpca (Kanjilal-style: stack cycles into a design matrix, SVD, subtract the first r maternal principal components; larger r = more adaptive). Templates are updated online (add new cycles, drop oldest); an incoming cycle whose Pearson correlation with the template is <= 0.8 is rejected. Important implementation note: maternal QRS detection was run on the CHEST channel (higher SNR, negligible FECG), then each maternal R-peak was re-adjusted to its exact location on the abdominal channel so template and cycles align. - ADAPTIVE FILTERS: classical adaptive noise cancelling, chest channel as reference input, abdominal channel as target; LMS and RLS, both LINEAR. - ESN (novel): the chest MECG drives a fixed random recurrent reservoir (a nonlinear, high-dimensional, memory-carrying projection, analogous to a kernel), and only a...

**D. Model.** ESN (the only trained "model"; reservoir computing, NOT deep learning): - Topology: K = 1 input unit (one chest ECG channel), M = 90 internal reservoir units, L = 1 output unit. State x(n) in R^90, extended state z(n) = [x(n) u(n)] in R^91 -> readout has ~91 learned weights. That is the entire trainable parameter count; the reservoir W (90x90) and Wi (90x1) are randomly generated ONCE and never trained. - Update: leaky-integrator neurons, x(n+1) = (1 - alpha) x(n) + f(W x(n) + Wi u(n+1)), with back-projection Wb set to 0 (purely input-driven). Reservoir activation f = tanh; output y_hat(n+1) = g(wo(n) z(n)) with g = identity. - Reservoir statistics: W is a random sparse 90x90 matrix with ~20% non-zero entries (sparsity = 20%, FIXED, not optimised); W is first normalised by its dominant eigenvalue to unit spectral radius, then multiplied by rho. Wi and wo initialised ~ U[-1, 1]. Input scaling = 1 (FIXED, not optimised) -- justified because the input normalisation already maps the signal into [-1, 1]. - Tuned hyperparameters and their grids (all searched on CRDB1 only): spectral radius rho in [0,1] step 0.05 -> 0.4; leakage rate alpha in [0,1] step 0.1 -> 0.4; reservoir size M in [10,250] step 20 -> 90; high-pass cut-off fb in [1,49] Hz step 3 -> 20 Hz; low-pass cut-off fh in [30,120] Hz step 5 -> 95 Hz. RLS forgetting factor for the readout fixed at 0.999. - Search protocol: stage-wise grid search -- (1) with a standard fb=2 Hz / fh=100 Hz front-end, find workable rho and...

**E. Evaluation.** Split: a single fixed TRAIN (CRDB1) / TEST (CRDB2) split with NO cross-validation and NO random segment shuffling. All hyperparameter grid search (fb, fh, rho, alpha, M, N, mu, lambda) was performed exclusively on CRDB1; CRDB2 was used only to report final numbers. Subject-independent: YES, and stronger than that -- it is effectively CROSS-DATASET. CRDB1 is 14 records from PhysioNet NIFECGDB (a single subject, 22-40 weeks gestation, g.BSamp amplifier, 0.01-100 Hz + 50 Hz notch acquisition filtering, manual FQRS labels). CRDB2 is 11 records from 8 different women from a private commercial database (28-channel system + invasive scalp electrode, scalp-ECG-derived labels). The authors state explicitly that train and test "were recorded with different hardware, following a separate protocol and at different stages of pregnancy for different subjects." LEAKAGE RISK: LOW. There is no subject overlap, no device overlap, no segment-level shuffling, and the test set was not used for tuning. Three caveats worth recording rather than ignoring: (1) the 4 abdominal channels of a given CRDB2 record are treated as independent samples, so the 220 min / 31,008 "reference FQRS" figure is inflated ~4x relative to independent data, and per-record F1 in Table 6.3 is an average over correlated channels -- this affects effective sample size and variance estimates, not train/test contamination; (2) ESNna (and the initialisation of ESNa/LMS/RLS) fits filter weights on the 30 s of signal immediately...

**F. Results.** TEST SET CRDB2 (Se / PPV / F1 / HRm, %) -- thesis Table 6.2: TSc 86.8 / 85.3 / 86.0 / 68.1; TSm 86.4 / 85.2 / 85.8 / 67.2; TSlp 85.9 / 85.5 / 85.7 / 66.7; TSpca 89.9 / 88.8 / 89.3 / 73.9; LMS 89.3 / 86.5 / 87.9 / 73.7; RLS 89.7 / 86.8 / 88.2 / 74.5; ESNna 89.3 / 86.5 / 87.9 / 73.9; ESNa 91.4 / 88.9 / 90.2 / 78.7 (BEST on both F1 and HRm). These match the published abstract exactly (ESN 90.2, LMS 87.9, RLS 88.2, TS 89.3). TRAINING SET CRDB1 (Se / PPV / F1 / HRm, %): TSc 91.2 / 90.5 / 90.8 / 57.9; TSm 90.3 / 90.0 / 90.1 / 56.7; TSlp 91.2 / 92.8 / 92.0 / 59.7; TSpca 94.7 / 96.0 / 95.4 / 68.7; LMS 95.8 / 95.0 / 95.4 / 69.3; RLS 96.2 / 95.6 / 95.9 / 70.6; ESNna 97.2 / 97.3 / 97.2 / 73.5; ESNa 96.8 / 97.2 / 97.0 / 72.2. SQI ABLATION on CRDB2 (F1 without -> with bSQI gating, discarding 3.6% of total signal length): TSpca 89.32 -> 90.39 (+1.07); LMS 87.88 -> 88.83 (+0.95); RLS 88.23 -> 89.27 (+1.04); ESNa 90.17 -> 91.28 (+1.11). PREPROCESSING ABLATION (the largest effect in the paper): TSlp with the conventional fb = 2 Hz, fh = 95 Hz gave F1 = 83.0% (CRDB1) and 80.0% (CRDB2); with fb = 20 Hz, fh = 95 Hz it gave F1 = 92.0% (CRDB1) and 85.7% (CRDB2) -- +9.0 points on train, +5.7 on test from the high-pass cut-off alone. At fb = 10 Hz most maternal P- and T-wave energy is already removed, leaving essentially only MQRS and FQRS. PER-RECORD (Table 6.3, F1 / HRm, CRDB2, best underlined): the four methods TSpca / LMS / RLS / ESNa trade wins record by record -- e.g. record 126:...

**G. Limitations.** 1. NOT single-channel in the sense our project uses: every winning method requires a MATERNAL CHEST REFERENCE electrode in addition to the abdominal channel. The paper's "single channel" refers to a single ABDOMINAL channel. Blind, reference-free operation is deliberately out of scope (deferred to the companion paper / Chapter 7). 2. Tiny cohort: 9 women total, only 8 in the test set, 11 test records. No arrhythmias, no pathology, no preterm-versus-term stratification. Data described as "relatively good quality", so the SQI benefit is admitted to be understated relative to real noisy recordings. 3. Test ground truth is itself produced by an automatic Pan-Tompkins detector on the scalp ECG, not by human annotators -- detector-specific biases in the reference are possible; CRDB1 uses a single human annotator with no inter-rater agreement measured. 4. Large train-to-test drop (F1 ~97% -> ~90%) shows the tuned hyperparameters generalise imperfectly; the author explicitly raises the possibility that the many ESN hyperparameters were over-fitted to CRDB1. 5. No statistical testing, no confidence intervals, no per-record variance; the ESN's margin over RLS/TSpca (1-2 F1 points on 11 records) is conceded to be "not significantly different". 6. The ESN is hard to design and reproduce: many hyperparameters, random reservoir connectivity that gives no insight into the dynamics, and instability with plain RLS readout training (needed the Douglas pre-whitened RLS variant). The author...

**H. Relevance.** Several things transfer directly to our single-channel abdominal-ECG + TDA project even though the paper contains no deep learning and no topology. (a) THE EVALUATION PROTOCOL. Adopt it verbatim so our numbers are comparable to the literature: F1 (harmonic mean of Se and PPV) as the primary metric, computed with a +/-50 ms matching tolerance (NOT the 150 ms ANSI/AAMI adult window -- the paper's explicit reasoning is the higher fetal heart rate), plus HRm = fraction of FHR estimates within +/-5 bpm of reference, and per-record tables rather than a single pooled number. This exact protocol became the field standard after the PhysioNet/CinC Challenge 2013. (b) THE PREPROCESSING RECIPE, which is the paper's biggest empirical finding and is architecture-agnostic. Work at 250 Hz. Use cascaded ZERO-PHASE Butterworth filters: 3rd-order high-pass at 20 Hz and 5th-order low-pass at 95 Hz. The unusually high 20 Hz high-pass removes maternal P and T wave energy and buys 6-9 F1 points -- more than any algorithmic choice in the paper. For our TDA pipeline this matters twice over: Takens embedding and persistent homology are extremely sensitive to low-frequency baseline drift and to large-amplitude maternal P/T excursions, which would otherwise dominate the persistence diagram. Then normalise in three steps: divide by the amplitude range of the first 5 s, subtract the mean of the first 5 s, and apply tanh() -- the tanh squashing is exactly what we want to stop artifact spikes producing...

**I. Research opportunity.** The gap this paper leaves open is precisely our project's territory. 1. It never operates on a truly single electrode-pair: every method needs a maternal CHEST reference, and the paper says the ESN's advantage over LMS/RLS is that the chest-to-abdomen mapping may be nonlinear. Nobody here attempts FQRS detection from ONE abdominal channel with NO chest reference and NO multi-channel BSS. TDA on a single abdominal channel is exactly that untested regime -- and the paper's own honest conclusion that the mapping is "mostly linear" implies the residual difficulty lies not in the cancellation model but in detecting the FQRS inside the residual, which is where a topological representation of the delay-embedded signal could add value. 2. It uses a FIXED, hand-designed detector (Pan-Tompkins energy detector, 150 ms refractory) on the residual and never varies it, while explicitly noting "better results may be achieved with an alternative QRS detector". The entire detector half of the pipeline is unoptimised. Replacing that stage with a learned detector -- CNN over persistence images, or over raw windows -- is untouched territory and the paper hands us the exact front-end and evaluation harness to plug it into. 3. It shows the FQRS embedded inside the maternal QRS is the hard case (their Figure 6.5b highlights one at t=48.2 s), and that the ESN's per-record wins came specifically where the fetal-to-maternal amplitude ratio was LOW. Nobody has reported F1 stratified by fetal/maternal...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

- Test set (CRDB2) F1 = 90.2% for ESNa (adaptive echo state network) -- best method; source: thesis Table 6.2, and confirmed verbatim in the published abstract retrieved via EuropePMC REST
- Test set F1 = 89.3% TSpca, 88.2% RLS, 87.9% LMS, 87.9% ESNna, 86.0% TSc, 85.8% TSm, 85.7% TSlp; source: thesis Table 6.2 (TSpca/RLS/LMS values independently confirmed by the published abstract and by Table 6.4)
- Test set HRm (fraction of FHR within +/-5 bpm) = 78.7% ESNa, 74.5% RLS, 73.9% TSpca, 73.9% ESNna, 73.7% LMS, 68.1% TSc, 67.2% TSm, 66.7% TSlp; source: thesis Table 6.2
- Training set (CRDB1) F1 = 97.2% ESNna, 97.0% ESNa, 95.9% RLS, 95.4% LMS, 95.4% TSpca, 92.0% TSlp, 90.8% TSc, 90.1% TSm; source: thesis Table 6.2, corroborated by the running text in Section 6.3.1 which quotes LMS Se=95.8/PPV=95.0/F1=95.4, RLS Se=96.2/PPV=95.6/F1=95.9, ESN Se=97.2/PPV=97.3/F1=97.2
- SQI gating raised test F1 from 89.32 to 90.39 (TSpca, +1.07), 87.88 to 88.83 (LMS, +0.95), 88.23 to 89.27 (RLS, +1.04), 90.17 to 91.28 (ESNa, +1.11), while removing 3.6% of total signal length; source: thesis Table 6.4 and Section 6.3.3
- Preprocessing ablation: TSlp F1 = 83.0% (CRDB1) and 80.0% (CRDB2) with fb=2 Hz vs 92.0% and 85.7% with fb=20 Hz (fh=95 Hz in both); source: thesis Section 6.4 Discussion
- Optimal preprocessing cut-offs fb = 20 Hz, fh = 95 Hz; 3rd-order zero-phase Butterworth high-pass cascaded with 5th-order zero-phase Butterworth low-pass; search ranges fb in [1,49] step 3 Hz and fh in [30,120] step 5 Hz; source: thesis Section 6.2.3 and Table 6.1
- Optimal ESN hyperparameters: reservoir size M = 90 (searched [10,250] step 20), spectral radius rho = 0.4 (searched [0,1] step 0.05), leakage rate alpha = 0.4 (searched [0,1] step 0.1), reservoir sparsity 20% (fixed), input scaling 1 (fixed), readout RLS forgetting factor 0.999 (fixed); source: thesis Table 6.1 and...
- Optimal LMS: N = 20 taps, mu = 0.1 (searched N in [1,262] step 5, mu in [0.01,0.46] step 0.05). Optimal RLS: N = 20 taps, lambda = 0.999 (searched N in [1,61] step 3, lambda in [0.8,1] step 0.0025); source: thesis Table 6.1 and Section 6.3.1
- Sampling rate: raw data 1 kHz 16-bit, downsampled to 250 Hz with anti-aliasing filter before extraction; source: thesis Section 6.2.1 and Section 4.2.1
- FQRS detection tolerance = +/-50 ms (vs 150 ms for adults under ANSI/AAMI), chosen because of the higher fetal heart rate; HRm tolerance = +/-5 bpm from industrial standards; source: thesis Section 4.4.1 and Table 4.5
- FQRS detector: Pan-and-Tompkins energy detector with a 150 ms refractory period applied to the residual; source: thesis Section 6.2.3

---

#### p14 — Behar J, Oster J, Clifford GD. Combining and benchmarking methods of foetal ECG extraction without maternal or scalp electrode data. Physiol Meas 35(8):1569-1589. DOI 10.1088/0967-3334/35/8/1569....

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** không · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** Non-invasive fetal ECG analysis was, in 2014, held back less by a shortage of algorithms than by the impossibility of comparing them: every published maternal-ECG-cancellation method had been evaluated on a different private dataset, with a different preprocessing chain, a different QRS detector and a different accuracy metric, and almost none had released code. The paper attacks two coupled problems. (i) Benchmarking: re-implement 15 published NI-FECG extraction methods (template subtraction in five variants, PCA, ICA, extended-Kalman-filter-based TS, and cascades of these) and evaluate them on one common public database, with one common preprocessing chain, one common QRS detector, one common channel-selection rule and one common metric, then open-source everything. (ii) Detection: build the best possible fetal-QRS/FHR estimator under the hard constraint that only four abdominal electrodes are available - no maternal chest reference (which the authors motivate by low chest signal quality corrupting adaptive filters, by cultural settings where chest electrodes are inappropriate, and by portable monitors with limited channel counts) and no fetal scalp electrode (which is invasive and only available intrapartum after membrane rupture). The paper is the authors' entry to, and post-hoc analysis of, the PhysioNet/Computing in Cardiology Challenge 2013.

**B. Data.** PhysioNet/CinC Challenge 2013 database. 447 records, each exactly 1 minute long, resampled/reformatted to fs = 1 kHz, each with 4 non-invasive abdominal channels (no chest lead in the released data). Pooled from five sources (thesis Table 4.2): ADFECGDB (25 records, public), NIFECGDB (14, public), AFECGDB (20, artificially generated with the fecgsyn dipole simulator), PNIFECGDB (340, private non-invasive database donated for the Challenge), PSFECGDB (48, private, never released, used only to score open-source entries in events E1-E2). Number of pregnant women is NOT REPORTED anywhere - the text only says the data 'were recorded with different hardware, following separate protocols and on a number of pregnant women', and that this allows testing 'on unseen patients'. Ground truth (reference FQRS annotations) was created three different ways depending on the source: for PSFECGDB and ADFECGDB the reference FQRS came from a simultaneously recorded fetal SCALP ECG; for the other non-invasive datasets the annotations were 'derived manually, or through additional abdominal maternal ECG leads that were not available to competitors'; for AFECGDB the FQRS were known exactly from the simulation parameters. A subset of both the initial and final reference annotations was manually verified by two Challenge organisers (Ikaro Silva, Joachim Behar) using the LightWAVE annotation viewer, but the authors state plainly that 'some minor errors in annotations persisted', and 7 Set A records...

**C. Method.** An eight-block deterministic pipeline. (1) Prefiltering of all four abdominal channels: cascaded zero-phase Butterworth low-pass (order 5) and high-pass (order 3), plus a conditional 50/60 Hz notch, plus range/mean normalisation followed by tanh compression. (2) Maternal QRS detection, run on each prefiltered channel and additionally on ICA-transformed channels (ICA is used here purely to boost the maternal component on some components, making MQRS detection easier). (3) Selection of one MQRS series - necessary because no chest lead exists and because on some channels the fetal ECG is of comparable amplitude to the maternal, which would otherwise generate a flood of false positives; selection is by a smoothing indicator on the RR series, assuming at least one of the four channels is maternally dominant. (4) Source separation to cancel the maternal ECG, producing 'residual' channels. The 15 methods are grouped into four classes: class I performs template subtraction in the observation domain (TS = plain subtraction, TSc = template scaled by one constant fitted by least squares, TSm = P, QRS and T segments scaled independently by a 3-vector fitted by least squares, TSlp = template built by MSE-optimal weighting of previous cycles rather than equal weights, TSpca = Kanjilal-style PCA-based template subtraction, TSEKF = extended Kalman filter with a Gaussian-mixture ECG model); class II applies BSS directly to the four abdominal channels (PCA, ICA); class III applies template...

**D. Model.** There is NO learned model and no trainable parameters - the 'architecture' is a fixed signal-processing graph with ~6 tuned scalars. Input: 4 x 60,000 samples (4 channels, 60 s, 1 kHz). Output: a variable-length list of fetal R-peak sample indices, converted to an RR series and a 12-point-per-minute FHR trace. Concrete settings. Prefilter: zero-phase Butterworth, low-pass order 5 at fh = 99 Hz, high-pass order 3 at fb = 10 Hz; notch at 50 or 60 Hz applied only when the power-spectrum peak inside the 50/60 +/- 4 Hz band falls within 50/60 +/- 1 Hz. Normalisation: x <- tanh((x - mean(x[1s:5s])) / range(x[1s:5s])), which bounds the signal, shrinks high-amplitude artefacts into the ECG range, leaves the centred ECG (nominally within [-0.5, 0.5]) nearly linear, and satisfies the scale-sensitivity requirement of PCA/ICA. Template subtraction: template built from the nbC = 20 most recent maternal cycles (TSlp uses 9 per the original publication), with fixed segment durations P = 0.20 s, QRS = 0.10 s, T = 0.40 s centred on the maternal R-peak; the template is updated online by adding incoming cycles and dropping the oldest; an incoming cycle whose Pearson correlation with the template is r <= 0.8 is rejected from the update (misdetection guard). TSpca uses nbPC = 2 principal components. TSEKF uses covariance gains GR = 100 (trust in observations) and GQ = 5 (trust in model), found by random search; the authors note the random-search surface shows high GR with low GQ is optimal,...

**E. Evaluation.** Two-tier evaluation. Tier 1 (in-sample): all Se/PPV/F1 numbers in the results table are computed on Set A, which is also the only set used to tune fb, fh, nbC, nbPC, GR, GQ and the empirical thresholds (29 bpm SMI, 0.4 BCM, 143 bpm and SMI > 26 for FUSE-CHALL). F1 is a gross average over records; the first and last 2 s of each 1-min segment are discarded; a match is a detection within +/- 50 ms of the reference (tightened from the ANSI/AAMI adult 150 ms because of the higher fetal heart rate); 7 of the 75 Set A records (a33, a38, a47, a52, a54, a71, a74) were excluded after visual inspection revealed inaccurate reference annotations, leaving 68 records. Tier 2 (held out): the official Challenge scores E1-E5 were produced by the organisers at the MIT Laboratory for Computational Physiology on Sets B and C, which the authors never saw - E1/E2 by uploading source code to a private PhysioNet server that executed it on Set C, E4/E5 by submitting an annotation file scored against expert annotations on Set B, with WFDB 10.5.19 and a heavy penalty for unannotated records. Entries were limited by phase (3, then 5, then 1). LEAKAGE JUDGEMENT: LOW risk for the headline Challenge scores. This is not a random-segment split - it is a record-wise split into disjoint public/private sets, drawn from five different acquisition sources with different hardware and protocols, scored by an independent third party on data the authors could not download, which is close to the strongest evaluation...

**F. Results.** Set A (in-sample), fb = 10 Hz, Se/PPV/F1 in %, with HRE and RRE being the Challenge-style HR and RR error scores. Class I template subtraction: TS 81.8/81.7/81.6 (HRE 655.5, RRE 27.9); TSc 81.6/81.7/81.5; TSm 82.2/82.2/82.1; TSlp 82.1/81.9/81.8; TSpca 88.1/84.5/86.1 (HRE 594.4, RRE 21.6); TSEKF 83.0/81.1/81.9. Class II BSS alone: ICA 69.1/60.0/63.7 (HRE 2852.1); PCA 57.4/47.9/51.6 (HRE 3892.1). Class III TS-then-BSS: TS-ICA 93.0/91.1/92.0; TSc-ICA 93.2/92.0/92.6; TSm-ICA 91.7/90.8/91.2; TSlp-ICA 88.4/88.7/88.4; TSpca-ICA 93.8/92.2/93.0 (HRE 153.2, RRE 16.9 - best individual method). Class IV BSS-then-TS: ICA-TSpca 90.1/88.8/89.2; ICA-TSpca-ICA 92.6/92.2/92.4. Controls and fusion: CONST-HR at 143 bpm 23.2/23.1/23.0 but HRE only 172.2 and RRE only 8.9; FUSE 95.6/94.3/95.0 (HRE 132.9, RRE 12.7); FUSE-SMOOTH 95.9/96.0/96.0 (HRE 19.1, RRE 6.3); FUSE-CHALL HRE 5.4, RRE 2.3 (F1 not applicable). Ablation of the high-pass cut-off, reported as a second F1 column at fb = 2 Hz: TS 81.6 -> 81.2, TSc 81.5 -> 81.4, TSm 82.1 -> 81.1, TSlp 81.8 -> 78.5, TSpca 86.1 -> 83.6, TSEKF 81.9 -> 78.4, TSpca-ICA 93.0 -> 92.4, FUSE 95.0 -> 94.2, FUSE-SMOOTH 96.0 -> 95.2 - i.e. the aggressive 10 Hz high-pass helps every method, by up to 3.5 F1 points, because it strips the maternal P and T waves and leaves essentially only maternal and fetal QRS energy. Hyper-parameter searches: grid search selected fb = 10 Hz and fh = 99 Hz; the nbC sweep selected 20 cycles; TSpca was best at 2 principal components;...

**G. Limitations.** (1) Four abdominal channels are assumed throughout. The high-performing configurations are exactly those that need multichannel BSS: with one channel, classes II, III and IV are unavailable and the method reduces to class I, i.e. F1 81.5-86.1% rather than 96.0%. (2) Even four channels are too few: the authors cite Sameni's estimate that 4-6 statistically uncorrelated dimensions are needed to represent ECG, which is why ICA and PCA applied directly to the mixture perform so badly (F1 63.7 and 51.6) and why a TS pre-step, which reduces the dimensionality of the problem, is needed before BSS helps. (3) PCA-specific flaws are acknowledged: the fetal and maternal sources have no reason to be orthogonal, PCA is sensitive to variable scaling, and removing second-order dependencies may not be the right criterion. (4) The Se/PPV/F1 table is training-set performance on the data used for tuning. (5) Reference annotations contain errors; 7 of 75 Set A records were dropped for this reason and the authors state minor errors persist in the rest. (6) No pathology whatsoever in the database - no arrhythmia, ST deviation, QT prolongation, late or early decelerations, or contractions - and the authors explicitly warn that most evaluated methods would degrade with a change in beat type such as an ectopic beat, proposing switching Kalman filters over beat-type modes as future work. (7) Records are only 1 min long, so long-term non-stationarity, fetal movement and electrode drift are untested....

**H. Relevance.** This paper is our benchmark and protocol anchor rather than a detector to copy, and it is unusually valuable in that role. First, it fixes the honest target. Our project is single-channel; every configuration in this paper above F1 ~89% requires four channels and BSS, which we structurally cannot use. The single-channel-feasible ceiling reported here is class I: TS at F1 81.6% and TSpca at F1 86.1% (Se 88.1, PPV 84.5). So a single-channel TDA+CNN detector should be compared against ~86% F1 on PhysioNet 2013 Set A, not against the 96.0% headline; beating 86% single-channel would be a real result, and any paper that quotes 96% as the bar is comparing across channel counts. Second, it gives us a protocol we can adopt verbatim so our numbers are commensurable: F1 with a +/- 50 ms match window (not the adult 150 ms), gross averaging across records, first and last 2 s of each 1-min segment discarded, and the named exclusion list a33/a38/a47/a52/a54/a71/a74. Third, the preprocessing findings transfer directly and matter more for us than for them. The 10 Hz high-pass ablation is the single most useful number in the paper for a TDA pipeline: a Takens embedding of an abdominal ECG is dominated by whatever carries the most energy and the strongest quasi-periodicity, which is the maternal complex including its P and T waves; a 10 Hz high-pass removes most of the maternal P/T energy and should therefore substantially reshape the delay-embedded point cloud and its persistence diagram....

**I. Research opportunity.** The paper leaves the entire learning problem untouched, and says so. (1) No learned detector: the fetal QRS detector is a hand-tuned Pan-and-Tompkins variant with fixed thresholds, and the fusion stage is a winner-take-all pick by an empirically thresholded RR-regularity heuristic that the authors call 'very simplistic', with 'room for improvement in the approach to combine the FQRS detection performed on different channels using different methods'. A learned selector, or an end-to-end learned detector, is an explicitly invited contribution. (2) The selection criterion is circular and anti-clinical: preferring the smoothest RR series systematically penalises true beat-to-beat fetal variability, which is precisely the clinical signal of interest; nothing in the pipeline estimates detection quality from the waveform itself. The authors point at Liu et al.'s sample-entropy SQI as 'theoretically more sensible' but did not adopt it. A data-driven, per-window confidence score - which a CNN over persistence images can produce natively - would replace SMI on principled grounds. (3) The single-channel case is left open. The paper establishes that BSS collapses without multiple channels and that the single-channel ceiling is TSpca at F1 86.1%, but offers nothing better for one channel; that is exactly the gap our project sits in. (4) Non-stationarity and morphology change break their templates: they state that TS/EKF performance degrades with beat-type change and that the EKF's...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

- 447 records, 1 min each, fs = 1 kHz, 4 abdominal channels (thesis Ch.4.2.2 text and Table 4.2)
- Database composition: ADFECGDB 25, NIFECGDB 14, AFECGDB 20 (synthetic), PNIFECGDB 340, PSFECGDB 48, total 447 (thesis Table 4.2)
- Splits: Set A = 75 records (records + annotations public), Set B = 100 (records public only), Set C = 272 (fully withheld) (thesis Ch.4.2.2 bullet list)
- 53 international teams, 93 open-source entries submitted to the Challenge (thesis Ch.7.4.3)
- Held-out Challenge scores for this paper: E1 = 179.44 bpm^2, E2 = 20.79 ms, E3 = 153.07 ms^2, E4 = 29.62 bpm^2, E5 = 4.67 ms (journal abstract via PubMed PMID 25069410; thesis Ch.7.3.2 and 7.5 give 179.4 / 20.8 / 153.1 / 29.6 / 4.7)
- Rank: best of all 53 teams on E1 and E2; 3rd and 2nd best on E4 and E5 (thesis Ch.7.5; abstract says 3rd/2nd for E3, E4, E5)
- Best Challenge scores achieved by anyone: E1 179.4, E2 20.8 (this paper), E4 18.1, E5 4.3 (Andreotti et al.) (thesis Ch.7.4.3 text and Table 7.3)
- Andreotti et al. post-Challenge refinement: E4 = 15.1, E5 = 3.3 (thesis Table 7.4)
- TS: Se 81.8, PPV 81.7, F1 81.6, HRE 655.5, RRE 27.9 (thesis Table 7.2, Set A, fb = 10 Hz)
- TSc: Se 81.6, PPV 81.7, F1 81.5, HRE 514.8 (thesis Table 7.2)
- TSm: Se 82.2, PPV 82.2, F1 82.1, HRE 551.9 (thesis Table 7.2)
- TSlp: Se 82.1, PPV 81.9, F1 81.8, HRE 902.0 (thesis Table 7.2)

#### p15 — Andreotti F, Behar J, Zaunseder S, Oster J, Clifford GD. An open-source framework for stress-testing non-invasive foetal ECG extraction algorithms. Physiological Measurement 37(5):627-648, 2016. DOI...

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** có (`p15_Andreotti2016-FECGSYNDB.pdf`, 1,834,088 byte) · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** Published NI-FECG extraction papers claim near-perfect fetal QRS detection but cannot be meaningfully compared: public annotated abdominal databases are scarce and small, every group uses a different performance measure and matching tolerance, and almost no reference implementations are open source. There is also no way, on real data, to know the true fetal ECG waveform underneath the maternal ECG and noise, so morphological claims (fetal QT, T/QRS ratio) are unverifiable. The paper's problem is therefore methodological rather than algorithmic: build a standardised, reproducible stress-testing framework - data, reference algorithms and evaluation routines - that can rank NI-FECG extraction methods and expose where they fail, particularly under non-stationary physiological events.

**B. Data.** Dataset: FECGSYNDB, newly created for this paper and released on PhysioNet as part of the fecgsyn toolbox (www.physionet.org/physiotools/ipmcode/fecgsyn/) under GNU GPL. It is fully synthetic - there are no human subjects. Generation: the fecgsyn simulator (Behar et al 2014c), an extension of the McSharry et al 2003 dynamical ECG model adapted to NI-FECG by Sameni et al 2007, represents maternal and fetal hearts as punctual dipoles of different magnitude and spatial position inside a cylindrical volume conductor, and propagates each signal component (fetal ECG, maternal ECG, noise) independently onto the electrode positions. Design: 7 scenarios (Table 1: Baseline = mixture with no noise or events; Case 0 = baseline + noise; Case 1 = fetal movement + noise; Case 2 = maternal/fetal HR acceleration-deceleration + noise; Case 3 = uterine contraction + noise; Case 4 = ectopic beats for both fetus and mother + noise; Case 5 = additional NI-FECG i.e. twin pregnancy + noise) x 10 heart-dipole model instantiations drawn at random from 9 available vectorcardiograms x 5 additive-noise levels (SNR of MECG over noise = 0, 3, 6, 9, 12 dB) x 5 repetitions with noise regenerated = 1750 recordings. Each recording is 5 min long on 34 channels (32 abdominal + 2 MECG reference), giving 145.8 h of multichannel data and ~1.1 million fetal QRS complexes. Sampling rate fs = 250 Hz (Table 2). Other Table 2 parameters: SNR of FECG relative to MECG ~ N(-9, 2) dB; fetal HR ~ N(135, 25) bpm; maternal...

**C. Method.** A three-experiment benchmarking protocol applied to eight open-source NI-FECG extraction algorithms in three families. (1) Blind source separation: BSSpca (principal component analysis) and BSSica (independent component analysis via JADE, and via FAST-ICA in both symmetric and deflationary variants). Mixing matrices are re-estimated on every 60 s block and used to process the following 60 s, so the method partially tracks non-stationarity while respecting the imposed 60 s initialisation limit. A PCA dimension-reduction step retaining 99.9% of data variance precedes ICA. (2) Template subtraction: TSc (Cerutti et al 1986, template adapted per beat by a scalar gain), TSpca (Kanjilal et al 1997, stacks maternal cycles, selects principal components, back-projects beat-by-beat), TSekf (extended Kalman filter, Sameni 2008 / Andreotti et al 2014, continuous adaptive sample-by-sample MECG estimation). (3) Adaptive methods using a single maternal reference lead (channel 33): AMlms (least mean squares, Widrow et al 1975), AMrls (recursive least squares), AMesn (echo state neural network, Behar et al 2014b) which handles nonlinear reference-to-abdominal mappings. Selection criteria for inclusion were: GPL-3.0-compatible open source, top-scoring in the PhysioNet/CinC 2013 Challenge, restricted inputs (MQRS reference plus at most one MECG lead plus abdominal channels), best-per-channel output evaluation, and a maximum 60 s initialisation window with no offline smoothing. After...

**D. Model.** There is no proposed neural network and no supervised training anywhere in this paper - the models are unsupervised/adaptive signal-processing operators, so most standard architecture fields do not exist. What is specified: (i) Detector back-end, identical for all methods: an adapted Pan-Tompkins algorithm (Behar et al 2014a); no layer/kernel structure, it is the classical band-pass -> derivative -> squaring -> moving-window-integration -> adaptive-threshold pipeline. Input to it is a single extracted channel at 250 Hz; output is a list of FQRS sample indices. (ii) FAST-ICA: maximum number of iterations set to 1000 x Nch where Nch is the number of channels; hyperbolic tangent nonlinearity as contrast function; both symmetric and deflationary orthogonalisation evaluated. (iii) JADE: default parameters. (iv) PCA reduction: keeps principal components accounting for 99.9% of the data variance; in practice yielded between 3 and 8 retained components depending on the dataset and simulated case. (v) BSS block processing: mixing matrix estimated on each 60 s window, applied to the next 60 s window. (vi) TSpca: template built from 20 maternal cycles, updated on every cycle, back-propagation step per beat. (vii) TSekf: initialised on the first 30 MECG cycles; models the FECG as white Gaussian noise (a modelling simplification the authors blame for its weaker F1). (viii) TSc: single template adapted per beat by a scalar gain. (ix) AMesn: an echo state network adaptive filter taken...

**E. Evaluation.** There is no train/test split and no cross-validation because no model is fitted to labelled data - all eight methods are unsupervised or online-adaptive and every one of the 1750 synthetic records is a test record. Evaluation is exhaustive over the full 145.8 h. Statistical comparison is done non-parametrically on the distribution of per-record medians: Kruskal-Wallis to test the effect of SNR within each method, a two-tailed Friedman test to test the effect of case and of method at each SNR level separately, and a post hoc Sign test for paired method-vs-method differences (Fig 5), with significance reported at p < 0.05, p < 0.01 and p < 0.001. Results are reported as median (interquartile range) rather than mean, which is appropriate for the heavy-tailed F1 distributions shown in Fig 4. LEAKAGE JUDGEMENT: classical train/test leakage risk is LOW-to-not-applicable, since nothing is trained on the evaluation labels and the entire corpus is synthetic - there are no subjects to split across. HOWEVER there is a serious and explicitly acknowledged form of ground-truth leakage into the method pipeline: 'the channel (or source) with highest F1 was selected for every 1 min epoch and gross statistics were reported' (section 2.4.1, repeated for experiment 2 in 2.4.2). Channel/component selection is therefore performed using the test annotations, so the reported figures are oracle upper bounds, not achievable online performance. The authors state this is deliberate - it sidesteps the...

**F. Results.** Experiment 1 (channels): F1 rises steeply from 2 to 4 channels and plateaus by 8; beyond Nch = 8 there is little gain, and without dimension reduction preliminary tests showed a continuous F1 decrease once Nch exceeded 8 (ICA square-mixing assumption violated). At 8 channels the mean F1 values were 97.46% (ICA-JADE), 97.40% (PCA) and 97.22% (FAST-ICA, symmetric and deflationary essentially identical). Adding the 99.9%-variance PCA reduction raised mean ICA F1 at Nch = 16 from 95.3% to 97.3%. JADE was selected for experiments 2-3, and 8 channels fixed. Experiment 2 (Table 3a, overall median F1 with IQR): BSSica 99.9 (6.4)%, BSSpca 98.7 (7.9)%, AMesn 97.9 (12.5)%, AMrls 97.3 (14.6)%, AMlms 97.1 (15.0)%, TSpca 96.0 (18.5)%, TSc 95.0 (18.8)%, TSekf 94.5 (19.8)%. The abstract summarises the family winners as 99.9% BSS, 97.9% AM, 96.0% TS. Per case, BSSica leads on Baseline (100.0), Case 0 (100.0), Case 2 HR accel/decel (100.0) and Case 5 twins (99.9), but loses on the highly non-stationary cases: on Case 1 fetal movement AMesn is best at 99.7 (2.7) vs BSSica 99.7 (3.9); on Case 3 uterine contraction BSSpca is best at 94.5 (7.2) vs BSSica 89.4 (23.2); on Case 4 ectopic beats TSekf is best at 88.4 (10.8) vs BSSica 87.1 (6.6) - Case 4 is the hardest for every method. Table 3b overall median MAE: TSekf 3.8 (2.7) ms is best, then AMesn 4.0 (1.5), BSSica 4.1 (1.9), AMlms 4.2 (1.5), AMrls 4.2 (1.6), BSSpca 4.4 (1.7), TSc 4.4 (1.5), TSpca 4.4 (1.6). So the best-F1 method is not the...

**G. Limitations.** (1) Entirely synthetic - no real abdominal recordings, no clinical annotations; the authors state the findings 'need to be validated using clinical data and expert annotations'. (2) The simulator is too simple: three orthogonal dipole projections give 3+3 cardiac sources whereas Sameni et al 2007 found adult ECG needs 4-8 components; the paper concedes 'these considerations enable BSS techniques to achieve better performance with a low number of channels', i.e. the near-perfect BSS scores are partly an artefact of the model. (3) Beats are modelled with linear phase, so beats are merely stretched/compressed - no T-wave prolongation, no ST variation, no QT hysteresis, no beat-to-beat P/QRS/T amplitude or width modulation. (4) The FECG was derived from adult ECG signals, producing non-physiological FQT: real FQT is roughly 200-340 ms whereas the simulated range is lower. Amplitudes and P-QRS-T ratios likewise disregard pathophysiological values. (5) Oracle channel/component selection using ground truth (highest-F1 channel per 1 min epoch), so the component-selection / ICA permutation-indeterminacy problem - which the authors call 'a problematic and decisive step' - is excluded; all reported numbers are upper bounds. (6) MAE is computed only on true positives and against a reference not re-aligned per channel, so a small systematic timing bias exists and MAE must be read alongside F1. (7) The morphological reference is a silver standard from ecgpuwave, an adult-trained...

**H. Relevance.** This paper is a protocol and data resource for our project, not an architecture source. Concretely usable: (a) FECGSYNDB itself - 145.8 h, ~1.1M exactly labelled fetal beats with the clean pre-mixture FECG also available - is an excellent pre-training and debugging corpus for a TDA+CNN detector, and lets us verify that persistence-image features actually encode fetal QRS geometry before we spend real-data labels. (b) The evaluation contract we should adopt verbatim so our numbers are comparable to the whole NI-FECG literature: SE, PPV and F1 with a 50 ms matching window (not 150 ms), plus MAE over true positives in ms, reported as median with IQR, with per-case and per-SNR breakdowns rather than one pooled figure. (c) The seven-case taxonomy is a ready-made robustness ablation. Our TDA pitch is that Takens embedding plus persistent homology captures the recurrent geometric structure of the fetal cycle independently of amplitude and of stationarity assumptions - exactly the regime (Case 1 fetal movement, Case 3 uterine contraction, Case 4 ectopics) where the BSS methods here break down, because ICA's stationarity assumption is violated. That gives our project a principled hypothesis to test rather than a generic accuracy claim. (d) Baseline numbers to beat: for single-lead-comparable methods the ceiling is AMesn 97.9% and TSpca 96.0% overall median F1, with Case 4 at only 88.4% for the best method of any family. (e) A concrete preprocessing recipe at 250 Hz: 3-100 Hz...

**I. Research opportunity.** The paper leaves several openings that map directly onto our project. First and largest: it benchmarks only classical unsupervised extraction - not a single learned detector, and certainly no topological or deep model - so FECGSYNDB has an established, well-characterised classical leaderboard but no modern learning entry; publishing a single-channel learned detector scored under exactly this protocol fills an explicit hole. Second: every method here needs either multiple abdominal channels (BSS collapses below 4 channels) or an MQRS/MECG reference; a genuinely single-channel detector that needs neither reference is untested in this framework, and the paper's own data show that at Nch = 2 F1 drops to roughly 93-95% (Fig 3), which is the bar for a reference-free single-channel method. Third: the oracle channel selection is admitted to be unresolved - 'the component selection, representing the foetal signal, is a problematic and decisive step' - so an automatic, annotation-free selection or a method that avoids selection entirely (as a single-channel detector does) is an open problem the authors flag. Fourth: the hardest cases are quantified and unsolved - ectopic beats cap out at 88.4% F1 and uterine contractions at 94.5%; these are precisely the non-stationary, morphology-perturbing regimes where an amplitude-invariant topological representation has a plausible mechanistic advantage, and nobody has tested that. Fifth: morphology extraction is essentially broken at low SNR...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

- 145.8 h of multichannel simulated data and ~1.1 million fetal QRS complexes in FECGSYNDB (Abstract, and Section 2.1 p629-630)
- 1750 synthetic recordings = 7 cases x 10 dipole-model draws x 5 SNR levels x 5 repetitions (Section 2.1, p629)
- Each recording 5 min, 34 channels = 32 abdominal + 2 MECG reference (Section 2.1, p630)
- Sampling frequency fs = 250 Hz (Table 2, p630)
- SNR of MECG over noise = {0, 3, 6, 9, 12} dB; SNR of FECG relative to MECG ~ N(-9, 2) dB (Table 2, p630)
- Fetal heart rate ~ N(135, 25) bpm; maternal heart rate ~ N(80, 20) bpm (Table 2, p630)
- Fetal HR accel/decel ~ N(30, 10) bpm; maternal ~ N(20, 10) bpm; fetal respiration ~ N(0.90, 0.05) Hz; maternal respiration ~ N(0.25, 0.05) Hz (Table 2, p630)
- FQRS matching window 50 ms, versus the adult ANSI/AAMI/ISO EC57 standard of 150 ms (Section 2.3.1, p633)
- Overall median F1 (IQR): BSSica 99.9 (6.4)%, BSSpca 98.7 (7.9)%, AMesn 97.9 (12.5)%, AMrls 97.3 (14.6)%, AMlms 97.1 (15.0)%, TSpca 96.0 (18.5)%, TSc 95.0 (18.8)%, TSekf 94.5 (19.8)% (Table 3a, p642)
- Family-best overall median FQRS accuracy: 99.9% BSS, 97.9% AM, 96.0% TS (Abstract, and Table 3a)
- Overall median MAE (IQR) in ms: TSekf 3.8 (2.7) best, AMesn 4.0 (1.5), BSSica 4.1 (1.9), AMlms 4.2 (1.5), AMrls 4.2 (1.6), BSSpca 4.4 (1.7), TSc 4.4 (1.5), TSpca 4.4 (1.6) (Table 3b, p642)
- Hardest case, Case 4 ectopic beats: best median F1 only 88.4 (10.8)% by TSekf; BSSica 87.1 (6.6)% (Table 3a, p642)

#### p16 — Niknazar M, Rivet B, Jutten C. Fetal ECG Extraction by Extended State Kalman Filtering Based on Single-Channel Recordings. DOI 10.1109/TBME.2012.2234456 (HAL hal-00831928)

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** có (`p16_Niknazar2013-EKF.pdf`, 1,614,040 byte) · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** Extracting the fetal ECG from an abdominal recording when only ONE electrode/channel is available. The fetal ECG is buried under a much stronger maternal ECG plus noise. Multichannel blind-source-separation methods (ICA, piCA, multichannel Kalman) outperform adaptive filters but need several sensors, which the authors argue is impractical for cheap, portable, long-term home monitoring. Existing single-channel approaches fail in a specific way: phase-space / PCA smoothing methods require empirical time-lag selection, wipe out inter-beat variation and need a correct embedding dimension that changes with noise statistics; and the prior single-channel EKF (Sameni's thesis) models only the dominant mECG and treats the fECG as Gaussian noise, so it fails precisely when maternal and fetal QRS waves overlap in time. The paper's stated goal is to find out what performance is achievable from one electrode by explicitly modelling the recording as a superposition of several named cardiac sources.

**B. Data.** Four data sources. (1) SYNTHETIC: mixtures of mECG + fECG + white Gaussian noise generated from a three-dimensional canonical dipole ECG model (Sameni et al. 2007) inspired by the McSharry single-channel dynamic model; sampling frequency 500 Hz; 20,000 samples per signal (= 40 s); 100 Monte-Carlo trials per parameter value; mECG power normalised to 1 (0 dB) with 5% random fluctuation; default fECG/mECG amplitude ratio 0.3; default maternal HR 1.1 Hz and fetal HR 2 Hz; 5% random per-beat fluctuation in amplitude and duration (1% in the heart-rate-ratio experiment); random initial phases. (2) DaISy fetal ECG database: a single cutaneous recording from one pregnant woman, 8 channels total (5 abdominal + 3 thoracic), 250 Hz, 10 s. (3) PhysioNet Non-Invasive Fetal ECG Database: 55 multichannel abdominal recordings taken from a SINGLE subject between 21 and 40 weeks of pregnancy; each has 2 thoracic and 3 or 4 abdominal signals; 1 kHz, 16-bit, 0.01-100 Hz bandpass and 50 Hz notch; records ecgca771 (ch 3), ecgca274 (ch 5), ecgca748 (ch 4), ecgca997 (ch 3), first 20 s used. (4) Twin fetal MCG recorded by a SQUID biomagnetometer at the Biomagnetic Center, Friedrich Schiller University Jena: 208 channels, 1025 Hz, 30 minutes; dataset q00002252, channels 92, 126, 152, 160, 8 s displayed. GROUND TRUTH: exists only for the synthetic data, where the true mECG, fECG and noise components are known by construction; on real data there is NO annotated ground truth at all - the authors say...

**C. Method.** Nonlinear Bayesian (extended Kalman) filtering with an EXTENDED STATE that models every cardiac source in the mixture simultaneously. Each ECG source is described by the polar-coordinate dynamic ECG model: the signal amplitude z is the sum of five Gaussian kernels indexed by the wave set {P, Q, R, S, T}, each with amplitude alpha_i, width b_i and phase centre psi_i, evaluated on a cardiac phase theta that advances linearly and wraps at 2*pi. For N sources the state is the concatenation [theta^(1), z^(1), ..., theta^(N), z^(N)]^T and the observation is [phi^(1), ..., phi^(N), s]^T, where each phi^(n) is a pseudo-measurement of that source's phase (obtained by linearly wrapping its own R-R intervals into [0, 2*pi) from detected R-peaks) and s is the single recorded channel, equal to the sum of the z^(n) plus measurement noise. This is called par-EKF (par-EKS with the backward smoother) and is contrasted with the conventional deflation scheme seq-EKF/seq-EKS, which extracts the dominant mECG first while calling everything else Gaussian noise, subtracts it, and re-runs on the residual. The authors' argument, borne out in the results, is that the sequential scheme's Gaussian-noise assumption is violated whenever a strong second cardiac source is present, so its errors propagate down the deflation chain, whereas the parallel scheme assumes Gaussianity only for the state and measurement noise vectors and therefore survives full temporal overlap of maternal and fetal QRS complexes.

**D. Model.** Architecture (state-space, no neural network): per source n, x^(n)_k = [theta^(n)_k, z^(n)_k]^T. Process equations: theta_{k+1} = (theta_k + omega*delta) mod 2*pi; z_{k+1} = -sum_{i in {P,Q,R,S,T}} (alpha_i * omega * delta / b_i^2) * dtheta_{i,k} * exp(-dtheta_{i,k}^2 / (2*b_i^2)) + z_k + eta_k, where dtheta_{i,k} = (theta_k - psi_i) mod 2*pi, delta is the sampling period, omega the phase increment and eta_k the state noise. INPUT SHAPE: one scalar sample per time step (the abdominal channel), plus N phase pseudo-observations per time step derived from the R-peak trains; there is no fixed-length window - the filter is a per-sample recursion over the whole record (40 s synthetic, 20 s NI-FECG, 10 s DaISy, 8 s MCG shown). STATE DIMENSION: 2N (N=2 for mECG+fECG, N=3 for the twin case). OBSERVATION DIMENSION: N+1. OUTPUT SHAPE: N separated waveforms, one sample each per time step. TRAINING PROCEDURE: none in the machine-learning sense; it is per-record parameter fitting. Step 1: detect R-peaks of all sources - maternal peaks are easy because the mECG dominates; fetal peaks are bootstrapped from a rough fECG estimate obtained by first running seq-EKF. Step 2: time-warp each beat (defined by two consecutive R-peaks) onto [0, 2*pi). Step 3: average all time-warped beats for each phase to get the mean waveform. Step 4: fit the 15 Gaussian parameters per source (alpha_i, b_i, psi_i for the 5 waves) to that mean waveform by nonlinear least squares, giving the MMSE parameter estimate....

**E. Evaluation.** There is NO train/test split and NO cross-validation, because nothing is trained in the supervised sense. Synthetic evaluation is Monte Carlo: 100 independent trials at each value of each swept parameter, with mean curves plus min/max envelopes reported (Figs. 2-4). Real-data evaluation is qualitative waveform comparison on 1 DaISy record, 4 NI-FECG records from a single subject, and 1 twin-MCG dataset, with one quantitative similarity figure (cosine 0.92 between the par-EKS estimate and the multichannel piCA reference on DaISy). LEAKAGE JUDGEMENT: classical ML leakage risk is LOW - there is no learned classifier that could memorise subjects, and the synthetic-to-real direction is safe. However two evaluation weaknesses matter more than leakage here and should be recorded as such. (a) TRANSDUCTIVE PARAMETER FITTING: the 15 Gaussian parameters per source are fitted to the very recording on which the method is then evaluated, so no held-out generalisation is ever demonstrated; the method is tuned per record by construction. (b) ORACLE INFORMATION: the algorithm consumes R-peak locations that are themselves derived from the test signal, and in the twin-MCG experiment the authors state the R-peak oracle was obtained using SEVERAL sensors and FastICA - i.e. multichannel information leaks into an experiment presented as single-channel. The authors are honest about this ("the crucial part of the proposed method is the R-peaks detection"; "the oracle is obtained using several...

**F. Results.** SYNTHETIC, SNR sweep (Fig. 2, input noise power -30 to +20 dB): both EKF and EKS improve SNR across the whole range; at -30 dB noise the MINIMUM fECG SNR improvement is 40 dB; even at +20 dB noise the fECG SNR improvement remains over 20 dB; mECG SNR improvement is roughly 20-25 dB. EKF is more effective when a relatively clean signal is available, while EKS significantly outperforms EKF as noise power grows - the authors explain this as the backward smoothing over-filtering an already-clean signal but successfully removing noise from a very noisy one. SYNTHETIC, amplitude-ratio sweep (Fig. 3, fECG/mECG amplitude ratio 0.1 to 1, mECG at 0 dB with 5% fluctuation, input SNR w.r.t. mECG 10 dB, HRs 1.1 and 2 Hz): fetal SIR improvement stays over 30 dB for ALL amplitude ratios, with EKS slightly better than EKF. SYNTHETIC, heart-rate sweep (Fig. 4, fetal HR 0.3 to 3.6 Hz, maternal HR fixed at 1.1 Hz, 1% fluctuation): the SIR curve has three deep local minima at heart-rate ratios 1, 2 and 3, because at those ratios the maternal and fetal spectra overlap closely; nevertheless fetal SIR improvement remains over 20 dB even at those minima; the authors note ratios 1 and 2 are physiologically unlikely since the real ratio is usually between 1 and 2. REAL DATA: on DaISy channel 1, par-EKS recovers fetal beats in the t = 6-7 s window where seq-EKS lets the fECG be destroyed during mECG extraction, and the par-EKS estimate has cosine 0.92 with the multichannel piCA subspace; par-EKS also...

**G. Limitations.** 1) The method presupposes fetal R-peak detection; the authors themselves call it "the crucial part of the proposed algorithm", which makes this paper unusable as a detector and makes it a consumer of, not a solution to, our problem. 2) The bootstrap is circular: fetal R-peaks come from a rough seq-EKF fECG estimate, and seq-EKF is the very scheme the paper shows to be unreliable under wave overlap. 3) In the twin experiment the R-peak oracle came from several sensors + FastICA, contradicting the single-channel framing for that dataset; the authors suggest replacing it with an extra acoustic sensor or echocardiography, i.e. more hardware. 4) No detection metrics and no annotated ground truth on real data; real-data validation is essentially visual inspection plus one cosine number. 5) Statistical weakness on real data: one DaISy record, one subject for all 55 NI-FECG recordings, one MCG dataset - no inter-subject variability is probed. 6) Performance degrades markedly at integer fetal/maternal heart-rate ratios (1, 2, 3). 7) The 5-Gaussian model cannot represent large morphological variation such as arrhythmia; the authors concede this and only offer residual inspection as a flag. 8) EKS is non-causal and needs a processing lag, so only the EKF variant is genuinely real-time. 9) The synthetic noise is white Gaussian only - no EMG, motion, baseline wander or electrode artefact, which are the dominant real-world nuisances. 10) The method is limited to a small number of...

**H. Relevance.** Three concrete things our single-channel fECG + TDA project can take. (A) CONCEPTUAL SUPPORT FOR THE TOPOLOGY: the paper's core state variable is a cardiac PHASE theta that advances linearly and wraps at 2*pi - a circular coordinate on the cardiac cycle. That is precisely the object persistent H1 on a Takens embedding is built to detect: one heartbeat = one loop. A maternal + fetal mixture is, in this paper's own model, the superposition of TWO independent circular processes at different frequencies, which in a delay embedding is a 2-torus-like structure whose two independent 1-cycles carry different persistence. Our TDA pipeline is therefore attacking the same latent geometry that this well-validated model exploits explicitly, and we can say so with a citation instead of hand-waving. (B) THE OBJECTIONS WE MUST PRE-EMPT: the introduction attacks phase-space/PCA-smoothing single-channel methods on exactly three grounds - the required time-lags are chosen empirically, the smoothing wipes out important inter-beat variation, and the correct embedding dimension changes as the noise statistics change. Every one of these lands on a naive Takens+PH pipeline. Our paper must therefore (i) justify the delay tau and dimension m by a stated criterion (mutual information / false nearest neighbours / persistence stability across tau, m) rather than picking them, (ii) show explicitly that beat-to-beat variability survives our persistence-image encoding, and (iii) report a tau/m sensitivity...

**I. Research opportunity.** The paper leaves a detector-shaped hole in its own pipeline and says so. Specifically: (1) It needs fetal R-peaks but supplies no single-channel detector - the bootstrap is a rough seq-EKF pass and for twins an explicit multi-sensor FastICA oracle; the authors' own proposed fixes are extra hardware (a sound sensor, echocardiography). A genuinely single-channel, annotation-free fetal QRS detector is therefore an immediately citable contribution that plugs into this exact gap. (2) It reports zero detection metrics and never touches an annotated benchmark - so quantifying fetal QRS detection sensitivity/PPV/F1 on annotated single-channel abdominal data (e.g. PhysioNet/CinC 2013, ADFECGDB) is an untouched axis. (3) Its real-data evidence is n=1 subject per database and qualitative; subject-independent, leave-one-subject-out evaluation over many subjects is unaddressed. (4) It fails at integer maternal/fetal heart-rate ratios because it reasons in the frequency domain about overlapping harmonics - a topological method that separates cycles by their geometry in delay space rather than by their spectra could in principle be immune to that specific degeneracy, and demonstrating that at ratios 1:1 and 2:1 would be a sharp, falsifiable claim directly against this paper's Fig. 4. (5) Its 5-Gaussian generative model cannot express arrhythmia or atypical morphology; a learned feature extractor that does not assume P-Q-R-S-T shape is the obvious complement. (6) No noise realism (white...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

- Published as IEEE Trans. Biomed. Eng. 60(5):1345-1352, 2013 (HAL cover page citation, hal-00831928)
- Synthetic data sampling frequency 500 Hz, 20,000 samples per signal = 40 s (Section III-A, article p.3)
- 100 Monte-Carlo trials carried out at each value of each swept parameter (Section III-A, article p.3)
- Default synthetic setting: mECG power normalised to 1 (0 dB) with 5% random fluctuation, fECG/mECG amplitude ratio 0.3, maternal HR 1.1 Hz, fetal HR 2 Hz, 5% per-beat amplitude and duration fluctuation (Section III-A-1, article p.4)
- Input noise power swept from -30 dB to +20 dB (Fig. 2, article p.4)
- At -30 dB noise the MINIMUM fECG SNR improvement is 40 dB (text of Section III-A-1 referring to Fig. 2, article p.4)

- Amplitude ratio swept 0.1 to 1 with input SNR w.r.t. mECG of 10 dB; fetal SIR improvement remains over 30 dB for all ratios (Section III-A-2 text and Fig. 3, article p.4)
- Fetal heart rate swept over 0.3 Hz to 3.6 Hz with maternal HR fixed at 1.1 Hz and 1% heart-rate fluctuation (Section III-A-3 text, article p.4)

#### p17 — Castillo E, Morales DP, Garcia A, Parrilla L, Ruiz VU, Alvarez-Bermejo JA. A clustering-based method for single-channel fetal heart rate monitoring. PLOS ONE 13(6):e0199308, 2018. DOI...

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** có (`p17_Castillo2018-Clustering.pdf`, 5,358,924 byte) · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** Measuring fetal heart rate non-invasively from an abdominal ECG (AECG) recorded by surface electrodes on the mother. The AECG is a composite of the much stronger maternal ECG, the weak fetal ECG, and interference (uterine contraction, fetal/maternal myography, fetal brain activity, motion artefacts). Two specific problems are attacked: (i) methods that first remove or separate the maternal ECG are heavy and degrade the fetal signal, and (ii) methods that detect fetal QRS directly from the denoised AECG rely on AMPLITUDE THRESHOLDS, which are impossible to fix in a general way because fetal RS amplitude depends on gestational week, electrode position and relative heart positions, and can even be comparable to maternal RS amplitude. The paper's aim is a fully automatic, threshold-free fetal QRS detector working on ONE abdominal channel.

**B. Data.** Two PhysioNet databases, both sampled at 1 ksps, both with expert reference fetal R-wave annotations. (1) Abdominal and Direct Fetal Electrocardiogram Database (ADFECGDB) - used for TRAINING / parameter tuning. 5 different women in labour (5 subjects). Each recording = four 5-minute differential abdominal signals PLUS a direct fetal scalp electrocardiogram used as the reference. 1 ksps, 16-bit resolution, signal bandwidth 1-150 Hz. Ground truth = the database's own reference annotations of fetal R-wave locations, derived from the invasive direct fetal scalp ECG (so ground truth is instrument-derived, not hand-drawn on the abdominal trace). A cardiology specialist further excluded r04 Ab-1, r07 Ab-1 and r10 Ab-3 (fetal beats undetectable, severe artefacts, saturation, or no clinical interest), and split the remaining 17 abdominal channel-recordings into Group 1 (11 channel-recordings, no evident artefacts) and Group 2 (6 channel-recordings with big/repetitive artefacts, saturation, or maternal-fetal PQRS indistinguishable). (2) PhysioNet/CinC Challenge 2013 Training Set A - used for TESTING. One-minute fetal ECG recordings at 1 ksps, each with four non-invasive abdominal signals, plus reference annotations marking fetal R-wave locations. A cardiology specialist selected 64 channel-recordings (spanning ~26 distinct records a03...a72, so roughly 26 subjects) for the study. Total abdominal data analysed: 55 minutes from ADFECGDB and 64 minutes from Challenge 2013 Set A = 119...

**C. Method.** A three-stage pipeline. Stage 1 (reused from the authors' earlier work): one-step wavelet-based simultaneous baseline-wander removal and denoising. Stage 2 (the paper's contribution): an unsupervised clustering stage that replaces amplitude thresholds. Every local maximum immediately followed by a local minimum in the denoised AECG is taken as an RS-peak candidate ("max-min point"). Each candidate gets two features: the amplitude distance A between the max and the min, and the number of samples NS between them. Two regimes are recognised: Scenario 1, where maternal RS amplitudes clearly exceed fetal RS amplitudes, so A alone separates three horizontal bands (maternal RS, fetal RS, other/noise); and Scenario 2, where maternal and fetal RS amplitudes are similar so A collapses the two into one band, and the product A x NS must be used instead (maternal RS time-width is reliably larger than fetal RS time-width). The regime is chosen automatically per window from the shape of the smoothed normalised amplitude histogram. The chosen 1-D feature vector is then partitioned by k-medoids++ into exactly three clusters; the fetal cluster is identified as the one whose median value lies between the medians of the other two. A classification-improvement step then prunes fetal-cluster members that violate amplitude and inter-beat time-distance limits derived from the amplitude distribution. Stage 3 (reused): a false-positive / false-negative correction stage that checks several RR time...

**D. Model.** There is no neural network and no supervised model; the "model" is a signal-processing chain plus an unsupervised clustering algorithm, and its full parameterisation is: - Wavelet denoiser: mother wavelet db6; decomposition depth L = 7; approximation coefficients at level 7 replaced by an all-zero vector; detail coefficients at levels i = 1 to M = 3 processed with the universal threshold, single rescaling and SOFT thresholding; details at levels M+1 = 4 through L = 7 left untouched; reconstruction from the zeroed A7, the modified D1-D3 and the original D4-D7. (Consequence, derived by me and not stated in the paper: at fs = 1000 Hz this keeps roughly the 3.9-62.5 Hz band while attenuating high-frequency detail bands.) - Input/output shapes: input = 1 x 50,000 real samples (one abdominal channel, 50 s at 1 kHz). Intermediate = an N x 1 vector of max-min candidate features (N = number of local-max-followed-by-local-min points in the window; on the order of several hundred per 10 s from Figs 2-3). Output = a variable-length list of fetal R-peak sample indices, then an FHR series in bpm. - Feature-selection front-end: MA = maximum of the max-min amplitudes in the window; the interval [0, MA] is divided into 50 equal bins ("found to be an optimum partitioning over a wide range of training sets"); the bin counts are normalised by the total number of max-min points; a smoothing filter (type and length not reported) is applied; local maxima and minima of this smoothed normalised...

**E. Evaluation.** Protocol: parameters were tuned on the Abdominal and Direct Fetal ECG Database (called the training database, because it carries R-wave markers), then the SAME single parameter set was applied unchanged to PhysioNet/CinC Challenge 2013 Training Set A, which is called the testing database. Detections are matched to reference annotations with a fixed +/-50 ms criterion; anything farther is counted as a false positive. Reporting is per channel-recording (Table 1, Table 2) with three summaries each: all analysed recordings, Group 1 only (ADFECGDB), and the best-performing channel per recording. LEAKAGE JUDGEMENT - mixed, and it must be stated at two levels. (a) For the ADFECGDB numbers (Table 1): HIGH optimism / in-sample risk. These are the very recordings on which the 35%-of-MA rule, the 50-bin histogram, the 20 replicates, the 50,000-sample window and the wavelet settings were chosen. There is no held-out split inside ADFECGDB, so Table 1 is a resubstitution result, not a generalisation estimate. Because the method has no trainable weights, this is hyperparameter leakage rather than classic train/test contamination, but it is still leakage and the Table 1 figures should not be quoted as generalisation performance. (b) For the Challenge 2013 Set A numbers (Table 2): LOW leakage risk. This is a genuinely separate database, different subjects, different recording conditions, different segment length, evaluated with frozen parameters. This is a proper cross-dataset test and it...

**F. Results.** ADFECGDB (training database, Table 1, +/-50 ms tolerance): - All analysed channel-recordings: reference FQRS 10,839; TP 10,000; FN 839; FP 413; Se 92.26%, PPV 96.03%, Acc 88.87%, F1 94.11%. - Group 1 (11 clean channel-recordings): 6,990 FQRS; TP 6,815; FN 175 (2.50%); FP 98 (1.40%); Se 97.50%, PPV 98.58%, Acc 96.15%, F1 98.04%. - Best channel per recording (5 recordings): 3,182 FQRS; TP 3,131; FN 51 (1.60%); FP 36 (1.13%); Se 98.40%, PPV 98.86%, Acc 97.30%, F1 98.63%. - Best single channel-recording: r08 Ab-4, Se 99.08%, PPV 99.08%, Acc 98.17%, F1 99.08%; r01 Ab-4, Se 99.07%, PPV 99.69%, Acc 98.76%, F1 99.38%. - Worst (Group 2, artefacts/saturation): r08 Ab-2, Se 71.23%, PPV 83.57%, Acc 62.48%, F1 76.91%; r01 Ab-2, Se 74.34%, PPV 86.13%, Acc 66.39%, F1 79.80%; r08 Ab-3, Se 78.77%, PPV 92.59%, Acc 74.10%, F1 85.12%. PhysioNet/CinC Challenge 2013 Training Set A (testing database, Table 2): - All 64 selected channel-recordings: 9,103 FQRS; TP 8,876; FN 227; FP 123; Se 97.51%, PPV 98.63%, Acc 96.21%, F1 98.07%. - Best channel per recording (26 channel-recordings): 3,627 FQRS; TP 3,552; FN 75; FP 32; Se 97.93%, PPV 99.11%, Acc 97.08%, F1 98.52%. - Many perfect channel-recordings (Se = PPV = Acc = F1 = 100%): a03 Ab-1, a05 Ab-4, a08 Ab-3, a13 Ab-2, a20 Ab-2, a22 Ab-1, a22 Ab-4, a24 Ab-3, a24 Ab-4, a25 Ab-2, a36 Ab-3, a44 Ab-1, a49 Ab-2, a49 Ab-3, a72 Ab-1, a72 Ab-2. - Worst test channel-recordings: a67 Ab-4, Se 88.24%, Acc 84.38%, F1 91.53%; a55 Ab-3, Se 90.14%, Acc 86.49%, F1...

**G. Limitations.** 1. Ground-truth recordings were hand-selected by a cardiologist: r04 Ab-1, r07 Ab-1 and r10 Ab-3 were dropped from ADFECGDB entirely, and 64 of the available Challenge-2013-A channel-recordings were chosen. The headline figures are Group-1 or best-channel subsets, not the full data. 2. Best-performing-channel summaries are an oracle: choosing the best of four abdominal channels after evaluation is not available to a real single-channel monitor, yet these are the numbers most often quoted (98.63% / 98.52% F1). 3. Robustness to artefacts is poor: on Group 2 recordings accuracy falls to 62.48-91.79% and sensitivity to 71.23%, and the all-data ADFECGDB accuracy is only 88.87%. 4. Very small data: 5 subjects in the training DB, ~26 in the test DB, 119 minutes total; no confidence intervals, no statistical significance testing, no bootstrap. 5. Long analysis window: 50,000 samples = 50 s of context is needed for the histogram and clustering statistics, which limits latency and prevents evaluation on very short segments; the paper only checks 10,000-60,000 samples. 6. Uses only two scalar descriptors per candidate (peak amplitude and peak width). No morphology, no template matching, no P/T wave information, so overlapping maternal-fetal QRS complexes can only be recovered indirectly by the RR-based FP/FN correction. 7. Several constants remain hand-tuned and unexplained: 35% of MA, 50 histogram bins, the unspecified smoothing filter, 20 clustering replicates. Only the window...

**H. Relevance.** Directly on-target and unusually useful for our project, on four counts. (1) Identical problem and identical benchmarks: single-channel abdominal fECG, ADFECGDB and PhysioNet/CinC Challenge 2013 Training Set A, Se/PPV/Acc/F1 with a +/-50 ms tolerance. Its Table 3 is a ready-made comparison table we can extend with our own row. (2) It is the classical baseline we must beat. Concretely: F1 98.07% on 64 Challenge-2013-A channel-recordings (cross-dataset, frozen parameters) and F1 98.04% on ADFECGDB Group 1. Any TDA+CNN detector that does not clear roughly 98% F1 under the same protocol has not justified its complexity. (3) The deepest connection: the paper's core feature IS a topological invariant, though the authors never say so. A local maximum immediately followed by a local minimum, described by its amplitude difference A(max-min), is exactly a 0-dimensional persistence pair of the 1-D signal under a superlevel-set filtration - A is the persistence (lifetime) of that peak. Their second feature, A x NS, is persistence weighted by the pair's temporal extent, i.e. a point in the (birth-death, time) plane which is precisely what a persistence image summarises. So this 2018 paper is, in effect, an unsupervised hand-crafted 0-D persistent-homology fetal QRS detector reaching ~98% F1. That is strong independent evidence that persistence carries the fetal-vs-maternal discriminative information, and it gives our project a clean narrative: "the best classical single-channel detector...

**I. Research opportunity.** The paper leaves several concrete gaps our project can occupy. 1. No learning at all. Every decision constant (35% of MA, 50 bins, the fetal-cluster-is-the-median-cluster rule, the amplitude/time pruning limits) is hand-set on 5 subjects. A learned detector over persistence features removes the need to guess a regime and a threshold per window - and can be trained end-to-end rather than hand-assembled in three stages. 2. Only 0-dimensional, amplitude-based topology. There is no use of H1 (cycles) or of Takens delay-embedding, so the QRS trajectory geometry - which is exactly what separates a fetal QRS loop from a maternal one and from noise - is untouched. Takens embedding + H0/H1 persistence images is a strict superset of their feature. 3. Artefact robustness is wide open. Group 2 performance (Se 71-91%, Acc 62-92%) is the paper's clear failure regime, and persistence-based features are intrinsically noise-robust (short-lifetime bars are noise by construction), so demonstrating that a TDA detector holds up on exactly r01 Ab-2, r08 Ab-2 and r08 Ab-3 would be a headline result. 4. Maternal-fetal QRS overlap is never handled explicitly, only patched afterwards by RR heuristics. 5. No latency or short-window analysis: they need 50 s of context; a CNN over persistence images of short (2-5 s) windows could offer a genuinely low-latency detector, which matters for real-time monitoring - and no one has measured this. 6. No ablation separating denoiser / clustering / RR-correction...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

- Sampling rate 1 ksps, 16-bit, 1-150 Hz bandwidth for ADFECGDB; 1 ksps for Challenge 2013 Set A - Methods, 'Fetal ECG datasets' section, p.4
- ADFECGDB = 5 different women in labour, four 5-minute abdominal differential signals + direct fetal scalp ECG reference per recording - Methods, p.4
- Wavelet preprocessing: db6, M = 3, universal threshold, single rescaling, soft thresholding, L = 7 - Results and discussions, p.13 (and Methods p.3)
- Histogram partitioning: range 0 to MA divided into 50 intervals - Methods, Step 2 'Signal feature selection', p.7
- Scenario switch threshold: dmax-max greater than 35% of MA -> Scenario 1 (amplitude feature); less than 35% -> Scenario 2 (amplitude x number of samples) - Methods, Step 2, Cases 1 and 2, pp.7-8
- Clustering: k = 3 clusters, squared Euclidean distance, 20 repeats with new initial medoid positions, 'plus' (++) initial medoid method, MATLAB kmedoids - Methods, Step 3 'Clustering classification', p.10
- Clustering window: 50,000 samples selected; range 10,000-60,000 samples showed no noticeable difference - Methods, Step 4, p.10
- Detection tolerance: candidates differing by more than 50 ms from the reference annotation are marked as FP - Results and discussions, p.13
- ADFECGDB all analysed recordings: T FQRS 10,839, TP 10,000, FN 839, FP 413, Se 92.26%, PPV 96.03%, Acc 88.87%, F1 94.11% - Table 1, 'Total' row, p.14
- ADFECGDB Group 1: 6,990 FQRS, TP 6,815, FN 175 (2.50%), FP 98 (1.40%), Se 97.50%, PPV 98.58%, Acc 96.15%, F1 98.04% - Table 1 'Group 1' row and text p.13
- ADFECGDB best channel per recording: 3,182 FQRS, TP 3,131, FN 51 (1.60%), FP 36 (1.13%), Se 98.40%, PPV 98.86%, Acc 97.30%, F1 98.63% - Table 1 'Best channel' row and text p.13
- Challenge 2013 Training Set A, all 64 selected recordings: 9,103 FQRS, TP 8,876, FN 227, FP 123, Se 97.51%, PPV 98.63%, Acc 96.21%, F1 98.07% - Table 2 'Total' row, p.16
