#### p18 — Le PKT, Vo VT, Tran LG. Increasing temporal accuracy of noninvasive fetal electrocardiogram QRS detection with modified superimposition template subtraction. Physiol Meas 46(7):075005, 2025. DOI...

**Mức truy cập:** `abstract_only` · **Tải về:** không · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** low

**A. Problem.** Non-invasive fetal ECG recorded from the maternal abdomen is dominated by the maternal ECG, which overlaps the fetal signal in both time and frequency, so fetal QRS complexes are hard to localise. The specific problem this paper attacks is not just DETECTING the fetal QRS but detecting it with high TEMPORAL accuracy - i.e. placing the fetal R-peak within a few milliseconds of truth, rather than within the conventional 50 ms tolerance used by the PhysioNet/CinC 2013 challenge. The clinical motivation given in the abstract is that accurate non-invasive fetal monitoring would enable early detection of fetal distress. Sub-50 ms timing matters because beat-to-beat fetal heart-rate variability is computed from R-R intervals, and a +/-50 ms jitter swamps the variability signal itself. (Source: abstract; full text not accessible.)

**B. Data.** Datasets named in the abstract: (1) recordings from the PhysioNet/Computing in Cardiology Challenge 2013, and (2) the Abdominal and Direct Fetal ECG Database (ADFECGDB). NOT ACCESSIBLE from the abstract: which subset of the 2013 challenge (set-a / set-b / set-c), how many records were used from each database, the number of distinct subjects, the sampling rate, the number of abdominal channels used, the recording length, and how ground truth was produced or verified. External background (NOT from this paper, provided only so the team knows what these databases normally contain and must be verified independently before citing): the CinC 2013 challenge set-a is commonly cited as 75 one-minute, 4-channel abdominal recordings at 1 kHz with expert-annotated fetal R-peaks; ADFECGDB is commonly cited as 5 five-minute recordings with 4 abdominal channels plus a simultaneously recorded direct fetal scalp electrode at 1 kHz, the scalp channel supplying the fetal R-peak ground truth. The ADFECGDB scalp reference is the reason a 10 ms tolerance is even meaningful - it gives a hardware-grade timing reference rather than an annotator's estimate. Whether this paper used exactly those configurations is unverified.

**C. Method.** Four method pipelines were constructed, each pairing a version of superimposition template subtraction (STS) with a version of independent component analysis (ICA). The stated design logic is complementarity: template subtraction removes the maternal complex well when the maternal beat morphology is stable but leaves residuals under morphology drift and when maternal and fetal complexes coincide, whereas ICA separates overlapping sources well when enough independent channels exist but can fail on low-SNR or rank-deficient recordings; combining them is intended to let each cover the other's failure mode. The STS component is described as 'modified', which is the paper's claimed novelty and the source of the improved temporal accuracy, but WHAT the modification is - how the superimposed template is built, aligned, scaled, or subtracted - is NOT ACCESSIBLE. The pipelines are said to 'adapt to various signal characteristics', implying some signal-quality-driven selection or ordering among the four, but the selection criterion is NOT ACCESSIBLE. This is a deterministic signal-processing method with no learned parameters.

**D. Model.** none - the paper presents no learned model. There is no neural architecture, no input/output tensor shapes, no training procedure, no optimiser, no epochs, no batch size, and no hyperparameter search, because the method is a classical DSP pipeline (template subtraction + ICA) whose parameters are set analytically or by hand rather than fitted. The algorithmic parameters that WOULD be needed to reimplement it (template window length, number of beats averaged into the template, alignment method, ICA variant such as FastICA vs JADE, number of components retained, component-selection rule, and the fetal-QRS peak-picking rule and refractory period) are all NOT ACCESSIBLE because the full text is paywalled.

**E. Evaluation.** Split and validation protocol NOT ACCESSIBLE in detail. However, LEAKAGE RISK IS INHERENTLY LOW, and this can be reasoned about without the full text: the method contains no trained parameters, so there is no training set to leak from a test set. The only leakage-like concerns that could apply are (i) hyperparameter/pipeline-variant tuning performed on the same records that are then reported - the abstract's mention of pipelines 'designed to adapt to various signal characteristics' and of a 'best performing' pipeline chosen over the whole dataset means the reported 95.2% may be a best-of-four selected post hoc, which is a mild optimistic bias, and (ii) the absence of any held-out database. Evaluation appears to be per-record over the full set of recordings from two public databases (a cross-database evaluation in the weak sense that two independent databases are used), scored as F1 against reference fetal R-peaks with a 10 ms matching window. Whether results were reported per-record, per-database, or pooled, and whether any statistical test or confidence interval was given, is unclear. Overall judgement: low leakage risk from the modelling side; UNCLEAR and mildly optimistic on the pipeline-selection side.

**F. Results.** The single verifiable result is from the abstract: over the whole dataset, the best-performing of the four pipelines achieved an average F1 score of 95.2% for fetal heart rate detection using a 10 ms error window. Everything else - the F1 of the other three pipelines, per-database breakdowns (PhysioNet 2013 vs ADFECGDB separately), sensitivity and positive predictive value, results at other tolerance windows such as the conventional 50 ms, RR-interval or FHR error in bpm, comparison against published baselines, worst-case records, and any figures or tables - is NOT ACCESSIBLE.

**G. Limitations.** The authors' own stated limitations are NOT ACCESSIBLE (no Discussion section available). Limitations that can be established from the abstract and metadata alone: (1) The ICA stage mathematically requires multiple simultaneous abdominal channels, so the method is not applicable to single-channel abdominal ECG - the exact setting of our project. (2) Four competing pipelines with a 'best performing' one reported suggests no single configuration works across all signal types, and the abstract gives no automatic, validated rule for choosing among them at inference time on unseen data. (3) Only two public databases are used, both small (tens of records, one-to-five-minute segments), so generalisation to long-duration clinical monitoring, to different gestational ages, and to different electrode montages is untested. (4) The abstract reports only F1; no timing-error distribution (e.g. mean absolute R-peak error in ms) is quoted, which is the natural metric for a paper whose stated goal is temporal accuracy. (5) No deep-learning comparator is mentioned. (6) The article is closed access with no repository copy, which itself limits reproducibility - no code or data availability statement is visible.

**H. Relevance.** Three things transfer to a single-channel fECG + TDA project. FIRST AND MOST IMPORTANT - the evaluation protocol. This paper establishes 10 ms as a defensible, publishable tolerance window and sets the number to beat at that tolerance (95.2% F1, multi-channel classical). Our project should report F1 at 10 ms AND at 50 ms; reporting only 50 ms will look dated next to a 2025 paper, and reporting 10 ms gives a direct, citable point of comparison. It also motivates reporting the R-peak timing error distribution in ms, not only F1. SECOND - the pre-processing front end. Superimposition template subtraction is the right thing to run before a topological stage: a Takens delay embedding of a raw abdominal segment will have its point cloud geometry, and therefore its persistence diagram, completely dominated by the large maternal QRS loop, so the small fetal loop will not survive into the persistence image. Cancelling the maternal complex first (maternal R-peak detection, beat-aligned template, subtraction) is what makes the fetal cycle the dominant recurrent structure in the embedding. THIRD - positioning. Because this method needs ICA and therefore >= 2 channels, it is the clean foil for a single-channel claim: 'state-of-the-art temporal accuracy currently requires multi-channel blind source separation; we ask whether a topological representation can recover comparable timing from one channel.' That is a well-defined, honest contribution statement. What does NOT transfer: any...

**I. Research opportunity.** Several gaps remain open, and they line up unusually well with the project. (1) SINGLE-CHANNEL TEMPORAL ACCURACY IS UNSOLVED HERE. This paper buys its 10 ms accuracy with multi-channel spatial diversity (ICA). Nobody in this line has shown comparable timing from one abdominal lead. A single-channel method that reaches even 85-90% F1 at 10 ms would be a real result, and TDA is a plausible route because a delay embedding manufactures a multi-dimensional representation from one channel - it is, in effect, a temporal substitute for the spatial diversity ICA needs. That framing ('Takens embedding provides the extra dimensions that ICA gets from extra electrodes') is a genuinely strong motivation sentence for our paper. (2) NO LEARNED COMPONENT. The pipeline is entirely hand-designed and requires choosing among four variants; a learned detector needs no such selection and can adapt to signal quality implicitly. Showing that one trained model matches four hand-tuned pipelines is a clean claim. (3) NO TIMING-ERROR DISTRIBUTION IS REPORTED (only F1 at a tolerance). Reporting a histogram or mean-absolute-error in ms of fetal R-peak localisation is an easy way for our paper to be strictly more informative than this one. (4) NO ROBUSTNESS ANALYSIS visible - no SNR sweep, no noise stress test, no performance-vs-signal-quality curve. A TDA-based detector is a natural candidate for such an analysis because persistent homology is designed to be stable under perturbation, and demonstrating...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

- 95.2% - average F1 score for fetal heart rate detection by the best-performing method pipeline, over the whole dataset. Source: abstract (verbatim: 'achieved an average F1 score of 95.2% for fetal heart rate detection using a small error window of only 10 ms'), retrieved from IOPscience article page, Europe PMC...
- 10 ms - the error/tolerance window used to match detected fetal R-peaks to the reference when computing that F1. Source: abstract, same sentence. NOTE for the team: the PhysioNet/CinC 2013 challenge convention is 50 ms; this paper's 10 ms is 5x stricter, which is the whole point of the paper.
- 4 - number of method pipelines developed, each combining a version of STS with a version of ICA. Source: abstract ('Four method pipelines were developed'). The per-pipeline scores are not accessible; 95.2% is the best of these four.
- 2 - number of databases used: PhysioNet/CinC Challenge 2013 recordings and the Abdominal and Direct Fetal ECG Database (ADFECGDB). Source: abstract. Record counts, subject counts, sampling rate and channel counts are NOT stated in any accessible text.
- 46(7):075005, 2025 - volume, issue and article number in Physiological Measurement. Source: Crossref API (volume 46, issue 7, page 075005) and Europe PMC journalInfo.
- Received 4 March 2025 / Revised 17 June 2025 / Accepted 30 June 2025 / Published 14 July 2025. Source: IOPscience article-information block (retrieved via text-extraction proxy).
- PMID 40587996; DOI 10.1088/1361-6579/adea2b; ISSN 0967-3334 / 1361-6579. Source: Europe PMC and Crossref.
- 127 total downloads as reported on the IOPscience article page at time of retrieval (2026-09-08); citedByCount = 0 per Semantic Scholar/Europe PMC. Source: IOPscience article-metrics block and Europe PMC field 'citedByCount'. Context only - indicates the paper is very new and not yet independently validated.
- NO NUMBER AVAILABLE for: per-database F1, sensitivity, PPV, F1 at 50 ms, R-peak timing error in ms, number of records, sampling rate, channel count, or any baseline comparison. These are behind the paywall and must not be guessed.

#### p19 — Sulas E, Urru M, Tumbarello R, Raffo L, Pani D. Systematic analysis of single- and multi-reference adaptive filters for non-invasive fetal electrocardiography. DOI: 10.3934/mbe.2020016

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** có (`p19_Sulas2020-AdaptiveFilters.pdf`, 1,562,015 byte) · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** How to cancel the dominant maternal ECG (mECG) interference from non-invasive abdominal recordings so that fetal QRS complexes (and hence fetal heart rate) can be detected, under the practical constraint of a *small* number of electrodes (wearable systems). The specific question the paper poses: is a SINGLE-reference (one thoracic lead) adaptive filter ever sufficient — in particular when the abdominal lead is chosen to be spatially parallel to the thoracic reference lead — or must one pay for MULTIPLE non-coplanar thoracic references? The authors frame this as a lead-placement / lead-orientation dependency problem: an adaptive noise canceller can only subtract the maternal component if the reference lead is morphologically similar to the maternal projection contaminating the abdominal lead, and a single projection direction cannot reconstruct the maternal heart vector in an arbitrary abdominal direction. They evaluate on SIR, mECG attenuation, and downstream fetal-QRS detection accuracy.

**B. Data.** REAL DATASET (custom, NInFEA project, University of Cagliari): 28 multichannel signals from 20 healthy pregnant women, gestational age 21st-27th week, healthy fetuses. Each recording fixed at 10 s duration (chosen "similar to the standard resting ECG"). 4 abdominal channels per recording -> 112 abdominal channels / 112 non-invasive fECG signals total. Acquisition: TMSi Porti7, 2048 Hz, 22-bit; NO analog high-pass (DC-coupled), bandwidth limited by a digital decimation filter with cut-off approx. 550 Hz. Electrode montage: 6 chest electrodes forming 3 NON-COPLANAR bipolar thoracic (maternal) leads (1m, 2m, 3m); 3 abdominal electrodes giving 3 digital bipolar abdominal leads (horizontal, vertical, oblique, 45 deg spacing) plus 1 unipolar abdominal channel (unipolar = referenced to the average of all unipolar electrodes in use); ground on right hip. Recorded at Division of Pediatric Cardiology, S. Michele Hospital, Cagliari; ethics approval by Independent Ethical Committee of AOU Cagliari; informed consent obtained. GROUND TRUTH (real data): simultaneous pulsed-wave Doppler (PWD) ultrasound trace acquired with a Philips iE33 machine, five-chamber apical window (mitral + aortic flows). Fetal QRS complexes were annotated MANUALLY "with the help of the simultaneous PWD signal" (Fig. 3 shows the PWD trace, its upper/lower envelopes, and the fECG lead with marked fetal QRS). Maternal QRS were detected automatically with Pan-Tompkins on the horizontal chest lead. Note: this is...

**C. Method.** Classical adaptive-noise-cancellation, no learning-based model. The abdominal lead d(k) = s(k) [fECG] + n(k) [mECG interference]; a thoracic reference x(k) correlated with n(k) but not s(k) drives an adaptive filter whose output y(k) = w^T x(k) estimates n(k); the error e(k) = d(k) - y(k) is taken as the extracted fECG. Filter: QR-decomposition recursive least squares (QRD-RLS) with back-substitution, chosen over plain RLS because direct matrix inversion in RLS is numerically ill-conditioned (convergence rate/misalignment/stability depend on the condition number of the input covariance matrix). QR decomposition triangularises the input data matrix X(k) [(k+1)x(N+1)] via an orthogonal Q(k) into an upper-triangular U(k) [(N+1)x(N+1)], implementable via Givens rotations or Householder transformation. Cost function is the exponentially weighted least squares with forgetting factor lambda. TWO CONFIGURATIONS COMPARED: - SR (single-reference): one thoracic lead as reference. They deliberately chose the FIRST thoracic lead (1m real / 5-6 synthetic), which is perpendicular to the sagittal plane and therefore PARALLEL to the horizontal abdominal lead — the most favourable case for SR. - MR (multi-reference): M = 3 non-coplanar thoracic leads (1m, 2m, 3m real; 5-6, 5-7, 7-8 synthetic), each with its own tap-delay line; "in a sample, we shift from each channel one at a time and progress recursively from the first to last channel" (i.e. the reference regressor is the concatenation of...

**D. Model.** NO neural network / no trained model. The "model" is a linear adaptive FIR filter bank. - Filter type: QRD-RLS adaptive noise canceller (transversal FIR, coefficients w adapted per sample). - Number of taps N = 20 per reference channel (SR: 20 taps total; MR: 20 taps for EACH of the 3 thoracic channels, i.e. 60 adaptive coefficients). - Forgetting factor lambda = 0.999. - Both lambda and N were "optimized in the training phase on the real dataset"; the authors note other studies (ref [20], Behar et al.) arrived at the same values by similar procedures. NO grid/ablation table for these values is shown. - Input: abdominal lead d(k) (primary input) at 2048 Hz + 1 or 3 thoracic reference leads x(k) at 2048 Hz. Output: e(k), the extracted single-channel fECG at 2048 Hz, same length (10 s = 20480 samples). - Pre-processing (applied because there is no analog HP filter and baseline wander from maternal breathing / fetal and cable movement differs per lead): bidirectional (zero-phase, forward-backward) equiripple FIR high-pass of order 1124, cut-off 1 Hz. Cut-off deliberately set ABOVE the 0.67 Hz clinical guideline (ref [32]) to fight the stronger baseline wander, but kept at 1 Hz (not higher) to preserve P, T and ST low-frequency content, following ref [20]. - No decimation/resampling reported; everything stays at 2048 Hz. - No amplitude normalisation, no loss function, no epochs, no class-imbalance handling, no post-processing/denoising stage (the authors explicitly state a...

**E. Evaluation.** NO train/test split is described. The paper states twice that a "training phase" existed — (i) lambda and N were "optimized in the training phase on the real dataset", and (ii) jqrs was selected over other fetal QRS detectors because it "performed better in the training phase" — but it never says which records formed that training set, whether they were excluded from the reported results, or how large it was. All reported numbers (Table 1, Figs. 8-10) are over the full 112 real channels and 400 synthetic channels. LEAKAGE RISK: HIGH-to-MODERATE, and definitely NOT subject-independent. Reasoning: (1) hyperparameters (lambda, N) and the choice of the downstream QRS detector were tuned on the same real dataset from which the reported accuracies come, with no stated held-out partition -> selection/optimistic bias on the real-data accuracy figures; (2) evaluation is not leave-one-subject-out, not record-wise held out, and not cross-dataset — real results are reported on the tuning data itself; (3) statistical non-independence: the Wilcoxon signed-rank tests treat the 112 real channels as samples, but they come from only 28 recordings and only 20 women (4 abdominal channels per recording, and some women contributed more than one recording), so the effective n is much smaller than 112 and p-values are optimistic. (4) Mitigating factors: the filter itself is fully adaptive/unsupervised per record — nothing is learned across records — so the leakage is confined to two scalar...

**F. Results.** Headline: the multi-reference filter beats the single-reference filter on EVERY metric on real data, and single-reference is declared unusable even for the modest goal of fetal heart rate. REAL DATA, fetal-QRS detection accuracy, median (Q1, Q3) [Table 1]: - horizontal lead (the one PARALLEL to the SR reference): OR raw 0.62 (0.50, 0.84); SR 0.85 (0.77, 0.94) is the MR value — correction: MR hor 0.85 (0.77, 0.94). (Table 1 prints the raw-hor accuracy 0.62 and MR hor 0.85; the SR-hor accuracy cell is not separately legible in the extracted table, but the SR-vs-MR difference is significant at p = 0.0043.) - vertical: SR 0.69 (0.52, 0.88) -> MR 0.92 (0.82, 0.96), p = 0.0009 - oblique: SR 0.61 (0.52, 0.84) -> MR 0.89 (0.77, 0.92), p = 0.0000 - unipolar: SR 0.70 (0.51, 0.90) -> MR 0.86 (0.79, 0.91), p = 0.0001 - pooled SR vs MR: p = 0.0000; conclusion text: SR accuracy is "poor on real recordings (68% on average, sigma = 0.0097)". REAL DATA, SIR [dB] median (Q1, Q3): hor OR -8.5 (-12.09, -4.97) -> SR -0.87 (-7.39, 2.23) -> MR +1.79 (-1.20, 5.17); ver OR -9.92 -> SR -4.85 -> MR -0.06; obl OR -13.05 -> SR -5.48 -> MR +2.03; unip OR -11.4 -> SR -6.72 -> MR +2.63. SR vs MR significant for every lead (p = 0.0009 / 0.0003 / 0.0008 / 0.0006; pooled p = 0.0000). REAL DATA, mECG attenuation [dB] median (Q1, Q3): hor SR 15.02 (9.11, 21.50) vs MR 25.75 (19.80, 33.41); ver SR 12.08 vs MR 33.79; obl SR 13.99 vs MR 35.09; unip SR 12.16 vs MR 31.50. All p = 0.0000/0.0001. SYNTHETIC DATA: SIR...

**G. Limitations.** 1. NOT a detector paper — no fetal-QRS detection algorithm is proposed; jqrs is used off-the-shelf purely as a downstream quality probe, and its parameters/tolerance window are never given. 2. Requires thoracic reference electrodes; the "single-channel" scenario of interest to our project (abdominal lead ONLY, no maternal reference) is exactly the case this method cannot handle at all. 3. Very short records: 10 s per recording; only 28 recordings from 20 women; narrow gestational window 21-27 weeks (deliberately chosen to minimise vernix caseosa effects), so no term/near-term or intrapartum data. 4. No train/test separation; lambda, N, and the detector choice were tuned on the same real dataset that produced the reported numbers. Not subject-independent, no LOSO, no cross-dataset validation. 5. Custom private dataset, not a public benchmark at the time of publication -> results are not directly comparable to PhysioNet-based literature. 6. Accuracy metric includes an ill-defined TN term for a peak-detection task, and no Se / PPV / F1 / detection tolerance is reported; statistical tests treat 112 correlated channels (28 recordings, 20 subjects) as independent samples. 7. No post-processing / denoising stage: the authors explicitly report that even the best MR output "seems to suffer from residual noise, beyond that of power-line interference" and recommend an ad-hoc denoising stage — which they did not implement or evaluate. 8. Only one adaptive-filter family (QRD-RLS) is...

**H. Relevance.** Directly relevant as a HARD BASELINE AND A WARNING, not as an architecture to copy. (a) It quantifies the ceiling of the classical route on real data: even the BEST configuration (3 non-coplanar thoracic references + QRD-RLS) reaches only 0.85-0.92 median fetal-QRS accuracy on clean-ish 21-27-week recordings, and the post-extraction SIR is barely positive (-0.06 to +2.63 dB median). If our TDA+CNN pipeline works directly on a single abdominal channel with no maternal reference and matches or beats ~0.85-0.90, that is a meaningful result; if it lands near 0.68 it is merely at the level of a discredited single-reference filter. (b) It gives us the exact preprocessing recipe used by a careful group on this signal class: 1 Hz zero-phase (bidirectional) high-pass, order-1124 equiripple FIR, deliberately above the 0.67 Hz guideline to kill baseline wander but low enough to preserve P/T/ST. We can adopt this verbatim as our front-end. Note their sampling rate is 2048 Hz with anti-alias cutoff ~550 Hz. (c) It supplies the montage rationale: bipolar/differential abdominal leads gave significantly larger fetal QRS amplitude than a unipolar channel on REAL data (33-38 uV vs 20 uV, p = 0.004/0.001). If our project can choose the abdominal derivation, prefer a differential one — but note this is exactly the effect that FECGSYN gets backwards. (d) The strongest transferable message for our validation design: DO NOT VALIDATE ON SYNTHETIC fECG ALONE. Here the synthetic set showed no...

**I. Research opportunity.** The paper closes the door it opens: it proves that with ONE reference channel a linear adaptive filter cannot produce a signal on which fetal QRS detection is reliable (68% accuracy on real data), and it explicitly declines to build the post-processing/denoising stage it says is needed. That leaves three concrete gaps our project can occupy. 1. THE TRUE SINGLE-CHANNEL GAP. Their "single-reference" still needs 1 abdominal + 1 thoracic electrode pair. Nobody here attempts fetal-QRS detection from a single abdominal channel with NO maternal reference at all. A nonlinear, morphology-agnostic detector — e.g. Takens embedding + persistent homology + persistence images + CNN — is precisely the class of method that could sidestep the reference-lead requirement, because it does not need to reconstruct the maternal heart vector; it needs only to distinguish the topological signature of a fetal beat cycle from a maternal one in the delay-embedded trajectory. Direct head-to-head against their Table 1 real-data medians (0.68 SR / 0.85-0.92 MR) is an immediately publishable comparison. 2. THE MISSING DETECTION STAGE. They ran an off-the-shelf jqrs energy detector on the residual and stopped. A learned detector operating on the SAME residual e(k) from their MR QRD-RLS filter would very likely lift accuracy above 0.92, and the ablation "MR-RLS + jqrs vs MR-RLS + TDA-CNN vs raw abdominal + TDA-CNN" isolates exactly how much of the gain comes from extraction and how much from detection — a...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

- Real dataset: 28 multichannel signals from 20 healthy pregnant women, 21st-27th week gestation, 112 abdominal channels total, 10 s each (Section 2.1.1)
- Acquisition: 2048 Hz, 22-bit, TMSi Porti7, no analog high-pass, decimation-filter cut-off approx. 550 Hz (Section 2.1.1)
- Synthetic dataset: FECGSYN, 2048 Hz, 10 s, SNRfm = -18 dB, MNR in {3, 5, 9, 12, 15} dB, 20 fetal heart positions per level -> 100 virtual subjects x 4 abdominal channels = 400 synthetic signals + 3 thoracic leads each (Section 2.1.2)
- Synthetic maternal HR fixed at 90 bpm, fetal HR fixed at 150 bpm (Section 2.1.2)
- QRD-RLS forgetting factor lambda = 0.999, number of taps N = 20 per reference channel (MR uses 20 taps for each of 3 channels) (Section 2.2.2)
- Pre-processing: bidirectional equiripple FIR high-pass, order 1124, cut-off 1 Hz (guideline value quoted as 0.67 Hz) (Section 2.2.3)
- Fetal QRS template window 40 ms; maternal QRS template window 100 ms; Pearson cross-correlation threshold 0.6; minimum 4 similar complexes else amplitude replaced by 4x std (Section 2.3)
- Real data fetal-QRS accuracy, median (Q1,Q3): SR ver 0.69 (0.52,0.88) vs MR ver 0.92 (0.82,0.96) (Table 1)
- Real data fetal-QRS accuracy: SR obl 0.61 (0.52,0.84) vs MR obl 0.89 (0.77,0.92) (Table 1)
- Real data fetal-QRS accuracy: SR unip 0.70 (0.51,0.90) vs MR unip 0.86 (0.79,0.91) (Table 1)
- Real data fetal-QRS accuracy: raw OR hor 0.62 (0.50,0.84) vs MR hor 0.85 (0.77,0.94) (Table 1)
- SR fetal-QRS detection accuracy on real recordings averaged 68%, sigma = 0.0097 (Conclusion, Section 4)

#### p20 — Clifford GD, Silva I, Behar J, Moody GB. Non-invasive fetal ECG analysis. DOI 10.1088/0967-3334/35/8/1521. Editorial / overview of the PhysioNet-Computing in Cardiology Challenge 2013 and its...

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** có (`p20_Clifford2014-ChallengeOverview.html`, 188,522 byte) · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** Two linked problems. (1) Scientific: non-invasive fetal ECG (NI-FECG) from maternal abdominal electrodes is the only cheap, safe, continuous, morphology-capable fetal monitoring modality, but the fetal signal is buried under a much larger maternal ECG plus noise, and MECG/FECG overlap in BOTH time and frequency (their Fig. 2 shows spectral overlap in the QRS band and temporally coincident MQRS/FQRS), so fetal QRS (FQRS) detection is hard. (2) Meta-scientific, and the actual point of the paper: before 2013 there was NO gold-standard public database with labelled FECG QRS complexes, so published NI-FECG work relied on proprietary data or "a very limited set of data recorded from few (or more often, just one) individuals", making methods mutually incomparable. The paper is the editorial/overview for the PhysioNet/Computing in Cardiology Challenge 2013 and its Physiological Measurement special issue; it defines the benchmark, the scoring protocol, and reviews the winning algorithm families. It is a benchmark-definition + review paper, not a new-method paper.

**B. Data.** CHALLENGE 2013 CORPUS (their Table 2): 447 records total, pooled from five sources - ADFECGDB (Matonia et al. 2006) 25 records; Simulated FECGs (Behar et al. 2014b, extended Sameni 2007 dipole model) 20; NIFECGDB (PhysioNet) 14; "Non-Invasive FECG" (donated, previously unpublished) 340; Scalp FECG Database (never made public, used only to score open-source entries on Set C) 48. UNIFORM FORMAT: "All records were formatted to have a 1 kHz sampling frequency, one minute duration, and four channels of non-invasive abdominal maternal ECG leads." So: fs = 1000 Hz, 60 s/record, 4 abdominal channels. Total signal = 447 min ~= 7.45 h. SPLITS AS RELEASED: Set A = 75 records, signals AND expert annotations public (training). Set B = 100 records, signals public, annotations withheld. Set C = 272 records, signals AND annotations both withheld (run on a private PhysioNet server). One record in Set A and two in Set B were unscoreable due to limited reference annotations. NUMBER OF SUBJECTS: not reported (the paper counts records, not mothers/fetuses; no subject IDs or subject counts are given anywhere, which is itself a stated weakness of the resource). GROUND-TRUTH CONSTRUCTION (two-stage, explicitly described): Stage 1 - reference annotations came from the source datasets; "for the non-invasive data sets, the annotations were obtained from FECG QRS estimates derived manually or through additional maternal ECG leads that were not available to competitors." ADFECGDB and the Scalp FECG...

**C. Method.** No new algorithm is proposed. The paper contributes (a) the benchmark and scoring protocol, and (b) a taxonomy plus detailed narrative reconstruction of the top entrants' pipelines. CANONICAL 5-STEP PIPELINE the organisers distilled from all entries: 1. Pre-processing (baseline-wander/trend removal, powerline notch, artifact rejection; often channel augmentation by subtracting pairs of channels or inverting channels to create differential pseudo-leads). 2. Estimation of the maternal component (subspace decomposition - ICA or SVD were "the two most common" - or template generation by averaging detected MQRS across channels and/or time, often SQI-weighted; Pan-Tompkins was the popular MQRS detector; sometimes the template is further reduced by curve fitting). 3. Removal of the maternal component (subspace reconstruction with non-fetal components zeroed; static or adaptive maternal template subtraction; adaptive filters - Kalman, LMS; asynchronous temporal windowing/gating). 4. FQRS estimation on the residual (RS-slope + R-amplitude thresholding, or modified adult QRS detectors; often merging annotations across channels/detectors by median voting). 5. Post-processing (constrain the FHR/RR series by physiological or statistical heuristics) - applied by only some competitors. STATED ASSUMPTIONS AND WHERE THEY BREAK (important for a single-channel project): subspace methods implicitly assume (a) sources are fixed, discrete and <= number of channels, (b) the subspace...

**D. Model.** No model is trained by this paper; there is no architecture of its own. The models it DESCRIBES, at the level of detail given: Behar et al. (2013) - best overall. Pre-processing: high-pass + notch filtering; the authors note a HIGH high-pass cut-off, "such as 10 Hz", improved detection, attributed to removal of the large maternal P and T wave components. MQRS: modified Pan-Tompkins. MECG removal: fusion of several source-separation techniques - PCA, template subtraction, and ICA. FQRS: modified Pan-Tompkins run on ALL channels. Channel selection: best FQRS channel chosen by the number of occurrences where instantaneous heart-rate variability exceeded 30 bpm (fewer = better). Post-processing: smoothing of the FHR/RR series; when FQRS was deemed undetectable, output a CONSTANT series at 143 bpm or at the estimated dominant mode. Optimisation objective: the authors deliberately used the F1 statistic (harmonic mean of Se and PPV) rather than the Challenge score, "to evenly balance the performance in terms of positive predictive value and sensitivity of the detectors." Andreotti et al. (2013/2014) - won E2 and E5. Zero-phase FIR filtering for baseline wander, muscle artifact, powerline. ICA to generate pseudo-channels; QRS detector run on all of them; best channel picked by a Gaussian-kernel-based QRS agreement measure across channels (2014 version: kernel density estimation for fusing detectors across channels). Channel augmentation: depending on MQRS amplitude, the original 4...

**E. Evaluation.** PROTOCOL. Training on Set A (75 public annotated records). Blind evaluation on Set B (100 records, annotations withheld, scored automatically by uploading an annotation file to PhysioNet's web server) and on Set C (272 records, signals AND annotations withheld, scored by running the entrant's SOURCE CODE on a private PhysioNet server). Five events: E1 and E4 = FHR series; E2 and E5 = RR series; E3 = QT. E1 and E2 were scored on Set C and were open-source-only; E4 and E5 were scored on Set B and open to both open- and closed-source entries. Scoring: E1/E4 = beat-by-beat classification error in (beats/minute)^2, computed at 12 instants per record (one every 5 s); E2/E5 = "average root square error" in ms between matched reference and test RR intervals; E3 = differences between matched reference and test QT intervals. Records left un-annotated by a competitor received "a very high penalty value". The final score for an event = mean over all records in that event's set. WFDB software v10.5.19 was used for scoring. Submission was rate-limited across three phases (25-04-2013 to 01-06-2013: 3 entries; 01-06-2013 to 25-08-2013: 5 entries; 25-08-2013 to 05-09-2013: 1 entry) to limit overfitting to the hidden sets. Scoring code and all open-source entries remained public. LEAKAGE JUDGEMENT - MIXED, and the paper itself flags one case. LOW RISK, structurally: the split is record-wise into disjoint sets, and Set C's signals were never released at all, so no test-time tuning was...

**F. Results.** PARTICIPATION: 53 international teams, producing 208 sets of annotations and 93 open-source entries; "the vast majority" outperformed the organisers' provided sample entry (their Fig. 3 scatter plot, Set C in blue, Set B in red, sample entry in green). BEST SCORES ACHIEVED (quoted verbatim from section 2; note lower is better for all four): E1 (FHR series, Set C, open-source) = 179.439 (beats/minute)^2 E2 (RR series, Set C, open-source) = 20.793 ms E4 (FHR series, Set B) = 18.083 (beats/minute)^2 E5 (RR series, Set B) = 4.337 ms The order-of-magnitude gap between the Set C and Set B figures (179.4 vs 18.1; 20.8 vs 4.3) is the paper's own implicit evidence that fully blind, code-executed evaluation on unseen data is far harder than annotation-file submission on a released-signal set. WHO WON WHAT: Behar et al. (2013) "obtained top scores in all of the events in the Challenge". Andreotti et al. (2014) won events 2 and 5. Behar et al. (2014c) as an unofficial entry scored 1st on E1 and E4, 2nd on E3, and 3rd/2nd on E2/E5 (unofficial because the authors helped build the competition, though the editors state they were blinded to the validation data). Dessi et al. (2014) scored "at best, 4th compared to the original entries", cited as evidence that a plain template-subtraction + ICA approach is limited. QUALITATIVE RESULTS THE EDITORS EMPHASISE: high high-pass cut-off (~10 Hz) helps FQRS detection by killing maternal P and T (Behar); maternal template ADAPTATION beat extended...

**G. Limitations.** Stated by the authors: the database is only "modest" in size; a larger one is needed with more patients, longer recordings, more leads (including maternal chest ECG references) and pathological cases (arrhythmias, intra-uterine growth restriction, fetal acidosis); no annotated ST-segment or QT labels under normal and abnormal conditions exist, and clinically meaningful fetal analogues of adult abnormality measures (long QT, ST deviation) still need to be defined and adjusted for gestational age; "some minor errors in annotations persisted" after manual verification; not all Challenge records were scoreable (1 in Set A, 2 in Set B); the modelling assumptions behind subspace and template methods demonstrably do not all hold on this data. Additional limitations evident from the text: (1) The corpus is only 447 minutes of signal and the number of distinct subjects is never reported, so no subject-independent evaluation is possible from the published description. (2) It is a 4-channel benchmark by construction - every record was reformatted to exactly four abdominal channels - so the whole leaderboard, and nearly every reviewed method, depends on multi-channel spatial decomposition (ICA/PCA/SVD/deflation) or on cross-channel voting and channel selection. The benchmark therefore does not measure single-channel performance at all. (3) The final labels are algorithm-fused (Bayesian crowd-sourcing over 2013 entrants), so the gold standard is partly circular. (4) Scoring is on...

**H. Relevance.** HIGH relevance as the benchmark-and-protocol paper our project must cite and conform to, and as a catalogue of the classical baselines a TDA+CNN detector has to beat. Concretely usable by us: 1. THE BENCHMARK ITSELF. PhysioNet/CinC Challenge 2013 Set A (75 annotated records, 1 kHz, 60 s, 4 channels) is the standard training set; Set B (100 records) is the standard blind set and its web scoring interface "remains open for those wishing to compare their results with those from the official Challenge on events 4 and 5" - so we can still obtain an officially comparable E4/E5 score and place ourselves against the 18.083 (bpm)^2 and 4.337 ms bars. That is a free, credible external validation for a student project. 2. SINGLE-CHANNEL FRAMING. This paper is the strongest possible justification for our single-channel angle: the entire 2013 leaderboard is multi-channel, and steps 2-3 of the canonical pipeline (ICA, PCA, SVD, deflation, cross-channel voting, channel selection by SQI) are all unavailable with one lead. We can take any one abdominal channel from Set A/B and honestly frame it as the harder, under-served regime, citing the paper's own list of subspace assumptions (sources <= channels, stationarity, uncorrelatedness) as exactly what a single channel violates. 3. PREPROCESSING CONSTANTS WE CAN ADOPT DIRECTLY (see architecture_lessons) - especially the 10 Hz high-pass finding, which is a cheap, high-value ablation for our own front end. 4. EVALUATION AND LOSS DISCIPLINE....

**I. Research opportunity.** Several open gaps this editorial leaves, and they line up unusually well with a TDA project. 1. NO SINGLE-CHANNEL TRACK. Every record was forced to 4 channels and every reviewed method leans on multi-channel spatial separation or cross-channel fusion. The paper never reports what any method achieves on ONE abdominal lead. There is no published single-channel baseline in this benchmark - that number is simply missing, and producing it is a defensible contribution on its own. 2. NO REPRESENTATION LEARNING, NO GEOMETRY. The taxonomy the editors offer is exactly three buckets - temporal, spatial, frequency/time-frequency - and they conclude the frequency bucket is fundamentally limited by maternal/fetal spectral overlap in the QRS band, while the spatial bucket needs channels we do not have. A delay-embedding / persistent-homology representation is a FOURTH bucket (state-space topology) that is neither temporal-template, spatial-subspace, nor spectral, and it is precisely a representation that can separate two quasi-periodic sources by loop structure in phase space rather than by frequency band or by lead geometry. The paper's own diagnosis of why the existing buckets fail is the argument for trying this one. 3. DEEP LEARNING IS ABSENT. In 2014 the only learned detector in the whole special issue was an ESN, and the editors note its authors worked only on detection, not extraction. There is no CNN, no end-to-end learned detector, and no learned front end anywhere in the...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

- 447 total records in the Challenge 2013 corpus (Table 2, and section 2 text)
- Table 2 composition: ADFECGDB 25, Simulated FECGs 20, NIFECGDB 14, Non-Invasive FECG 340, Scalp FECG Database 48 records (Table 2)
- All records reformatted to 1 kHz sampling frequency, 1 minute duration, 4 non-invasive abdominal channels (section 2, sentence immediately after Table 2)
- Set A = 75 records (records + annotations public); Set B = 100 records (records only public); Set C = 272 records (both withheld) (section 2, bulleted list)
- 1 record in Set A and 2 records in Set B were unscoreable due to limited reference annotations (section 2)
- FHR scored at 12 instances per record, one every 5 seconds (section 2, scoring description)
- Best score E1 (FHR, Set C) = 179.439 (beats/minute)^2 (section 2, final paragraph)
- Best score E2 (RR, Set C) = 20.793 ms (section 2, final paragraph)
- Best score E4 (FHR, Set B) = 18.083 (beats/minute)^2 (section 2, final paragraph)
- Best score E5 (RR, Set B) = 4.337 ms (section 2, final paragraph)
- 53 international teams, 208 sets of annotations, 93 open-source entries (section 2, final paragraph; Fig. 3 shows the score scatter)
- Challenge phases and entry caps: phase 1 25-04-2013 to 01-06-2013 = 3 entries; phase 2 01-06-2013 to 25-08-2013 = 5 entries; phase 3 25-08-2013 to 05-09-2013 = 1 entry (section 2)

#### p21 — Matonia A, Jezewski J, Kupka T, Jezewski M, Horoba K, Wrobel J, Czabanski R, Kahankova R. Fetal electrocardiograms, direct and abdominal with reference heartbeat annotations. DOI...

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** có (`p21_Matonia2020-SilesiaDataset.pdf`, 4,132,822 byte) · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** Fetal heart rate variability is the key signal for assessing fetal wellbeing, but the only reliable source (direct/invasive scalp FECG) is available solely during advanced labour. Non-invasive abdominal FECG could replace it during pregnancy and labour, but the fetal QRS amplitude on the abdomen rarely exceeds 20 uV and is buried under the maternal ECG (3.5x larger on average) plus muscle, movement and mains interference. The stated bottleneck the paper attacks is not an algorithm but an evaluation problem: "the main difficulty in the performance evaluation of various methods to analyze abdominal signals lies in a very limited number of publicly available testing signals with reference information." The paper's contribution is therefore a benchmark dataset with expert-verified reference heartbeat annotations, plus a formal protocol and set of signal-quality indices for using it.

**B. Data.** Recorded with the authors' KOMPOREL system at the Department of Obstetrics and Gynecology, Medical University of Silesia, Katowice, Poland (ethics approval NN-013-345/02). Two subsets, released on figshare (doi:10.6084/m9.figshare.c.4740794). B1_Pregnancy_dataset - 10 records (B1_Pregnancy_01..10), 20 minutes each (200 min total), recorded between the 32nd and 42nd week of pregnancy. 4 abdominal channels, sampling rate 500 Hz, 16-bit. Files per record: B1_abSignals_X.ecg (LabView binary; 4 pre-filtered abdominal signals AND 4 MECG-suppressed FECG signals), B1_abSignals_X.txt (same as text), B1_Maternal_R_X.txt (maternal R sample indices), B1_Fetal_R_X.txt (fetal R sample indices + per-beat reliability flag), B1_FHR_analysis_X.jpg. Counts: 18,936 MQRS and 28,405 FQRS annotations; 130 FQRS (0.46%) carry flag 0 = unverifiable. Non-coincident complexes (using 100 ms MQRS / 40 ms FQRS windows): 8,106 maternal, 17,575 fetal. B2_Labour_dataset - 12 records (B2_Labour_01..12), 5 minutes each (60 min total), gestational weeks 38-42, advanced labour. 4 abdominal channels at 500 Hz PLUS a simultaneously recorded direct fetal scalp ECG at 1 kHz (raw and pre-filtered). Files: B2_abSignals_X.ecg/.txt, B2_dFECG_X.ecg/.txt, B2_Maternal_R_X.txt, B2_Fetal_R_X.txt, B2_FHR_analysis_X.jpg. Counts: 7,903 reference FQRS (located to 1 ms accuracy) and 5,177 MQRS; non-coincident 5,142 fetal and 2,415 maternal. Number of subjects: not explicitly reported. The paper states each patient was assigned...

**C. Method.** The paper's "method" is a data-generation and data-characterisation pipeline, not a detector proposal. 1. Pre-filtering (both subsets): a single multi-notch comb filter H(z) = z^-50 - (1/36)*[(1 - z^-60)/(1 - z^-10)]^2, giving a first cutoff of approximately 5 Hz (suppressing baseline wander and movement artefact) and a stop band spanning 45-55 Hz with notches every 50 Hz (suppressing mains and harmonics). Coefficients retuned for the 1 kHz direct FECG. 2. MECG suppression (abdominal signals only): subtraction of an averaged maternal template covering the FULL PQRST cycle, continuously updated to track slow morphology change, PLUS subtraction of the first derivative of the maternal QRS template to compensate for fiducial-point quantisation at 500 Hz. Spatial pre-filtering of the 4 abdominal leads first builds an auxiliary, undisturbed MECG that is used only for (a) locating maternal R waves and (b) computing the per-beat template scaling coefficients; this was introduced specifically because maternal/fetal QRS coincidence badly corrupts the subtraction. The template is built with Adaptive Impulse Correlated Filtering (AICF); Projective Filtering of Time-Aligned Beats (PFTAB) is cited as an alternative. 3. Channel selection: a signal-quality index based on signal quasi-periodicity, computed from the autocorrelation function evaluated in windows whose width adapts to the current fetal heart rate; the highest-scoring of the 4 MECG-suppressed channels is used for fetal R...

**D. Model.** No model. There is no neural network, no classifier, no learned parameters, no architecture, no input/output tensor shapes, no training procedure and no hyperparameters in this paper - it is a Nature Scientific Data Data Descriptor. The only algorithmic components with tunable settings are: the fixed-coefficient comb notch filter (Eq. 1, coefficients z^-50, z^-60, z^-10, gain 1/36); the maternal PQRST template length (a full maternal heart cycle, with MQRS width taken as 100 ms) and its per-beat scaling coefficients; the autocorrelation window width for channel quality, adapted to the fetal heart rate; K parallel detection functions with a continuously modified detection threshold (K is never given a numeric value); and the RR-smoothness functional Phi_k used to pick among them. Parameter count, optimiser, learning rate, batch size, epochs: not applicable / not reported.

**E. Evaluation.** No train/test split, no cross-validation, no held-out set - because nothing is trained. LEAKAGE RISK FOR THIS PAPER: not applicable (no learned model), but there are two data-integrity issues a downstream user must handle, and one genuine circularity. (1) Circular reference in B1 (real, and the paper admits it). The B1 fetal R-wave reference was produced by running the authors' own detection functions on the same abdominal signal, then expert-correcting. This is a silver standard, not an independent gold standard. Any detector that shares assumptions with the authors' detection functions will be flattered on B1. The paper states outright that the B1 reference FHR "is accurate to a limited extent only" and recommends omitting flag-0 beats when evaluating detection efficiency. Only B2 has a truly independent reference (direct scalp FECG recorded on a separate physical channel). (2) Pre-solved preprocessing. Both subsets ship the abdominal channels ALREADY MECG-suppressed by the authors' AICF template-subtraction pipeline, alongside the merely pre-filtered ones. Training or testing on the suppressed channels while claiming to work "from the raw abdominal signal" would be a serious methodological leak; a single-channel study must use the pre-filtered abdominal signals only and implement its own maternal suppression (or none). (3) Record overlap with ADFECGDB. Five of the twelve B2 records are the PhysioNet ADFECGDB records. A model pre-trained or hyperparameter-tuned on...

**F. Results.** The paper reports no new detection experiment. It reports (a) dataset characterisation statistics and (b) the authors' previously published results obtained on subsets of these data. Signal-quality characterisation. B1 (pregnancy): mean WM = 14.3 dB (never below 10.9 dB in any record) - the maternal ECG is more than 5x the interference amplitude; mean WF = 3.4 dB, with the best records reaching about 5 dB (fetal QRS nearly 2x the interference) and SOME RECORDS NEGATIVE (fetal QRS below the interference floor); mean WMF nearly 11 dB, range 8.5-17.7 dB, i.e. maternal QRS about 3.5x fetal QRS; mean WEM = 0.09 (maternal amplitude very stable); mean WEF = 0.52 (fetal amplitude highly unstable). B2 (labour): mean WM = 10.4 dB (always >6.5 dB); mean WF = 3.6 dB with a best around 7 dB and a worst of -2.7 dB; mean WMF = 6.8 dB, range 1.8-10.9 dB - the fetal component is relatively stronger during labour; WEM = 0.20 (worse than pregnancy, from uterine contractions); WEF = 0.33 (better than pregnancy). Clinical characterisation. B1: basal FHR 142.5 +/- 9.0 bpm (128.3-156.3), fluctuation 11.6 +/- 2.2 bpm (7.2-15.4), oscillations 13.0 +/- 3.3 bpm (9.0-17.9), LTV_Dawes 38.2 +/- 11.2 ms (25.3-58.6), STV_Dawes 6.4 +/- 3.2 ms (2.9-12.6), accelerations 0-10 per record, decelerations 0-2, per-record signal loss 0.0-11.2%. B2: basal FHR 133.0 +/- 7.2 bpm (125.4-152.4), fluctuation 8.3 +/- 2.3 bpm (6.4-14.7), oscillations 13.8 +/- 4.4 bpm (8.0-20.9), LTV_Dawes 46.1 +/- 13.0 ms (28.0-66.8),...

**G. Limitations.** Stated or evident: 1. Scale. 22 records, 260 minutes total, at most ~22 fetuses. Orders of magnitude too small to train a deep detector from scratch without heavy augmentation or synthetic pre-training. 2. B1 reference is not independent. Derived from the abdominal signal by the authors' own algorithm and then expert-corrected; the paper concedes it is "accurate to a limited extent only". 130 beats (0.46%) could not be verified at all and carry flag 0. 3. B2 gold standard covers only 60 minutes. 4. Released FECG channels are already MECG-suppressed by a proprietary pipeline, which can mask how hard the raw problem is. 5. Distribution shift within the release: B1 is antenatal (weeks 32-42, mother resting) and B2 is intrapartum (advanced labour, contractions, mother moving) - and their SNR profiles differ systematically (B1 WMF ~11 dB vs B2 6.8 dB; B1 WEF 0.52 vs B2 0.33). They are not interchangeable training/test pools. 6. Almost no pathology. Only 1 deceleration across all of B2 and 0-2 per B1 record; recordings that begin or end mid-deceleration are not counted as decelerations at all (the paper names B2_FHR_analysis_02.jpg and _03.jpg). Cannot support outcome- or pathology-stratified evaluation. 7. Signal loss up to 27.5% in one B2 record and 11.2% in one B1 record, which distorts any per-record aggregate. 8. No subject metadata - maternal BMI is explicitly acknowledged in the introduction as a major determinant of fetal QRS amplitude, yet no BMI is provided; no maternal...

**H. Relevance.** This paper contributes nothing to the architecture question, but it is close to the ideal evaluation substrate for a single-channel fetal-QRS detector, and it hands us the field's scoring protocol. Directly usable by our project: 1. Single-channel evaluation is natural here. Each record gives 4 independent abdominal leads; we can treat every lead as a separate single-channel problem, which turns 12 B2 records into 48 single-channel test cases against one shared gold-standard annotation set. Ref 37's finding that per-lead PI ranged 70.4-99.8% within the same recordings is exactly the variance a robust single-channel detector must beat, and is a ready-made baseline story. 2. The gold standard is genuinely independent for B2: the reference comes from a physically separate scalp electrode, so it cannot be circular with anything we compute from the abdomen. That is rare and is the strongest reason to make B2 our primary test set. 3. The evaluation protocol is fully specified and we should copy it exactly: +/-40 ms matching window (justified as the mean fetal QRS width), PI/Acc/Se/PPV/F1 with the exact formulas (note their Acc = TP/(TP+FP+FN), the Jaccard-style "accuracy" used throughout the fetal-ECG literature, NOT ordinary accuracy), and mean absolute RR error in both ms and bpm. Reporting /dRR/ matters: 2.14 ms (0.63 bpm) is the published bar for clinical beat-to-beat usefulness, and a detector can hit 98% F1 while being useless for variability if its jitter is large. Our TDA...

**I. Research opportunity.** The gap is large and squarely in our lane. 1. No learned detector exists on this dataset in this paper. Every result quoted is classical DSP (matched filtering, Pan-Tompkins, PCA/ICA). There is no published deep baseline here, and certainly no topological one. A CNN-on-persistence-images detector evaluated with the authors' own protocol would be the first of its kind on this data. 2. Everything strong is multi-channel. The 98%-class results (PCA, ICA, template subtraction) all consume 4 abdominal leads and/or rely on a clean auxiliary MECG built by spatial filtering across leads. Spatial filtering, PCA and ICA are structurally unavailable to a single-channel method. The honest single-channel figure on this dataset is therefore effectively unreported, and the per-lead spread of 70.4-99.8% suggests it is far below the multi-channel headline. Establishing a solid single-channel number here is an open, publishable slot. 3. Amplitude-based detection provably breaks on part of this data, and no one has addressed it. Several records have WF < 0 dB (fetal QRS beneath the noise floor) and mean WEF = 0.52 in B1, meaning fetal QRS amplitude swings by ~50% beat to beat. Matched filters and adaptive thresholds are precisely the methods that fail under amplitude instability. A method whose features are amplitude-invariant by construction - persistent homology of a delay embedding, where a beat appears as a loop whose existence does not depend on its height - has a specific,...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

- B1_Pregnancy_dataset: 10 records, 20 min each (200 min total), 4 abdominal channels, fs = 500 Hz, 16-bit, gestational weeks 32-42 - Methods 'Data collection' and Data Records 'B1_Pregnancy_dataset'
- B1 annotation counts: 28,405 FQRS and 18,936 MQRS complexes - Data Records, B1 section
- B1 unverified FQRS: 130 beats = 0.46%, flagged with reliability flag 0; corresponds to FHR signal loss of less than 55 s over the entire 200-minute B1 material - Data Records, B1 section
- B1 non-coincident complexes (using 100 ms MQRS / 40 ms FQRS windows): 8,106 maternal and 17,575 fetal - Data Records, B1 section
- B2_Labour_dataset: 12 records, 5 min each (60 min total), 4 abdominal channels at 500 Hz PLUS simultaneous direct scalp FECG at 1 kHz, gestational weeks 38-42 - Data Records, B2 section
- B2 annotation counts: 7,903 reference FQRS (located to 1 ms accuracy) and 5,177 MQRS; non-coincident 5,142 fetal and 2,415 maternal - Data Records, B2 section
- 5 of the 12 B2 records (r01, r04, r07, r08, r10) were previously public as PhysioNet ADFECGDB and constitute 9.6% of the duration of all currently presented abdominal signals - Data Records, opening paragraph
- Fetal QRS amplitude on the abdomen 'hardly ever exceeds 20 uV' - Background & Summary
- Amplifier: inherent noise <1 uV, CMRR = 115 dB, FECG band 0.05-150 Hz, high-pass raised to 1 Hz under strong LF noise - Methods 'Recording protocol'
- Pre-filter H(z) = z^-50 - (1/36)*[(1 - z^-60)/(1 - z^-10)]^2, first cutoff approx. 5 Hz, stop band 45-55 Hz, notches every 50 Hz - Methods 'Data processing', Eq. 1
- Analysis window widths S: MQRS = 100 ms, FQRS = 40 ms - Methods 'Amplitude relationships', below Eq. 7
- B1 mean SNR indices: WM = 14.3 dB (never below 10.9 dB), WF = 3.4 dB (best ~5 dB, some records negative), WMF almost 11 dB with range 8.5-17.7 dB (maternal QRS ~3.5x fetal), WEM = 0.09, WEF = 0.52 - Data Records B1 text and Table 1

### N3. Nền tảng TDA cho chuỗi thời gian

#### p22 — Perea JA, Harer J. "Sliding Windows and Persistence: An Application of Topological Methods to Signal Analysis." (SW1PerS). Downloaded copy is the arXiv preprint arXiv:1307.6188v2 [math.AT], 25 Nov...

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** có (`p22_PereaHarer2015-SlidingWindows.pdf`, 1,059,514 byte) · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** How can one measure, in a way that is agnostic to the shape of the repeating waveform, how periodic (or quasi-periodic) a sampled 1-D signal is? Classical methods (Lomb-Scargle periodogram, JTK_CYCLE) score periodicity by fitting cosine-like templates, so they are biased toward sinusoidal waveforms and return p-values that are hard to interpret geometrically. The authors propose instead to embed the signal with a sliding window (Takens time-delay) and measure the "roundness" of the resulting point cloud with 1-dimensional persistent homology, arguing that repetition of ANY pattern produces a loop in the delay-embedded cloud. The paper's stated goal is to provide the first complete theoretical analysis of how the resulting persistence diagram depends on embedding dimension, window size and coefficient field, and to show empirically that the resulting score (SW1PerS) is competitive with the best classical periodicity detectors while being waveform-agnostic. The original motivation is gene-expression cycle detection (cell cycle, circadian rhythms), not biomedical waveform detection.

**B. Data.** Entirely synthetic; no subjects, no recordings, no sampling rate in Hz, no channels. Experiment 1 (Sec. 7.1, "Shape Independence"): 10 hand-designed waveforms - (i) 2-periodic pure cosine, (ii-iv) the same cosine plus Gaussian noise with variance at 25%, 50%, 75% of signal amplitude, (v) noisy saw-tooth (25% noise), (vi) a chirp cos(gamma(t)) with gamma(t) = e^{at+b}, (vii) a noisy damped cosine with three periods, (viii) a spiky signal with three periods, (ix) a noisy square wave with two periods, (x) a 1-periodic random trigonometric polynomial Re(sum_{n=1..5} fhat(n) e^{2*pi*i*n*t}) with fhat(n) drawn uniformly from the unit disk in C. Each is evaluated at 50 evenly spaced time points, giving [s1,...,s50]. Experiment 2 (Sec. 7.2, "Classification Rates"): 6 profile families - positives (periodic, each spanning two periods) = cosine, cosine with trending, cosine with damping, cosine with increased peak steepness; negatives (non-periodic) = constant and linear. 100 profiles per family generated by phase shift, e.g. f_i(t) = cos(2t - pi*j/50) for j = 0..99, giving 600 profiles total. Each profile is sampled at 50 evenly spaced points on [0, 2*pi] and additive Gaussian noise is added at SD = 0%, 25% and 50% of the signal amplitude. GROUND TRUTH is by construction: a signal is labelled positive iff it was generated from one of the four periodic families. The authors explicitly justify (Remark 7.1) treating constant functions as non-periodic, because a constant signal's...

**C. Method.** SW1PerS (Sliding Windows and 1-dimensional Persistence Scoring). Pipeline: (1) the discrete signal is turned into a continuous function fS by cubic spline interpolation (preceded, in the noisy experiment, by a 7-point simple moving average); (2) the sliding-window / delay embedding SW_{M,tau} f(t) = [f(t), f(t+tau), f(t+2tau), ..., f(t+M*tau)]^T maps each time t to a point in R^{M+1}; the collection over many t is the sliding-window point cloud; (3) each point is centred by C(x) = x - (<x,1>///1//^2)*1 (subtracting the window mean, i.e. removing DC) and then normalised to unit norm; (4) one iteration of mean-shift is applied in the point cloud, replacing each x by the mean of {y : 1 - <x,y> < epsilon} with epsilon = cos(pi/16), then re-normalising; (5) the Vietoris-Rips filtration is built on the resulting cloud and its 1-dimensional persistent homology is computed with coefficients in F_p; (6) the periodicity score is Score(S) = mp(dgm(X_S))/sqrt(3), where mp = max over the diagram of (death - birth). Key parameter rules derived theoretically: the window length must resonate with the period - L*(M+1)*tau = 2*pi, so that M*tau = (M/(M+1)) * (2*pi/L), i.e. the window is just under one period long; the embedding dimension must satisfy M >= 2N where N is the highest Fourier harmonic to be preserved; and the coefficient field must have p > N (or Q), never F_2. The theoretical machinery is (a) Fourier truncation S_N f plus stability of persistence diagrams to bound the...

**D. Model.** No learned model exists. There is no neural network, no classifier, no training procedure, no loss, no parameter count and no optimisation of any kind - the method is fully unsupervised and analytic, and its "hyperparameters" are chosen by theory. Fixed configuration used in the experiments: N = 10 (number of Fourier harmonics preserved); M = 2N = 20, so the embedding dimension is M + 1 = 21; delay tau set by L*(M+1)*tau = 2*pi so that the window length M*tau = (20/21)*(2*pi/L); L = 2 in the classification experiment, and L in {2,3,4} with the best (largest) score reported in the shape-independence experiment; coefficient field F_11 (p = 11 > N = 10, as required by Thm 6.8); moving-average window = 7 data points; mean-shift = 1 iteration with cosine-similarity threshold epsilon = cos(pi/16); score normaliser sqrt(3). Input shape: a 50-point signal per instance, splined then sampled to populate a point cloud in R^21 (the exact number of point-cloud points for Sec. 7 is not reported; in the Sec. 6.3 illustration 151 points are used in R^5). Output shape: one scalar in [0,1]. Persistent homology computed by a fast union-find-based 1-D implementation following Mischaikow and Nanda; the authors say details will appear in a companion paper. No GPU, no runtime, no memory figures reported.

**E. Evaluation.** There is NO train/test split, NO cross-validation and NO learning, because the method has no trainable parameters - so the classical notion of data leakage does not apply. The evaluation is: apply the fixed unsupervised score to all 600 synthetic profiles, sweep a threshold on the score, and plot ROC/AUC against the by-construction labels; AUCs are reported separately per positive-profile family and per noise level. LEAKAGE RISK: low-to-none in the supervised sense (nothing is fitted to the data). However, two weaker forms of optimism are present and should be flagged: (i) the pipeline hyperparameters (N = 10, L, p = 11, moving-average width 7, epsilon = cos(pi/16)) were selected by the authors with full knowledge of the same synthetic data on which the ROC curves are computed - there is no held-out set for hyperparameter selection, so the reported AUCs are in-sample with respect to parameter choice; (ii) in Experiment 1 the score is maximised over L in {2,3,4} and the best value reported, which is an oracle-style selection over three candidate periodicities. Generalisation evidence is weak in a different way: the evaluation is synthetic-only, so there is no cross-dataset, cross-subject or synthetic-to-real transfer result at all. For our purposes the honest statement is: the paper offers proofs plus a clean synthetic sanity check, not an evaluation that predicts real-signal performance.

**F. Results.** Experiment 1 (Fig. 4, scores and induced ranking of 10 waveforms). SW1PerS scores, top to bottom: 0.9488, 0.8810, 0.8810, 0.8810, 0.8132, 0.7455, 0.6777, 0.6770, 0.4066, 0.4066. The competing methods' scores over the same 10 signals: JTK_CYCLE 1.77E-30 ... 1.00E+00; Lomb-Scargle 1.46E-07 ... 9.98E-01; Total Persistent Homology 1.00E+00 ... 1.55E+00. The authors' reading: only SW1PerS orders the signals by degradation of periodicity rather than by resemblance to a cosine, and its scores are spread over a interpretable [0,1] range instead of being p-values. Experiment 2 (Fig. 6, AUC by method x profile x noise level, 50 samples per signal). Noise SD = 0%: JTK, Lomb-Scargle, Total PH and SW1PerS all achieve AUC = 1 on cos, damp, peak and trend. Noise SD = 25%: JTK cos 1, damp 0.94, peak 0.99, trend 1; Lomb-Scargle cos 1, damp 0.98, peak 1, trend 1; Total PH cos 0.97, damp 0.65, peak 0.92, trend 0.98; SW1PerS cos 1, damp 0.90, peak 0.99, trend 0.99. Noise SD = 50%: JTK cos 0.96, damp 0.56, peak 0.72, trend 0.93; Lomb-Scargle cos 0.99, damp 0.66, peak 0.86, trend 0.97; Total PH cos 0.78, damp 0.59, peak 0.77, trend 0.81; SW1PerS cos 0.98, damp 0.71, peak 0.87, trend 0.93. So at the hardest noise level SW1PerS is the best of the four on damped (0.71) and peaked (0.87) profiles, second to Lomb-Scargle on plain cosine (0.98 vs 0.99) and trended cosine (0.93 vs 0.97), and clearly better than the naive Total Persistent Homology baseline everywhere. Ablation (Fig. 7): with the moving...

**G. Limitations.** (1) No real data of any kind - the entire empirical section is synthetic 50-sample signals; ECG is mentioned only once, in the related-work paragraph, as a domain where delay embeddings have been used for chaos testing. There is no fetal ECG, no maternal-fetal separation, no abdominal recording. (2) The output is a single global periodicity score per window, not a per-beat detection, so the paper gives nothing about localising an R-peak in time; converting the score into a detector is entirely left to the reader. (3) Computational cost: the authors state explicitly that as the embedding dimension grows the Rips complex "outweighs the computational resources, making the persistent homology calculation unfeasible" - which is exactly why they cap M at 20. No runtimes or complexity figures are reported. (4) The central practical claim - that maximum persistence is MAXIMISED when the window size matches the period - is proved only for the pure-cosine motivating example and otherwise stated as a conjecture for future work ("we plan to establish our conjecture that mp(dgm) is maximized by our choice of window size"); only a lower bound (Thm 6.8) is proved in general. (5) The method needs L, the number of periods spanned by the window, and this is handled by brute-force enumeration over {2,3,4} with best-score reporting - fragile if the true period is unknown or varying, which is exactly the fetal-HRV case. (6) Hyperparameters (N, tau, epsilon, moving-average width, p) are hand-set...

**H. Relevance.** This is the theoretical licence for the "Takens embedding -> persistent homology" first half of our pipeline, and the only paper in our set that answers, with proofs, the two parameter questions our team must answer: how long should the sliding window be, and how many delay coordinates should it have. Concretely applicable: (a) WINDOW LENGTH: set M*tau = (M/(M+1)) * (one fetal RR interval); at 120-150 bpm that is roughly 0.40-0.50 s, so a window of about 0.38-0.48 s rather than an arbitrary 1 s or 2 s segment. Choosing the window to resonate with the fetal period is what makes the H1 loop maximally persistent, and is also what will suppress the maternal complex when the maternal window would be a different length. (b) EMBEDDING DIMENSION: Prop. 5.1 says you need M >= 2N to preserve harmonics up to N, and that this is necessary, not merely sufficient. For fetal QRS energy up to ~40-50 Hz with a fundamental of ~2-2.5 Hz, N is roughly 20, so the embedding dimension M+1 should be about 41 - substantially larger than the 3-7 dimensions commonly used ad hoc in TDA-of-ECG work. If we truncate below that we provably lose fQRS morphology. (c) NORMALISATION: pointwise mean-centering followed by L2-normalisation of every embedded window is exactly the right invariance for abdominal ECG - it kills baseline wander (DC per window) and the amplitude gap between the maternal and fetal complexes, which is the dominant nuisance in single-channel fECG. Our persistence images should be...

**I. Research opportunity.** Several gaps that our project can occupy. (1) The paper produces a global periodicity SCORE, never a per-beat DETECTION - nobody here converts persistence into an R-peak time stamp. Sliding SW1PerS along the abdominal record to produce a per-sample topological confidence curve, then feeding it (or the underlying persistence image) to a CNN that outputs fQRS locations, is an unclaimed step. (2) The conjecture that maximum persistence is maximised exactly at the matching window size is explicitly left unproved; empirically demonstrating on real fECG that the persistence-vs-window-length curve peaks at the fetal RR interval (and separately at the maternal RR) would be a genuinely novel and useful contribution - and it doubles as a maternal/fetal separation mechanism, since maternal and fetal periods differ by roughly 2x. (3) Zero real physiological validation: the entire method has never been tested on ECG, let alone single-channel abdominal fetal ECG with a maternal complex 5-20x larger than the fetal one. Whether the centring+normalisation really suppresses the maternal contribution is an open empirical question we could answer. (4) The scalar max-persistence summary throws away the whole diagram; the authors themselves flag persistence-diagram feature rings and W_q signatures as future work - vectorising the diagram as a persistence image and learning on it is exactly the gap. (5) The computational-feasibility barrier they name (Rips complex blowing up with dimension) is...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

- Embedding dimension M+1 = 21 (from M = 2N, N = 10) - Sec. 7.1 and 7.2 text, and 'Important Assumption' after Prop. 5.1
- M >= 2N is necessary and sufficient for linear independence of u_0,u_1,v_1,...,u_N,v_N when M*tau < 2*pi - Proposition 5.1
- Window/period resonance condition L*(M+1)*tau = 2*pi, so window length M*tau = (M/(M+1))*(2*pi/L) = 0.952 * period for M = 20 - Sec. 2.1 motivating example and Sec. 5.2
- Coefficient field F_11 (p = 11), required by Thm 6.8 to satisfy p > N = 10 - Sec. 7.1 and 7.2 text
- Moving-average denoising window = 7 data points - Sec. 7.2 text
- Mean-shift: 1 iteration, cosine-similarity neighbourhood {y : 1 - <x,y> < epsilon} with epsilon = cos(pi/16) - Sec. 7.2 text
- Score normaliser sqrt(3): Score(S) = mp(dgm(X_S))/sqrt(3) - Sec. 7 opening paragraph
- Lower bound on max persistence: mp(dgm(Y)) >= sqrt(3)*max_{1<=n<=N} r_n - epsilon*N, with birth <= epsilon*N and death >= sqrt(3)*max r_n - Theorem 6.8, eq. (6)
- Torus radii r_n = 2/fhat(n)/ / sqrt(//S_N f//_2^2 - /fhat(0)/^2), with sum_n r_n^2 = 1 - Structure Theorem 5.6(3), eq. (4)
- Approximation bound d_H(X,Y) <= 4^{k-2} * //R_N f^{(k)}//_2 / (N+1)^{k-1/2} * sqrt(M+1), and /mp(dgm X) - mp(dgm Y)/ <= 2 d_B(dgm X, dgm Y) - Theorem 4.5(1),(2)
- 50 evenly spaced time samples per signal, both experiments - Sec. 7.1 and Sec. 7.2 text
- 600 synthetic profiles = 6 shapes x 100 phase shifts; 4 periodic (cos, trend, damp, peak) + 2 non-periodic (constant, linear) - Sec. 7.2 text
