#### p23 — Adams H, Emerson T, Kirby M, Neville R, Peterson C, Shipman P, Chepushtanova S, Hanson E, Motta F, Ziegelmeier L. Persistence Images: A Stable Vector Representation of Persistent Homology.

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** có (`p23_Adams2017-PersistenceImages.pdf`, 2,656,168 byte) · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** Persistence diagrams (PDs) summarise persistent homology as a multiset of points in the plane. That space carries only a metric structure (bottleneck / p-Wasserstein), which is enough for clustering and nearest-neighbour methods but NOT for the large majority of ML tools (SVM, decision trees, neural networks, feature selection, dimension reduction, means and variances). In addition, bottleneck/Wasserstein distances grow expensive as the number of off-diagonal points increases. The paper's explicit Problem Statement asks: how can a PD be represented so that (i) the output is a vector in R^n, (ii) the representation is stable with respect to input noise, (iii) it is efficient to compute, (iv) it maintains an interpretable connection to the original PD, and (v) it lets the user adjust the relative importance of points in different regions of the diagram (e.g. emphasising medium-persistence rather than high-persistence features, as Bendich et al. 2016 needed for brain-artery age regression). Prior vectorizations either lacked stability (binning by Rouse et al.; Gaussian smoothing by Donatini/Ferri, Chung, Pachauri - all discontinuous when a point emerges from the diagonal), had constants that blow up with diagram size (Carriere et al.'s L2 bound), or were kernel-only and thus impractical for large training sets with linear methods (Reininghaus et al.).

**B. Data.** Not a physiological study: no subjects, no sampling rate, no channels, no annotators. Three purely synthetic, self-generated datasets. (1) SIX-SHAPE POINT CLOUDS: six classes - unit cube, circle of diameter 1, sphere of diameter 1, three clusters with centres random in the unit cube, three-within-three clusters (minor centres perturbed by <0.1 from the major centres), torus with major diameter 1 and minor diameter 0.5. 25 point clouds per class, each 500 points sampled uniformly at random, giving 150 point clouds embedded in R^3; two additive Gaussian noise levels sigma = 0.05 and sigma = 0.10 are generated separately. H0 and H1 PDs computed with the Vietoris-Rips filtration under the Euclidean metric. (2) LINKED TWIST MAP: x_{n+1} = x_n + r*y_n(1-y_n) mod 1, y_{n+1} = y_n + r*x_n(1-x_n) mod 1; five parameter values r = 2.5, 3.5, 4.0, 4.1, 4.3; 50 randomly chosen initial conditions per value; 1000 iterations per trial -> 250 planar point clouds. (3) ANISOTROPIC KURAMOTO-SIVASHINSKY PDE: du/dt = -Laplacian(u) - 2*Laplacian^2(u) + r*(du/dx)^2 + (du/dy)^2; five anisotropy values r = 1, 1.25, 1.5, 1.75, 2 with 30 trials each = 150 simulations; Fourier spectral method, periodic boundary conditions, 512x512 spatial grid (the authors note each surface is effectively a point in R^262144, quoted in the text as R^266144), fourth-order exponential time-differencing Runge-Kutta time stepping, low-amplitude white-noise initial conditions; surfaces snapshotted at t = 3, 5, 10, 15, 20 and...

**C. Method.** The persistence image pipeline, in five steps. (1) Compute the PD B (Vietoris-Rips for point clouds, sublevel set for functions) and discard infinite-persistence points (e.g. the H0 bar for global connectivity). (2) Apply the linear map T(x,y) = (x, y-x) to obtain birth-PERSISTENCE coordinates; the diagonal becomes the horizontal axis. (3) Choose a differentiable probability density phi_u with mean u; the paper uses the normalised symmetric Gaussian g_u(x,y) = (1/(2*pi*sigma^2)) * exp(-[(x-u_x)^2 + (y-u_y)^2]/(2*sigma^2)). (4) Choose a non-negative weighting function f that is ZERO along the horizontal axis, continuous and piecewise differentiable - this zeroing is what buys stability, because it makes a point emerging from the diagonal contribute continuously. Definition 1: the persistence surface is rho_B(z) = sum over u in T(B) of f(u)*phi_u(z). (5) Definition 2: overlay a grid of n pixels on a chosen subdomain and set each pixel to the integral of rho_B over that box; the resulting matrix, flattened, is the persistence image. Multiple homological dimensions are handled by computing one PI per dimension and CONCATENATING the vectors. When all birth coordinates are zero (typical H0), the authors instead use 1-dimensional distributions and produce a 1-D PI. The three user choices are resolution, distribution (and its variance), and weighting function; the paper explicitly calls the flexibility a strength and the non-canonicity a weakness, and leaves the variance choice as...

**D. Model.** NO NEURAL NETWORK EXISTS IN THIS PAPER. The 'model' is the deterministic feature map above plus off-the-shelf shallow classifiers. Input shape: a PD (variable-size multiset). Output shape: a fixed-length vector - 20x20 = 400 dims in Sections 6.1, 6.2, 6.3 and 6.4.1; 10x10 = 100 dims in Section 6.4.2; concatenated H0+H1 doubles this. Concrete hyperparameters actually used: Section 6.1/6.2 - resolution 20x20, Gaussian variance sigma = 0.1, ramp weighting w_b; Section 6.3 (SSVM) - resolution 20x20, variance sigma = 1e-4, noise level 0.05; Section 6.4.1 (linked twist map) - resolution 20x20, variance sigma = 0.005, 'chosen after a preliminary parameter search and classification effort'; Section 6.4.2 (aKS) - resolution 10x10, variance sigma = 0.01. Weighting function throughout: f(x,y) = w_b(y) with w_b(t) = 0 for t <= 0, t/b for 0 < t < b, and 1 for t >= b, where b is the largest persistence value over all trials in the experiment. Training procedures: (a) K-medoids with K = 6, Voronoi iteration, 1000 random medoid initialisations, keeping the clustering with the lowest score; (b) one-against-all 1-norm-regularised linear SVM with 5-fold cross-validation, features retained by non-zero weight magnitude ratios per Chepushtanova et al. 2014; (c) subspace discriminant ensemble (many weak learners on random fixed-dimension subspaces, scores averaged, Ho 1998), 10 trials averaged for the linked twist map. No learning rate, optimiser, epoch count, batch size, dropout, parameter count...

**E. Evaluation.** Mixed and mostly weak by modern standards; the notion of 'subject' does not exist, so subject-independence is not applicable - every instance is an independent simulation from a random initial condition, which makes instance-level splitting legitimate in principle. Section 6.1 (K-medoids, Table 1): NO train/test split at all - all 150 point clouds are clustered together and 'accuracy' is the in-sample proportion assigned to a medoid of the correct class. Because the method is unsupervised there is no label leakage, but there is also no generalisation estimate, and the number of clusters K = 6 is set to the known number of classes. Section 6.2 (parameter sweep): the same in-sample K-medoids accuracy is reported across 20 resolutions x 20 variances, so the robustness claim is a robustness-of-fit claim, not a robustness-of-generalisation claim. Section 6.3 (sparse SVM): 5-fold cross-validation, 100% accuracy - but the paper does NOT state that the sparse-SVM feature selection was nested inside the CV folds, and it then reports that classification using only the selected 10 pixels is lossless; if selection saw all folds this is classic selection bias. UNCLEAR, moderate risk. Section 6.4.1 (linked twist map): 'classification and cross-validation with a discriminant subspace ensemble', 10 trials averaged - fold count, split ratio and whether splits are by initial condition are NOT reported; UNCLEAR. Section 6.4.2 (aKS): the variance-based baseline fits on 2/3 and tests on 1/3,...

**F. Results.** Table 1 (K-medoids on 150 shape point clouds; accuracy and seconds to build the 150x150 distance matrix from precomputed PDs). Noise sigma = 0.05 / noise sigma = 0.1: PD H0 L1 96.0% 37346s / 96.0% 42613s; PD H0 L2 91.3% 24656s / 91.3% 25138s; PD H0 Linf 60.7% 1133s / 63.3% 1149s; PD H1 L1 100% 657s / 96.0% 703s; PD H1 L2 100% 984s / 97.3% 1042s; PD H1 Linf 81.3% 527s / 66.7% 564s; PL H0 L1 92.7% 29s / 96.7% 33s; PL H0 L2 77.3% 29s / 82.0% 34s; PL H0 Linf 60.7% 2s / 63.3% 2s; PL H1 L1 83.3% 36s / 80.7% 48s; PL H1 L2 83.3% 50s / 66.7% 69s; PL H1 Linf 74.7% 8s / 66.7% 9s; PI H0 L1 93.3% 9s / 95.3% 9s; PI H0 L2 92.7% 9s / 95.3% 9s; PI H0 Linf 94.0% 9s / 96.0% 9s; PI H1 L1 100% 17s / 95.3% 18s; PI H1 L2 100% 17s / 96.0% 18s; PI H1 Linf 100% 17s / 96.0% 18s. Summary given in the text: PI beats nearly every PL matrix and beats PD in half the trials, at 1/60 to 1/4000 of the PD computation cost. Section 6.2: accuracy is visually flat across resolutions 5x5 to 100x100 and variances 0.01 to 0.2 (Figure 2). Section 6.3: 5-fold-CV one-against-all sparse SVM on 400-dim H1 PIs reaches 100% accuracy; the union of selected pixels over all six binary models is only 10 distinct pixels; the circle class is separated by pixels 21 and 22 (high-persistence region), the torus by pixels 59 and 98 (short-to-moderate persistence), the sphere by pixels 58, 59, 60, 79, 98, the cube by 59 and 79. Section 6.4.1 (linked twist map, subspace discriminant ensemble, mean of 10 trials): H0 PIs alone 49.8%, H1...

**G. Limitations.** (1) Entirely synthetic evaluation - no real-world data of any kind, let alone biomedical or physiological. (2) No time series and no delay/Takens embedding anywhere; the PDs come from static point clouds or static surfaces, so the paper says nothing about temporal windowing, overlap, latency or event localisation. (3) No neural network: PIs are never fed to a CNN, so the paper does not validate the specific PI-to-CNN step our project proposes. (4) Downstream classifiers are shallow and the datasets tiny (150-250 instances, 25-50 per class). (5) No held-out evaluation in the headline Table 1 experiment - accuracy is in-sample clustering accuracy with K set to the true class count. (6) Hyperparameters for Section 6.4.1 were selected by a preliminary search on the same data; the weighting parameter b uses the global maximum persistence over all trials. (7) Feature selection in 6.3 is not stated to be nested within the cross-validation. (8) No error bars, standard deviations or significance tests on any accuracy (only 'averaged over 10 trials' or 'over 100 partitions'). (9) The Gaussian variance choice is explicitly declared an open problem, and the choices of resolution, distribution and weighting are admitted to be non-canonical. (10) Stability is proved only with respect to the 1-Wasserstein distance, not the bottleneck distance (Remark 6, following Reininghaus et al. Theorem 3); since W1 can be much larger than the bottleneck distance, the guarantee is weaker than the PD's...

**H. Relevance.** This paper supplies the third block of our stated pipeline (Takens embedding -> persistent homology -> PERSISTENCE IMAGES -> CNN) and nothing else. What we can take: (a) the exact PI definition and the birth-persistence coordinate change, so our implementation is provably the canonical one and citable; (b) the stability theorems - Theorem 9 bounds the L1 change of the surface by (sqrt(5)/grad f/ + sqrt(10)//f//_inf/sigma) * W1(B,B'), and Theorem 10 extends this to the PI vector in L1, L2 and L-infinity with constants independent of the number of diagram points. This is the single strongest theoretical argument in our paper for why a CNN on PIs should be robust to abdominal-ECG noise and maternal-ECG residue: a bounded perturbation of the signal gives a bounded W1 perturbation of the diagram gives a bounded change in every CNN input pixel. Note the constant contains 1/sigma, so a very small Gaussian variance destroys stability - a concrete design constraint we should state and respect. (c) The empirical robustness to resolution and variance means we do NOT need an expensive grid search over PI parameters and can justify a single sensible setting. (d) The H0+H1 concatenation result (+16.8 points on the linked twist map, +4.0 points on aKS) argues for feeding both dimensions as separate channels to the CNN rather than picking one; for a delay-embedded ECG, H1 should carry the loop structure of each cardiac cycle and H0 the cluster/amplitude structure. (e) The sparse-SVM...

**I. Research opportunity.** Wide open gaps that our project sits squarely inside. (1) The paper never couples PIs to a convolutional network - PIs are only ever fed to K-medoids, a linear sparse SVM, and a random-subspace ensemble. Whether a CNN can exploit the spatial structure of a PI better than a linear model on the same 400 pixels is untested. (2) No time-series application at all: no sliding-window PI, no Takens/delay embedding, and therefore no evidence on how PI parameters should scale with embedding dimension, delay, or window length - all of which we must choose. (3) No event-detection or localisation task: PIs are used for whole-object classification, so the question of whether a PI computed on a short window can LOCALISE a fetal R-peak to within 50 ms is entirely unaddressed; this is arguably the core research question we can claim. (4) No biomedical, physiological or noisy real-world signal; the noise model is i.i.d. Gaussian on point coordinates, nothing like maternal-ECG interference, baseline wander, EMG or electrode artefact. (5) The Gaussian variance is left explicitly as an open problem, and no principled rule is offered for choosing the PI subdomain or the weighting parameter b in a train/test-clean way. (6) Stability holds only for 1-Wasserstein; nobody has quantified the empirical W1 perturbation induced by realistic ECG noise, so the practical value of the bound is unknown. (7) No comparison of PIs against non-topological baselines on the same task (only against PDs, PLs,...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

- PI, H1, all three metrics (L1/L2/Linf): 100% K-medoids accuracy at noise sigma=0.05 - Table 1, p.16
- PI, H1, all three metrics: 95.3% / 96.0% / 96.0% at noise sigma=0.1 - Table 1, p.16
- PI, H0: 93.3% / 92.7% / 94.0% (L1/L2/Linf) at noise 0.05 and 95.3% / 95.3% / 96.0% at noise 0.1 - Table 1, p.16
- Best PD baseline: 100% (H1, L1 and L2, noise 0.05) but only 60.7% for H0/Linf at noise 0.05 - Table 1, p.16
- Best PL baseline: 92.7% (H0, L1, noise 0.05); most PL rows fall between 60.7% and 83.3% - Table 1, p.16
- PI distance-matrix construction time: 9 s for H0 and 17-18 s for H1, versus 37,346 s and 42,613 s for the PD H0 L1 matrix - Table 1, p.16 (hardware stated in Sec 6.1: 1.3 GHz Intel Core i5, 4 GB RAM)
- Dataset size for Table 1: 150 point clouds = 6 classes x 25 clouds x 500 points each - Sec 6.1 text
- Parameter sweep: 20 resolutions from 5x5 to 100x100 in steps of 5 and 20 variances from 0.01 to 0.2 in steps of 0.01, accuracy insensitive throughout - Sec 6.2 text and Figure 2
- Sparse SVM: 100% accuracy under 5-fold cross-validation on 400-dimensional H1 PIs (resolution 20x20, variance 1e-4, noise 0.05) - Sec 6.3 text
- Only 10 distinct pixels out of 400 were needed, cumulatively across the six one-against-all models, with no loss of accuracy - Sec 6.3 text and Appendix D
- Discriminative pixel indices: circle = 21 and 22; torus = 59 and 98; cube = 59 and 79; sphere = 58, 59, 60, 79, 98 (all out of 400) - Figure 3 caption and Figure 11 caption (Appendix D)
- Linked twist map: 82.5% accuracy with concatenated H0+H1 PIs versus 65.7% with H1 only and 49.8% with H0 only, averaged over 10 trials - Sec 6.4.1 text

#### p24 — Carriere M, Chazal F, Ike Y, Lacombe T, Royer M, Umeda Y. PersLay: A Neural Network Layer for Persistence Diagrams and New Graph Topological Signatures.

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** có (`p24_Carriere2020-PersLay.pdf`, 798,689 byte) · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** Persistence diagrams - the standard output of persistent homology - live in a metric space that is not a Hilbert space: they are unordered multisets of variable cardinality, with no well-defined addition or scalar multiplication. This blocks their direct use in machine learning. Prior work handled this either with fixed, hand-designed vectorisations (persistence landscapes, silhouettes, persistence images) that have very few trainable parameters and so cannot adapt to a task, or with kernels (PSS, PWG, Sliced Wasserstein, Persistence Fisher) whose cost is at least linear and often quadratic in the number of diagram points per matrix entry, making them intractable on large datasets of large diagrams. The paper asks: can we LEARN the vectorisation of a persistence diagram, end-to-end, as a differentiable neural-network layer, in a way that (a) is permutation invariant, (b) recovers the existing vectorisations as special cases, and (c) scales to 100,000 diagrams? A second, orthogonal problem is addressed: which real-valued vertex function should be used to build a persistence diagram from a graph - the paper proposes the Heat Kernel Signature with extended persistence and proves it stable.

**B. Data.** No human subjects, no physiological signals, no sampling rate, no channels - this is a pure machine-learning methods paper on synthetic point clouds and graph benchmarks. (a) ORBIT datasets (Table 3, supplementary): points generated by the discrete dynamical system x_{n+1} = x_n + r y_n (1 - y_n) mod 1, y_{n+1} = y_n + r x_{n+1}(1 - x_{n+1}) mod 1, from a random initial (x0, y0) in [0,1]^2. Five classes correspond to r = 2.5, 3.5, 4.0, 4.1, 4.3. N = 1,000 points per orbit. ORBIT5K = 1,000 orbits per class = 5,000 point clouds (same data as Le and Yamada 2018). ORBIT100K = 20,000 orbits per class = 100,000 point clouds. Classes balanced by construction. Ground truth = the generating parameter r, i.e. it is exact by construction, no annotation and no annotator variability. (b) Eleven public graph-classification benchmarks (Table 4, supplementary), all with pre-existing published labels: REDDIT5K (5,000 graphs, 5 classes, av. 508.5 nodes / 594.9 edges), REDDIT12K (12,000 / 11 / 391.4 / 456.9), COLLAB (5,000 / 3 / 74.5 / 2457.5), IMDB-B (1,000 / 2 / 19.77 / 96.53), IMDB-M (1,500 / 3 / 13.00 / 65.94), COX2 (467 / 2 / 41.22 / 43.45), DHFR (756 / 2 / 42.43 / 44.54), MUTAG (188 / 2 / 17.93 / 19.79), PROTEINS (1,113 / 2 / 39.06 / 72.82), NCI1 (4,110 / 2 / 29.87 / 32.30), NCI109 (4,127 / 2 / 29.68 / 32.13). Sampling rate: not applicable. Number of channels: not applicable (the closest analogue is the 2 or 4 parallel diagram-type "channels"). NOTE: the PMLR main PDF (10 pp) does NOT...

**C. Method.** Two contributions. (1) NEW GRAPH SIGNATURE. Define the normalised graph Laplacian L_w = I - D^{-1/2} A D^{-1/2}, with eigenvalues 0 = lambda_1 <= ... <= lambda_n <= 2 and orthonormal eigenfunctions psi_k. The Heat Kernel Signature with diffusion parameter t is hks_{G,t}(v) = sum_{k=1..n} exp(-t lambda_k) psi_k(v)^2. This vertex function induces an EXTENDED persistence filtration on the graph (sublevel graphs G_alpha for alpha increasing, then superlevel graphs G^alpha for alpha decreasing). Extended persistence, unlike ordinary persistence, produces four point types - Ord0 (downward branches), Rel1 (upward branches), Ext0+ (connected components), Ext1- (loops) - and crucially all points have FINITE coordinates, so no special handling of infinite bars is needed and one generic architecture works for every homology dimension. Two stability theorems are proved: Thm 2.2, d_B(Dg(G,t), Dg(G',t)) <= C(G,t) //W//_F for a Laplacian perturbation W; Thm 2.3, the map t -> Dg(G,t) is 2-Lipschitz in bottleneck distance, so the diagrams are robust to the choice of t and it suffices to sample a few t on a log scale. Diagrams computed with GUDHI. (2) PERSLAY. A permutation-invariant differentiable layer: PersLay(Dg) := op({ w(p) . phi(p) : p in Dg }), where op is a permutation-invariant operator (sum, max, min, k-th largest), w: R^2 -> R is a trainable point-weight function, and phi: R^2 -> R^q is a trainable point transformation; both w and phi are parameterised so that gradients are known...

**D. Model.** Deliberately minimal TWO-LAYER network (Fig. 3), explicitly chosen "to produce knowledge and understanding rather than achieving the best possible performances". FORWARD PATH: each object is encoded as a SET of persistence diagrams (2 for orbits: Alpha_0, Alpha_1; 4 for graphs: Ord0, Rel1, Ext0+, Ext1-); each diagram goes through its OWN independent PersLay instance (parallel branches, no weight sharing); the branch outputs are concatenated, together with an optional non-topological feature vector (for graphs: the eigenvalues of the normalised graph Laplacian plus the deciles of the HKS); the concatenation passes through BATCH NORMALISATION and then a single FULLY-CONNECTED layer whose output gives the class labels. INPUT SHAPE: a variable-cardinality multiset of R^2 points per diagram, truncated by prom(500) to at most 500 points, coordinates in the unit square. OUTPUT SHAPE: a length-C class vector (C = 2 to 11). TWO PERSLAY BRANCH TYPES (supplementary notation, Section C.3): Im(p,(a,b),q,op) = Gaussian point transformation phi_Gamma sampled on a p x p grid over the unit square (a p x p persistence image per point), followed by a convolution with a filters of size b x b; weight function w optimised on a q x q grid; aggregation op. Pm(d1,d2,q,op) = line point transformation phi_L with d1 lines, followed by a permutation-EQUIVARIANT function (Zaheer et al. 2017) in dimension d2; weight function w on a q x q grid; aggregation op. WEIGHT FUNCTION: w(p) = w_ij . 1{p in C_ij},...

**E. Evaluation.** ORBIT: "we split observations in 70%-30% training-test sets and report the average test accuracy over 100 runs", chosen for consistency with Le and Yamada 2018. GRAPHS: for each dataset, 10 independent ten-fold cross-validations; each ten-fold splits the data into 10 equally sized folds and records accuracy on fold i after training on the other 9, cycling i from 1 to 10. Two numbers are reported: "Mean" = the mean of those 10 ten-fold experiments (consistent with Zhang et al. 2018 / RetGK), and "Max" = the best single ten-fold accuracy obtained, presented as comparable to the single-10-fold numbers reported by Tran 2018, Verma and Zhang 2017, Xinyi and Chen 2019 and Xu et al. 2019. Subject-independent: NOT APPLICABLE - there are no subjects, no sessions and no grouping structure. Each orbit is an independently and randomly initialised simulation and each graph is an independent sample, so the classic within-subject segment-leakage failure mode cannot occur here. LEAKAGE JUDGEMENT: LOW risk of sample-level leakage (independent samples, split at the object level, no overlapping windows, no shared source per class beyond the class definition itself). However there are two real optimistic biases that our own protocol must not copy. (i) The "Max" column is the best of ten 10-fold runs - selecting the best repetition by its test score is test-set selection, and the authors themselves note that only "Mean" is "naturally more robust"; the graph-benchmark comparisons in Table 2...

**F. Results.** ORBIT (Table 1, accuracy %, 70/30 split averaged over 100 runs): ORBIT5K - PSS-K 72.38 (+/-2.4), PWG-K 76.63 (+/-0.7), SW-K 83.6 (+/-0.9), PF-K 85.9 (+/-0.8), PersLay 87.7 (+/-1.0); ORBIT100K - PersLay 89.2 (+/-0.3), with no kernel competitor able to run at that scale. So PersLay gains +1.8 points over the best kernel on ORBIT5K, and gains a further +1.5 points simply by using 20x more data. GRAPHS (Table 2, PersLay Mean / Max vs. best competitor): REDDIT5K 55.6/56.5 (GIN 57.0, RetGK 56.1); REDDIT12K 47.7/49.1 (RetGK 48.7); COLLAB 76.4/78.0 (RetGK 81.0, GIN 80.1, FGSD 80.0); IMDB-B 71.2/72.6 (GIN 74.3, FGSD 73.6); IMDB-M 48.8/52.2 (FGSD 52.4, GIN 52.1); COX2 80.9/81.6 (RetGK 80.1); DHFR 80.3/80.9 (RetGK 81.5); MUTAG 89.8/91.5 (FGSD 92.1, RetGK 90.3); PROTEINS 74.8/75.9 (GCNN 76.3, GIN 75.9); NCI1 73.5/74.0 (RetGK 84.5, GIN 82.7); NCI109 69.5/70.1 (FGSD 78.8). PersLay beats the earlier persistence-diagram network of Hofer et al. 2017 (54.5 and 44.5 on REDDIT5K/REDDIT12K) with 55.6 and 47.7. ABLATION 1 - trainable weight grid size N (Table 6, train/test accuracy %): MUTAG None 92.3/88.9, 2x2 91.1/88.8, 5x5 91.7/89.6, 10x10 92.3/89.9, 20x20 93.7/88.3, 50x50 94.1/87.7; COLLAB None 76.5/75.3, 2x2 78.6/75.8, 5x5 79.0/76.2, 10x10 80.0/76.5, 20x20 83.5/73.9, 50x50 94.0/71.3. Larger grids monotonically raise TRAIN accuracy but overfit past 10x10 on both datasets. Run-time cost of the grid is small (MUTAG 2.30 s with no grid vs 2.78 s at 50x50; COLLAB 26.0 s vs 45.6 s). ABLATION 2 -...

**G. Limitations.** (1) DOMAIN: no time series, no biosignal, no ECG, no 1-D data - only synthetic 2-D point clouds and graph benchmarks; nothing about noise, non-stationarity, baseline wander or maternal interference. (2) TASK SHAPE: PersLay maps a whole diagram to a single vector; the network emits one label per object. There is no per-point, per-window or per-sample output, hence no temporal localisation, no peak picking, no post-processing rules, no receptive field in ms - it is a feature extractor and classifier, not a detector, and the paper offers nothing on turning it into one. (3) REPORTING GAPS: the loss function is never named; parameter counts are never given; there is no learning-rate schedule, no dropout, no weight decay, no early-stopping criterion, no validation split. (4) OPTIMISTIC STATISTICS: the "Max" column is the best of ten 10-fold runs and is nevertheless compared against competitors' single 10-folds; competitor numbers in Table 2 are quoted from other papers under possibly different protocols. (5) HYPERPARAMETER SELECTION: Table 5 shows large per-dataset variation (filtration function, prom on/off, Im vs Pm, grid 10 vs 20, epochs 70 to 1000, EMA decay 0.0/0.9/0.99) with no described selection procedure. (6) HEADLINE ACCURACY IS NOT SOTA on graphs: PersLay loses to RetGK/GIN/FGSD on COLLAB, IMDB-B, IMDB-M, NCI1 and NCI109, sometimes by a wide margin (NCI1: 73.5 vs RetGK 84.5). The authors are candid that the two-layer net is for interpretability, not performance. (7)...

**H. Relevance.** This paper is the direct methodological ancestor of the "persistence image -> CNN" block our project proposes, and it says that block should be LEARNED rather than fixed. Six things transfer concretely. (i) Replace the fixed persistence image with a PersLay layer: PersLay proves that persistence images are the special case (phi = Gaussian on an R^2 grid, op = sum, w arbitrary), so making the pixel weights w and the Gaussian samples trainable is a strict generalisation that costs almost nothing and gave measurable gains (Table 6: MUTAG test 88.9 with no weight grid vs 89.9 with a 10x10 trainable grid; COLLAB 75.3 vs 76.5). Public TensorFlow code and a GUDHI module exist, so this is a low-risk substitution. (ii) Use the Im(p,(a,b),q,op) recipe as our block: p x p Gaussian persistence image, a conv filters of size b x b, q x q trainable weight grid, sum aggregation - their working values were p = 10/15/20, a = 10, b = 2, q = 10/20. That is a tiny CNN head we can afford to run per sliding window. (iii) Set defaults from their ablations rather than guessing: sum, not max (won on both datasets); a 10x10 trainable weight grid, not larger (10x10 was the test-accuracy optimum on BOTH MUTAG and COLLAB, with 20x20 and 50x50 raising train accuracy while dropping test accuracy - direct evidence of overfitting in exactly the regime we might have chosen); treat phi (Gaussian / line / triangle) as a genuine hyperparameter because no transformation won uniformly. (iv) prom(500) - keep only...

**I. Research opportunity.** The gap is large and lands squarely where our project sits. (1) NO TIME SERIES AND NO DETECTION. PersLay has never been applied to a 1-D biosignal, and it produces one vector per object with no temporal output. Nobody in this paper turns a learnable persistence-diagram layer into a per-sample or per-window DETECTOR with peak picking and refractory-period post-processing. A sliding-window PersLay over Takens-embedded single-channel abdominal ECG, emitting a fetal-QRS probability trace and evaluated with the standard detection metrics (sensitivity, PPV, F1 at a 50 ms tolerance) rather than accuracy, is a genuinely new construction. (2) NO NOISE OR NON-STATIONARITY STUDY. Their inputs are clean synthetic orbits and combinatorial graphs; abdominal ECG is dominated by maternal QRS, muscle noise and baseline wander. The paper's own discussion notes that PersLay with op=sum is not a continuous map and that they deliberately do NOT force the weight function to vanish near the diagonal - the near-diagonal region is exactly where signal noise lives in a Takens-embedding persistence diagram. Empirically characterising what a trained PersLay weight grid does to near-diagonal points on noisy physiological data would be a real contribution, and their Fig. 5 (visualising the learned 20x20 weight grid before and after training) gives us a ready-made interpretability figure format. (3) NO STRONG NON-TDA BASELINE. On ORBIT they compare only against other TDA kernels, so the paper never...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

- ORBIT5K accuracy: PersLay 87.7 (+/-1.0) % vs PF-K 85.9 (+/-0.8), SW-K 83.6 (+/-0.9), PWG-K 76.63 (+/-0.7), PSS-K 72.38 (+/-2.4) - Table 1, main paper p.5 (verified by rendering the page)
- ORBIT100K accuracy: PersLay 89.2 (+/-0.3) %, no kernel competitor reported (all dashes) - Table 1, main paper
- ORBIT protocol: 70%-30% train-test split, average test accuracy over 100 runs - Section 3.2 text, main paper
- ORBIT data: 5 classes r = 2.5, 3.5, 4.0, 4.1, 4.3; N = 1,000 points per orbit; ORBIT5K = 1,000 orbits per class (5,000 clouds); ORBIT100K = 20,000 orbits per class (100,000 clouds) - Section 3.2 text and supplementary Table 3
- Graph accuracy, PersLay Mean/Max: REDDIT5K 55.6/56.5, REDDIT12K 47.7/49.1, COLLAB 76.4/78.0, IMDB-B 71.2/72.6, IMDB-M 48.8/52.2, COX2 80.9/81.6, DHFR 80.3/80.9, MUTAG 89.8/91.5, PROTEINS 74.8/75.9, NCI1 73.5/74.0, NCI109 69.5/70.1 - Table 2, main paper p.8 (verified by rendering)

#### p25 — Som A, Choi H, Ramamurthy KN, Buman MP, Turaga P. PI-Net: A Deep Learning Approach to Extract Topological Persistence Images.

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** có (`p25_Som2020-PINet.pdf`, 837,767 byte) · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** Persistence diagrams (PDs) and persistence images (PIs) are useful ML features but suffer two bottlenecks: (i) computing them analytically (filtration -> PD -> PI) is extremely slow, and (ii) the analytic pipeline is not differentiable, so it cannot be embedded inside a neural network and trained end-to-end. The paper asks: can a CNN be trained to REGRESS the persistence image directly from raw input data in one forward pass, so that PIs become cheap and differentiable? They call the resulting networks Signal PI-Net (1D multivariate time series input) and Image PI-Net (multi-channel image input). This is an amortisation / surrogate-model paper, not a detection paper — there is no QRS, ECG, or biomedical time-series task anywhere in it.

**B. Data.** FOUR datasets, none biomedical-ECG. (1) GENEactiv [ref 50]: 29 human-activity classes, 152 subjects, wrist-worn tri-axial accelerometer, recorded at 100 Hz, DOWN-SAMPLED to 50 Hz. Non-overlapping fixed frames of 250 time-steps (5 s) and 500 time-steps (10 s). Class distribution shown in Fig. 5 (largest classes ~6000-8000 frames). ~75% of frames used for training, rest for test. (2) USC-HAD [ref 53]: 12 activity classes, 14 subjects, tri-axial MotionNode accelerometer at front right hip, 100 Hz down-sampled to 50 Hz, frames of 250 time-steps only. Split by SUBJECT: first 8 subjects train, remaining 6 subjects test. Much smaller (Fig. 6: per-class counts roughly 20-160 frames). (3) CIFAR10 / CIFAR100 [26]: 50000 train / 10000 test, 32x32x3. (4) SVHN [32]: 73257 train / 26032 test, 32x32x3. GROUND TRUTH: not human labels — the "ground truth" is the analytically computed persistence image. For time series they use Scikit-TDA (Ripser for PDs, Persim for PIs) with LEVEL-SET (scalar-field / sub-level-set) filtration computed separately on each of the x, y, z accelerometer channels; a local minimum gives birth to a 0-dim homology class that dies at the local maximum. PDs are plotted as birth-time vs LIFE-TIME (not birth vs death) to use the full 2D plane. For images each channel is turned into a 3D point cloud (x-coord, y-coord, intensity), x/y normalised to [0,1], and 1-dimensional persistent homology is computed per channel; PD points with life-time < 0.02 are discarded....

**C. Method.** Replace the analytic TDA pipeline with a supervised REGRESSION network. Stage 1: compute ground-truth PIs analytically for the training set. Stage 2: train a CNN to map raw input -> 50x50 PI per channel (a regression problem, MSE for signals, binary cross-entropy for images). Stage 3: at inference, PIs are obtained in a single forward pass (3 orders of magnitude faster on GPU), and because the network is differentiable, PIs can be fused into any downstream deep model. Transfer learning is explored for the image branch: train Image PI-Net on CIFAR100 (source), then fine-tune on CIFAR10/SVHN (target) either with all target training data (variant "FA" = Fine-tune All) or with only 500 images per class ("FS" = Fine-tune Subset), to simulate limited target data. For the signal branch, the Signal PI-Net trained ONLY on GENEactiv training data is applied without retraining to the GENEactiv test set AND to both train and test sets of USC-HAD (cross-dataset transfer with no fine-tuning). Downstream fusion: the predicted PI is concatenated with features learned by a conventional classifier (MLP, 1D CNN, or DenseNet) and the fused model is trained for classification.

**D. Model.** Implemented in Keras / TensorFlow. SIGNAL PI-NET (the branch relevant to 1D physiological signals). Input: b x t x n (batch x time-steps x channels); in their experiments n = 3 (x,y,z accelerometer), t = 250 or 500 at 50 Hz. Encoder: FOUR 1D convolution layers, kernel size 3 for ALL layers, stride 1, zero ("same") padding so length is preserved by the convolutions. Filter widths: 128, 256, 512, 1024. Layers 1-3: Conv1D -> BatchNorm -> ReLU -> MaxPool1D (pool size 3, stride 2). Layer 4: Conv1D -> BatchNorm -> ReLU -> GlobalAveragePooling1D (so the whole frame collapses to a single 1024-dim vector). Head: one Dense layer of size 2500 x n, ReLU activation, reshaped to 50 x 50 x n. So one 50x50 PI is emitted per input channel. No dilation, no residual connections, no attention, no skip connections. No dropout mentioned in Signal PI-Net. IMAGE PI-NET. Input b x h x w x c (32x32x3). Same encoder topology but 2D convolutions, and here ALL conv layers use 128 filters with kernel 3 (not the increasing 128/256/512/1024 widths of the signal branch), same padding, MaxPool2D kernel 3 stride 2. Encoder output -> latent Dense layer of size 2500 -> reshape to 50x50 -> decoder = ONE 2D deconvolution layer, kernel size 50, stride 1, c filters, zero-padded to preserve h,w -> BatchNorm -> Sigmoid. Output 50x50xc. TRAINING - Signal PI-Net: batch size 128, 1000 epochs, Adam, MSE loss. Learning-rate schedule 1e-3 for first 300 epochs, 1e-4 for next 300, 1e-5 for final 400. Trained only on the...

**E. Evaluation.** Two different evaluations must be judged separately. (a) PI-Net regression quality: reported only as final train/test MSE (signals) and BCE (images) in Table 1. There is NO held-out metric on PI reconstruction other than test loss, no per-pixel error distribution, no Wasserstein/bottleneck distance to the true PD, and no visual error analysis quantified. (b) Downstream classification: weighted F1 (accelerometer) over 5 runs mean+/-std; accuracy (images) over 3 runs mean+/-std with a two-sided t-test p-value vs the DenseNet baseline. LEAKAGE JUDGEMENT. - GENEactiv: HIGH RISK. The paper says only "approximately 75% of the frames for training and the rest as the test set" across 152 subjects. It never states the split is subject-wise or record-wise, and frames are non-overlapping but come from the same continuous recordings. A random frame-level split within the same subjects is the natural reading, so adjacent frames from one subject can straddle train and test. Same-subject leakage is likely. - USC-HAD: LOW RISK for the classifier — explicitly subject-independent (first 8 subjects train, last 6 test). Additionally LOW RISK for the PI generator, because Signal PI-Net was trained only on GENEactiv and applied to USC-HAD with no fine-tuning, i.e. genuine cross-dataset transfer (different sensor, different body location, different subjects). - Images: standard public train/test splits, low risk; the CIFAR100 -> CIFAR10/SVHN transfer is cross-dataset, low risk. - Additional...

**F. Results.** Regression loss (Table 1): Signal PI-Net t=250 train 0.00159 / test 0.00158; t=500 train 0.00187 / test 0.00187 (MSE). Image PI-Net (BCE): CIFAR10 1.99793/2.06193, CIFAR10 FA 2.02095/2.04441, CIFAR10 FS 0.51734/0.52560, SVHN 1.53533/1.51732, SVHN FA 1.57923/1.54195, SVHN FS 0.41519/0.40955 (values transcribed exactly as printed; the FS rows are oddly far below their base rows and the paper offers no explanation). Weighted F1, accelerometer (Table 2), columns GENEactiv t=250 / GENEactiv t=500 / USC-HAD t=250: MLP-PI 46.45+/-0.32 / 49.67+/-0.63 / 43.21+/-0.66 MLP-Signal PI-Net 49.47+/-0.69 / 53.69+/-1.08 / 48.15+/-0.67 MLP-SF 41.70+/-0.41 / 42.01+/-0.42 / 35.68+/-0.11 MLP-SF+PI 48.57+/-0.37 / 49.82+/-0.62 / 44.31+/-0.36 MLP-SF+Signal PI-Net 50.66+/-0.78 / 54.44+/-0.80 / 48.97+/-0.30 1D CNN 53.56+/-0.31 / 54.97+/-1.35 / 54.58+/-0.64 1D CNN+PI 54.28+/-0.23 / 56.38+/-0.23 / 54.64+/-0.62 1D CNN+Signal PI-Net 54.41+/-0.21 / 56.41+/-0.22 / 57.82+/-0.78 Take-aways stated by the authors: PIs (analytic or learned) beat the 19-dim statistical feature; fusing PI with SF helps a lot in the MLP setting; the 1D CNN is a much stronger classifier than the MLP; and fusing PIs into the 1D CNN gives only a MARGINAL gain (+0.85 / +1.44 / +3.24 F1 points). Notably the LEARNED PI is never worse than the analytic PI and is sometimes better (e.g. USC-HAD 1D CNN: 57.82 learned vs 54.64 analytic). Image classification (Table 3), CIFAR10 / SVHN accuracy with p-value vs DenseNet: DenseNet 83.80+/-0.12 /...

**G. Limitations.** 1. It is an approximation with no error guarantee. There is no bound relating the predicted PI to the true PI, no bottleneck/Wasserstein stability analysis of the learned map, and no reported per-pixel or per-dataset PI reconstruction error beyond a single scalar loss. The paper never inspects WHERE the network's PIs are wrong. 2. The whole PI is regressed from a single global-average-pooled vector (1024-dim for signals), so all spatial/temporal localisation is destroyed at the bottleneck. The network outputs one PI per frame, never a per-time-step or per-beat output. It is fundamentally a whole-window summariser, not a detector. 3. Frame length is fixed by architecture (the head is a Dense layer of size 2500 x n), so the model cannot be applied to variable-length signals without retraining. 4. Only 0-dimensional level-set homology on 1D signals and 1-dimensional homology on image point clouds are handled. No Takens/delay embedding, no Vietoris-Rips on delay-embedded point clouds, no H1 for signals. 5. The downstream gains are small when the base model is already strong: only +0.85 to +3.24 weighted F1 when fused into the 1D CNN, and about +1 accuracy point on CIFAR10. Authors themselves call the image gain "marginal". 6. The evaluation is thin: no cross-validation, no ablation of PI-Net's own architecture (depth, kernel size, filter widths, pooling, loss choice are all asserted, never justified), no comparison against other learned-topology approaches such as Hofer et...

**H. Relevance.** Relevance is INDIRECT but real, and it is highest on the "make TDA affordable" axis rather than the "optimal detector architecture" axis. 1. This is the canonical citation for the amortisation trick our project may need. If Takens embedding -> persistent homology -> persistence image is too slow to run per-window over long abdominal ECG records, we can train a small CNN to regress the persistence image from the raw window and get ~1400x speed-up on GPU, with downstream accuracy equal to or better than the analytic PIs (Table 2: 57.82 learned vs 54.64 analytic on USC-HAD). That makes real-time / whole-record TDA feasible and, crucially, makes the topological branch DIFFERENTIABLE so the whole fECG detector can be trained end-to-end instead of using frozen precomputed PIs. 2. Concrete fusion recipe we can copy verbatim: reduce the vectorised PI (50x50xn = 2500 per channel) to 32 dimensions by PCA and concatenate it to the global-average-pool / penultimate feature of the 1D CNN detector. This keeps the added parameter count negligible and is exactly the "does TDA help on top of a strong CNN?" experiment our project is designed to answer. Their answer on accelerometer data — a strong 1D CNN gains only +0.85 to +3.24 F1 from PIs, whereas a weak MLP gains +7 to +12 — is the single most important warning for us: TDA helps a lot when the base learner is weak and much less when it is strong. Our ablation must therefore include a properly tuned non-TDA 1D CNN baseline, or we will...

**I. Research opportunity.** Several gaps this paper leaves wide open that our project sits directly inside. 1. PI-Net only ever emits ONE persistence image per fixed-length frame from a global-average-pooled bottleneck. There is no dense / per-sample topological output. A fetal-QRS detector needs a per-sample (or per-window-stride) output. Nobody here has built a fully-convolutional, encoder-decoder or U-Net-style PI-Net that produces a topological feature map at signal resolution — that is a genuine architectural contribution our project could make. 2. Signal PI-Net supports only 0-dim level-set homology of the raw signal. It never learns PIs from a Takens/delay-embedded point cloud, i.e. exactly the H0/H1 Rips persistence our project is built on. Learning a delay-embedding-based PI surrogate for 1D signals is unaddressed. 3. No biomedical signals at all, and in particular no signal where the topological feature of interest is a sparse transient (QRS) buried in a stronger interfering waveform (maternal ECG). Whether the learned PI survives that regime is untested. 4. No ablation of PI-Net's own architecture or of PI hyperparameters, so there is no guidance on how deep/wide the surrogate must be, or what grid size and sigma matter. An ablation study on a fECG dataset would be new. 5. No accuracy/approximation analysis: nobody has quantified how much downstream detection performance is lost by using a learned PI instead of an exact one, as a function of the surrogate's capacity. On a detection task...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

- Signal PI-Net encoder: 4 Conv1D layers, kernel size 3, stride 1, filters 128/256/512/1024; MaxPool1D kernel 3 stride 2 after layers 1-3 (Section 4.2, 'Signal PI-Net' paragraph, page 4-5)
- Signal PI-Net head: Dense of size 2500 x n, ReLU, reshaped to 50 x 50 x n (Section 4.2, page 5)
- Input window: t = 250 or 500 time-steps at 50 Hz = 5 s or 10 s; n = 3 channels (Section 4.2 and Section 5.1, page 5)
- Signal PI-Net local receptive field before global average pooling = 45 samples = 0.90 s at 50 Hz [DERIVED BY ME from the reported kernel/stride/pool spec; NOT stated in the paper]
- Signal PI-Net training: Adam, MSE loss, batch size 128, 1000 epochs, LR 1e-3 (ep 1-300) / 1e-4 (ep 301-600) / 1e-5 (ep 601-1000) (Section 5.1, 'Training Signal PI-Net', page 5-6)
- Signal PI-Net final loss, t=250: train 0.00159, test 0.00158 (Table 1, page 6)
- Signal PI-Net final loss, t=500: train 0.00187, test 0.00187 (Table 1, page 6)
- Image PI-Net BCE loss: CIFAR10 1.99793/2.06193, CIFAR10 FA 2.02095/2.04441, CIFAR10 FS 0.51734/0.52560, SVHN 1.53533/1.51732, SVHN FA 1.57923/1.54195, SVHN FS 0.41519/0.40955 (Table 1, page 6; transcribed exactly as printed, the FS rows look anomalous)
- 1D CNN classifier: 10 Conv1D layers, 32 filters, kernel 3, stride 1, zero-padded; MaxPool kernel 3 with stride 1 on odd layers and stride 2 on even layers; global-average-pool on the 10th layer (Section 5.1, 'Data Characterization and Classification', page 6)
- 1D CNN classifier receptive field approx 217 samples approx 4.34 s at 50 Hz, total downsampling 16x [DERIVED BY ME from the reported layer spec; NOT stated in the paper]
- MLP classifier: 8 dense layers of 128 units, ReLU, Dropout 0.2 + BatchNorm after each (Section 5.1, page 6)
- GENEactiv weighted F1 (Table 2, page 6): MLP-SF 41.70+/-0.41 (t=250); MLP-PI 46.45+/-0.32; MLP-Signal PI-Net 49.47+/-0.69; MLP-SF+PI 48.57+/-0.37; MLP-SF+Signal PI-Net 50.66+/-0.78

#### p26 — Turkes R, Montufar G, Otter N. On the Effectiveness of Persistent Homology. Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 35 (NeurIPS 2022). arXiv:2206.10551. No DOI. Code:...

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** có (`p26_Turkes2022-EffectivenessPH.pdf`, 1,337,208 byte) · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** The paper asks a foundational, method-diagnostic question rather than an application question: persistent homology has been used successfully in hundreds of applications, but it is not known WHY it works, for which classes of problems it is effective, or what topological and geometric features it can actually detect. The authors distinguish the 'usefulness' of PH (empirically attested) from its 'effectiveness' (its demonstrable capability to produce an intended result), and set out to identify concrete, well-specified problem classes where PH provably and empirically performs well. They select three fundamental shape-analysis primitives - the number of 1-dimensional holes, curvature, and convexity - because each is a canonical topological or geometric property and because shape classification underpins computer vision and physical-structure recognition. A secondary, and for our purposes equally important, aim is to settle an ongoing dispute in the TDA community over the interpretation of long versus short persistence intervals (the folklore that long bars are signal/topology and short bars are noise/geometry). Explicitly NOT a goal: beating task-specific state of the art. The claim is only that PH can solve tasks it was never designed for, and can reveal topology and geometry simultaneously without combining several specialist models.

**B. Data.** No physiological data of any kind; no subjects, no sampling rate, no channels. Datasets are point clouds generated by the authors plus one public image dataset. (1) NUMBER OF HOLES: 20 different shapes in R2 and R3, arranged so that four distinct shapes share each hole count in {0, 1, 2, 4, 9}; 50 point clouds sampled per shape, each of 1000 points drawn uniformly over the shape, giving a balanced set of 20 x 50 = 1000 point clouds. Ground truth = the hole count of the generating shape, known by construction. (2) CURVATURE: unit disks D on surfaces of constant curvature - Euclidean plane (kappa=0), sphere of radius 1/sqrt(kappa) (kappa>0), Poincare disk model of the hyperbolic plane (kappa<0), with kappa restricted to [-2,2] so a unit-radius disk embeds as a spherical cap. Training set: kappa in {-2, -1.96, ..., -0.04, 0, 0.04, ..., 1.96} (101 values), 10 point clouds each of 500 points sampled with probability proportional to surface area = 1010 clouds. Test set: 100 clouds built the same way at 100 kappa values chosen uniformly at random from [-2,2]. Ground truth = the generating kappa. All these disks are homeomorphic (contractible), so ordinary homology cannot separate them - by design the task isolates geometry. (3) CONVEXITY: 5000 points sampled per shape in R2. 'Regular' subset = triangle, square, pentagon, circle and a concave variant of each (8 shapes x 60 = 480 clouds). 'Random' subset = 480 clouds, convex ones from the convex hull of 10 random points via...

**C. Method.** Three parallel PH pipelines, each hand-designed so that the filtration encodes the specific signal of its task, followed by a shallow classical learner - plus a matched set of learned baselines on the same train/test splits. HOLES: approximate each point cloud at scale r by its ALPHA simplicial complex (chosen over Vietoris-Rips purely for computational efficiency; the authors state Vietoris-Rips would work equally well), filtered by the Distance-to-Measure function rather than the plain distance function so that outliers only enter late in the filtration. Extract the 1-dimensional persistence diagram, vectorize it as a persistence image, a persistence landscape, or a plain vector of the 10 longest lifespans, and classify with an SVM. Two variants are reported throughout: 'PH simple' (SVM on the 10 lifespans, no PH-side tuning at all) and 'PH' (grid search over signature type and its parameters). CURVATURE: compute the exact geodesic pairwise distance matrix appropriate to the sign of the curvature (hyperbolic, Euclidean or spherical - available in closed form because the disks have constant curvature), build the Vietoris-Rips filtration from it using Ripser, extract 0- and 1-dimensional diagrams, vectorize as above, regress with an SVR. CONVEXITY: this is the novel pipeline. Rasterize the point cloud to a 20x20 binary image (pixel black if it contains any point) and build a CUBICAL complex - deliberately not Vietoris-Rips, because in a simplicial complex the two components...

**D. Model.** There is no proposed neural network. The learner on PH features is an SVM. Full specification of everything trained: PH HEAD - sklearn SVC for classification and SVR for regression, regularization C in {0.001, 1, 100}. Signature hyperparameters searched jointly: simple signature of the 10 longest lifespans; persistence images at resolution 10x10 with bandwidth sigma in {0.1, 0.5, 1, 10} and weight function w(x,y) in {1, y, y^2}; persistence landscapes at resolution 100 keeping the longest 1, 10 or all intervals. DTM filtration parameter m = 0.03, i.e. 0.03 x 1000 = 30 nearest neighbours. Libraries: GUDHI for the holes and convexity tasks, Ripser/ripser.py for curvature (chosen as the fastest Vietoris-Rips implementation). ML BASELINE - SVM on the normalized pairwise distance matrix, upper triangle flattened to a vector; because the matrix dimension grows quadratically in the number of points, each cloud is subsampled to 100 distinct random points, giving a 100x100 matrix; C in {0.001, 1, 100}. NN BASELINES - multilayer perceptrons on the same normalized distance matrices; depth in {1, 2, 3, 4, 5} (searched only for the 'NN deep' variant; 'NN shallow' has a single hidden layer), layer widths in {64, 256, 1024, 4096}, learning rate in {0.01, 0.001}; softmax activation; cross-entropy loss for classification and MSE for regression; batch normalization with zero momentum and dropout at rate 0.5 applied after every input and hidden layer; each grid setting trained for 2 epochs...

**E. Evaluation.** Split protocol differs per task, and the leakage picture differs with it. HOLES: train on 80% of the 1000 point clouds and test on the remaining 20% (200 clouds), with the test set optionally subjected to transformations or noise. This is a RANDOM SPLIT OVER CLOUDS, and each of the 20 generating shapes contributes 50 clouds, so the same underlying shape appears in both train and test. In our terminology that is the analogue of a within-subject random segment split, i.e. HIGH leakage risk in principle. The mitigating fact is that four different shapes share each label, so a memorising model would still have to generalise across shapes within a class; and the transformation/noise test conditions do impose a genuine distribution shift. Net judgement: moderate-to-high risk for the clean condition, lower for the noisy/transformed conditions. CURVATURE: LOW risk and the cleanest protocol in the paper - training curvatures lie on a fixed grid of 101 values while test curvatures are 100 values drawn uniformly at random from the continuous interval, so essentially every test kappa is unseen; this is a true generalisation-to-new-parameter-values test, analogous to a subject-independent split. CONVEXITY: LOW risk, and the strongest design here. Four conditions are reported - train regular / test regular, train random / test random, and both out-of-distribution directions (train regular / test random, train random / test regular). The two OOD conditions are a genuine cross-distribution...

**F. Results.** PH wins on all three tasks, and its margin grows exactly where deep baselines are weakest - transformation, noise, out-of-distribution shift, and small training sets. HOLES (test accuracy, mean of 3 runs, Table 2): tuned PH scores 1.00 on clean data and holds 1.00 under translation, 1.00 under rotation, 0.98 under stretch, 1.00 under shear and 1.00 under Gaussian noise, degrading only to 0.93 under up-to-10% outliers. The untuned 'PH simple' variant, which is nothing more than an SVM on ten sorted lifespans, still reaches 0.94 clean and 0.82 with outliers. PointNet matches PH at 1.00 on clean data but collapses to 0.20 under translation, 0.74 under rotation, 0.84 under stretch, 0.87 under shear and 0.55 under outliers - the authors note this transformation-sensitivity is consistent with several prior reports despite PointNet's design intent. The SVM on distance matrices sits at 0.62-0.68 and the MLPs at 0.47-0.54, essentially chance for a 5-class problem; the authors connect the MLP failure to prior work showing neural networks progressively destroy the Betti numbers of their input. CURVATURE (MSE, mean of 3 runs, Table 3): the best result is the simplest - an SVR on all 0-dimensional lifespans at 0.06, with the grid-searched 0-dim PH at 0.08. Restricting to the ten longest 0-dim bars degrades this to 0.21, a 3.5x worsening, which is the paper's cleanest evidence that many short bars carry the geometric signal. 1-dimensional PH is worse overall (all bars 0.34, ten longest...

**G. Limitations.** Stated by the authors: only three selected problems and datasets are studied, so the conclusions may not extend to other tasks; and there is no extensive comparison against the state of the art for each problem - the benchmark is against 'some well-performing methods', not the best available. The convexity pipeline as built mispredicts when concavity is barely pronounced or when it happens to be missed by the 9 chosen tubular lines, and would also fail on shapes with small or non-central holes (a square with a hole in a corner); the authors propose finer cubical resolution, more or non-central lines, or adding 1-dimensional PH as fixes but do not evaluate them. Curvature data is limited to surfaces of CONSTANT curvature, and the authors explicitly flag non-constant curvature as future work. Additional limitations I judge relevant to us: (1) Nothing in the paper is a time series, a signal, or a detection/localisation problem - all outputs are one label or one scalar per whole point cloud, so nothing here demonstrates that PH can localise an event in time. (2) There is NO deep model on PH features anywhere in the paper - the only learners on persistence features are an SVM and a linear regression - so the paper offers zero evidence about persistence-image-plus-CNN, which is precisely our architecture. The authors list 'deep learning models on PH features' explicitly as future work. (3) The PointNet comparison is weakened by the authors disabling its native rotation and jitter...

**H. Relevance.** This paper is the strongest available justification-and-design reference for the TDA half of our project, and simultaneously proof that it contains no detector architecture we can copy. What transfers: (1) THE DECISION PROCEDURE. The paper's central, repeatedly demonstrated lesson is a three-step recipe - first identify the signal, then choose a filtration that encodes that signal, then choose a signature that weights the right bar lengths. Applied to fetal-QRS detection: our signal is a short, low-amplitude, transient event localised in time and buried under a maternal complex roughly an order of magnitude larger. That immediately tells us that a plain Vietoris-Rips filtration on a Takens cloud of a whole window is the wrong instrument, because the paper shows Vietoris-Rips encodes the SIZE of a cycle while height and tubular filtrations encode its POSITION. If we need to say WHERE the fetal R-peak is, we need a position-encoding filtration (height / tubular / sublevel-set along the time axis) or very short sliding windows, not a global VR persistence image. (2) SHORT BARS ARE NOT NOISE - and this is quantified, not asserted. Curvature MSE degraded from 0.06 to 0.21 when the pipeline was restricted to the ten longest 0-dim bars. Our fetal complex is exactly the kind of small, short-lived feature that a long-bars-only or a y-weighted persistence image would delete. This single result should govern our weight-function choice. (3) OUTLIER ROBUSTNESS VIA DTM. Swapping the...

**I. Research opportunity.** The gap this paper leaves open is precisely the space our project occupies, and the authors say so themselves in their Future Research paragraph: they list 'deep learning models on PH (and standard) features or kernels' and 'an additional network layer for topological signatures' as unexplored, having used only an SVM and a linear regression on persistence features throughout. Concretely, the openings are: (1) NO TIME-SERIES EVALUATION. Every task is a whole-cloud classification or regression - there is no experiment anywhere in which PH must localise an event in time or space. Whether the paper's conclusions about filtration choice and short bars survive the move from 'label the whole cloud' to 'find the event at sample t' is completely untested, and a sliding-window fetal-QRS detector is a natural first test of it. (2) NO PERSISTENCE-IMAGE-PLUS-CNN. Their persistence images were 10x10 and fed to an SVM. Nobody here asks whether a CNN over persistence images beats an SVM over the same images, nor whether the learned weighting a CNN induces recovers the weight function they had to grid-search by hand. That is a clean, publishable ablation for us: SVM-on-PI versus CNN-on-PI versus CNN-on-raw-signal versus the fusion of the last two. (3) NO POSITION-ENCODING FILTRATION APPLIED TO A SIGNAL. They invent the tubular filtration for convexity in R2 images; applying a time-axis analogue (height or tubular filtration along the embedding's time coordinate, or sublevel-set filtration...

**Số liệu then chốt (kèm nguồn trong bài):**

#### p27 — Tran QH, Hasegawa Y. Topological time-series analysis with delay-variant embedding. Physical Review E 99, 032209 (2019). DOI 10.1103/PhysRevE.99.032209; arXiv:1803.00208v4 (10 Jan 2019). Code:...

**Mức truy cập:** `full_text_read` · **Tải về:** có (`p27_TranHasegawa2019-DelayVariant.pdf`, 4,941,117 byte) · **Độ tin cậy trích xuất:** high

**A. Problem.** Topological data analysis of a time series normally starts with a Takens delay embedding [x(t), x(t-tau), ..., x(t-(m-1)tau)], and everything downstream depends on the single scalar time delay tau. The paper's premise is that this choice is the method's weak point: Takens' theorem says all tau are equivalent only for noise-free series of unlimited length, so for real, noisy, finite data the "optimal" tau has no theoretical grounding, is chosen by heuristics (mutual information, autocorrelation, correlation sum, attractor-expansion geometry) that trade redundancy against irrelevance, is application-dependent, and a tau that works on one task can fail on another with the same data. The authors argue that the topological features extracted at any single tau - even a well-chosen one - are insufficient to characterise qualitative changes in dynamics, and that this defect is what limits the practical power of topological features for time-series analysis. Their goal is to remove tau as a fixed hyperparameter, prove that the resulting features are noise-stable, and show they classify real time series better than both single-delay TDA and standard time-series classifiers.

**B. Data.** Three data sources. (1) SYNTHETIC Hes1 genetic oscillator: a stochastic delayed model with mRNA M and protein P, four reactions (mRNA degradation rate mu_m, protein degradation rate mu_p, translation rate alpha_p, delayed Hill-type transcription g(n_p) = alpha_m * (1 + [n_p/(Omega*P0)]^h)^-1 with delay drawn from K(tau)), simulated by the delayed Gillespie algorithm. Oscillatory regime parameters P0=300, h=1, tau=0, alpha_m=alpha_p=1, mu_m=mu_p=0.07, Omega=20; non-oscillatory regime P0=100, h=3, tau=18, alpha_m=alpha_p=1, mu_m=mu_p=0.03, Omega=20. 1,000 cells simulated in EACH regime (2,000 series total). Protein levels measured every Delta = 64, 32, 16 or 8 min over 4,096 min after a 5,000 min equilibration, giving 64, 128, 256 or 512 samples per series. #subjects = 2,000 independent simulated cells; sampling rate = 1 sample per Delta minutes; #channels = 1 (protein level only). Ground truth = the generating parameter regime, i.e. exact by construction, no annotation. (2) SYNTHETIC frequency-modulated signal, 20 carrier frequencies fc = 5f (f = 1..20) x 20 noisy realisations = 400 series of 501 samples at 0.0002 s spacing (5 kHz), used only for a qualitative principal-component visualisation; ground truth = fc by construction. (3) REAL: eight datasets taken verbatim from the UCR/UEA Time Series Classification Archive - ECG200 (200 series, length 96, 2 classes: normal vs myocardial infarction), ECG5000 (5,000 series, length 140, 5 congestive-heart-failure levels),...

**C. Method.** Delay-variant embedding. Instead of embedding once at a chosen tau, the series is embedded at EVERY tau in a predefined set T = {tau_1 < tau_2 < ... < tau_K} at a common embedding dimension m. For each tau a Vietoris-Rips filtration VR(P, epsilon) is built over the embedded point cloud (an n-simplex whenever all pairwise distances are <= 2*epsilon) and persistent homology gives the ordinary 2-D persistence diagram F_l,tau(x) of l-dimensional holes (l = 0 components, l = 1 loops). These are then stacked along a third axis: PD_l^(3)(x) = {(b, d, tau) : (b,d) in F_l,tau(x), tau in T}, a THREE-DIMENSIONAL persistence diagram whose extra coordinate is the delay itself. The diagonal becomes a diagonal PLANE W^(3) = {(b,b,tau)}, and points near it are noise. Theoretical contribution: a bottleneck distance d_B^(3,nu) is defined on 3-D diagrams using the relative infinity-norm d^(nu)(p,q) = max(/b1-b2/, /d1-d2/, nu/tau1-tau2/), and the authors prove (Appendix B, via the Chazal-de Silva-Oudot 2-D stability result d_B^(2) <= 2*d_Hausdorff and the bound d(X_tau(t0), Y_tau(t0)) <= sqrt(m)*max_t/x(t)-y(t)/) that d_B^(3,nu)(Dg^(3)(x), Dg^(3)(y)) <= 2*sqrt(m)*max_t/x(t)-y(t)/ for any positive nu - i.e. the representation's sensitivity to additive noise is bounded and grows only as the square root of the embedding dimension. Statistical contribution: a positive-definite kernel on 3-D diagrams (Eq. 4), proved p.d. in Appendix C by exhibiting an explicit L2 feature map. Interpretation...

**D. Model.** There is NO neural network. The "model" is a kernel + SVM pipeline. Input: one univariate series of 64-900 samples. Stage 1 - delay-variant embedding: embedding dimension m swept over {2,3,...,10}; delay set T = {1,2,...,10} (K = 10, unit sampling interval in tau) for all classification experiments; K = 20 (tau = 1..20) only for the frequency-modulation visualisation. Effective embedding dimension M = m*K (e.g. m = 6, K = 20 -> M = 120). Output of stage 1: K point clouds in R^m. Stage 2 - Vietoris-Rips persistent homology per point cloud, homology degrees 0 and 1 only. Output: two 3-D persistence diagrams per series (H0 and H1), each a variable-size multiset of (birth, death, tau) triples. Stage 3 - kernel: k_{sigma,nu}(Dg1^(3), Dg2^(3)) = (1/(8*pi*sigma^2)) * sum_{p in Dg1, q in Dg2} [ exp(-d^2(p,q)/(8*sigma^2)) - exp(-d^2(p, q_bar)/(8*sigma^2)) ], with q_bar the reflection of q in the diagonal plane, d^2(p,q) = /b1-b2/^2 + /d1-d2/^2 + nu^2/tau1-tau2/^2 and d^2(p,q_bar) = /b1-d2/^2 + /d1-b2/^2 + nu^2/tau1-tau2/^2. Points near the diagonal plane contribute almost nothing because then d^2(p,q) ~ d^2(p,q_bar) and the two exponentials cancel - i.e. noise suppression is built into the kernel rather than done by thresholding persistence. Stage 4 - normalisation: k(E,F) -> k(E,F)/sqrt(k(E,E)*k(F,F)). Stage 5 - fusion across homology dimensions: Gram matrix K_{NxN} = alpha_0 * K^(0)_{NxN} + alpha_1 * K^(1)_{NxN} with alpha_0, alpha_1 >= 0 and alpha_0 + alpha_1 = 1. Stage 6 -...

**E. Evaluation.** Two protocols. (1) SYNTHETIC Hes1: each of the 2,000 simulated cells is assigned with equal probability to train or test (an approximately 50/50 random split); accuracy is averaged over 100 independent random splits, reported as curves vs m, vs M, vs measurement interval Delta and vs noise variance. LEAKAGE RISK: LOW for the delay-variant method in the "subject" sense - every series is an independent stochastic simulation of a distinct cell, so a random split cannot put two segments of the same recording on both sides; there is no segment-level splitting at all. BUT there is a clear model-selection leakage in the BASELINE: the paper states that for the single-delay method "we use the maximum accuracy in the test dataset over all the values of tau", i.e. the competing method's tau is chosen on the TEST set. This is acknowledged and it biases the comparison IN FAVOUR OF THE BASELINE, so the paper's conclusion (delay-variant still wins) is conservative - but the absolute single-delay numbers in Table I are optimistic and must not be quoted as clean held-out performance. Whether the delay-variant method's own m and alpha_0 cross-validation was run strictly inside the training folds is stated for the real data ("selected by cross-validation, see Appendix C") but the fold structure is not described; for the Hes1 experiment the m-dependence is shown as a curve rather than selected, so no selection leakage there. (2) REAL data: the fixed train/test split distributed with the...

**F. Results.** REAL DATA (Table I, test accuracy %, delay-variant / single-delay / NN-Euclidean / NN-DTW / NN-autocorrelation / NN-power-spectrum / elastic-ensemble / learned-shapelets): ECG200 90.0 / 87.0 / 88.0 / 88.0 / 82.0 / 86.0 / 88.0 / 88.0. ECG5000 93.6 / 92.1 / 92.5 / 92.5 / 91.0 / 93.6 / 93.9 / 93.2. NonInvasiveFetalECGThorax1 91.8 / 78.2 / 82.9 / 82.9 / 72.1 / 87.5 / 84.6 / 25.9. NonInvasiveFetalECGThorax2 93.0 / 83.6 / 88.0 / 87.0 / 75.2 / 88.4 / 91.4 / 77.0. ECGFiveDays 99.9 / 92.0 / 79.7 / 79.7 / 98.1 / 100.0 / 82.0 / 100.0. TwoLeadECG 99.4 / 94.0 / 74.7 / 86.8 / 80.4 / 96.1 / 97.1 / 99.6. EigenWorms 83.1 / 83.1 / 61.0 / 58.4 / 76.6 / 81.8 / 68.8 / 72.7. FordB 90.8 / 78.2 / 60.6 / 59.9 / 78.0 / 79.0 / 66.2 / 91.7. Summary claims made by the authors: delay-variant beats single-delay on ALL datasets (EigenWorms is in fact a tie at 83.1), is best on 4 of 8 and second-best on the remaining 4, and has the highest average accuracy of all eight methods - the abstract calls this result "surprising". SYNTHETIC Hes1 (figures only, no tabulated numbers): with long series (Delta = 8 or 16 min, i.e. 512 or 256 samples) the two methods reach almost the same accuracy for m >= 4; with short series (Delta = 32 and 64 min, i.e. 128 and 64 samples) delay-variant is higher across the whole range m = 4-10, showing that single-delay degrades quickly as the series shortens because it is highly sensitive to the tau choice. When compared at equal EFFECTIVE dimension M = m*K, delay-variant accuracy...

**G. Limitations.** (1) TASK MISMATCH for our project: whole-series classification with one label per fixed-length series - there is no localisation, no per-sample output, no peak detection, so the method as published cannot detect fetal QRS complexes. (2) NO neural network, no persistence images, no vectorisation - the paper stops at a kernel, so it validates nothing about detector architecture, receptive field, or post-processing. (3) ACCURACY IS THE ONLY METRIC. For a 5-class CHF dataset and a 42-class fetal ECG dataset this hides per-class performance completely; no sensitivity, PPV, F1, AUC, confusion matrix, or per-class breakdown anywhere. (4) NO error bars or significance tests on the real-data results - a single fixed split, single run, and the winning margins on ECG200 (90.0 vs 88.0 on 100 test series) are within plausible noise. (5) NO computational cost reported at all. This is the most serious practical omission: the kernel is a double sum over all point pairs of two diagrams and the Gram matrix is N x N (N up to 5,000), and the delay sweep multiplies both the persistent-homology cost and the diagram size by K = 10. The fact that they had to downsample the longest series by 2-3x is indirect evidence the pipeline was expensive, but no runtime, memory or complexity figure is given. (6) NO ABLATION on the delay grid: K is fixed at 10 (or 20), spacing is fixed at 1, the range always starts at tau = 1; the effect of K, of non-uniform or logarithmic tau spacing, or of a larger tau range...

**H. Relevance.** Directly usable for the TDA half of our pipeline, useless for the detector half. Concretely: (a) THE TAU PROBLEM IS REAL AND EXPENSIVE. Our plan is Takens embedding -> persistent homology -> persistence images -> CNN, and the standard recipe would be to fix tau by mutual information or first autocorrelation zero. This paper is direct evidence that that choice can cost double-digit accuracy on the exact kind of data we care about: on NonInvasiveFetalECGThorax1 fixed-tau TDA scored 78.2 and swept-tau TDA scored 91.8 (+13.6 points), and on Thorax2 83.6 -> 93.0 (+9.4). These are the two largest single-delay-to-delay-variant gaps in the table and they are on real non-invasive fetal ECG. Note that in Fig. 3 the authors used mutual information to pick the single tau (getting tau = 4 non-oscillatory, tau = 2 oscillatory) and it was still not enough. (b) IMMEDIATE ARCHITECTURAL TRANSLATION. The 3-D persistence diagram (birth, death, tau) becomes, in a CNN world, a STACK of K persistence images used as K input channels - so tau becomes a channel axis the network learns to weight instead of a hyperparameter we must guess. This is a cheap, well-motivated design we can adopt on day one and ablate against single-tau. (c) SHORT WINDOWS FAVOUR THE SWEEP. Their Hes1 result is that single-delay and delay-variant tie on long series but the sweep wins clearly on the SHORT ones (64-128 samples). Fetal QRS detection works on short windows (a 250 ms window at 250-1000 Hz is 62-250 samples), so we...

**I. Research opportunity.** Several open gaps, and our project sits in the largest one. (1) NO LEARNED REPRESENTATION ON TOP OF DELAY-VARIANT FEATURES. The authors stop at a fixed positive-definite kernel plus an SVM and explicitly criticise deep learning as a black box, so nobody has combined delay-variant embedding with a differentiable vectorisation (persistence images, PersLay-style learned layers) and a CNN. Stacking the K delay-indexed persistence images as CNN channels is an obvious, untried, and directly publishable step - and it also removes their cross-validated alpha_0 grid search and their fixed nu = sigma by making both learnable. (2) NO DETECTION/LOCALISATION. Everything here is one label per whole series. Applying delay-variant topological features to per-sample or per-window fetal QRS LOCALISATION (sliding-window detection with peak-picking) is completely unexplored and is exactly our task. (3) THE DELAY GRID ITSELF IS UNSTUDIED. K, the tau spacing, and the tau range are fixed by fiat (tau = 1..10, unit spacing) with no ablation. For fetal ECG the physiologically meaningful delays are tied to QRS width and to the fetal RR interval, so a principled or learned tau schedule - and an ablation of K and spacing - is an open contribution. (4) NO COST ANALYSIS. The paper reports zero runtime or memory numbers despite a method whose cost scales linearly in K on top of Vietoris-Rips; a study that reports the accuracy-vs-compute trade-off of the delay sweep (including the downsampling factor they...
